\displaystyle=s_{i}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\partial_{\ell}F(\hat{\mathbf{f}}^{\star}(\mu))\nabla f_{\ell}(s_{i},x_{i})

\displaystyle=s_{i}{\mathrm{D}}\hat{u}(\mu,x_{i}),

where the last equality comes from Lemma
2.8
. This proves (
2.33
).
Using again Lemma
2.8
, and observing that
\nabla f_{\ell}(s_{j},x_{j})=0
for
j>N(\varepsilon,\mathbf{s})
(since
s_{j}<\varepsilon
),
we
establish
(
2.34
) by repeating the computation in the last two lines. To prove (
2.36
) we observe that, since
\hat{u}(\mu-s_{j}\delta{x_{j}}+s_{j}\delta_{z})=(\hat{u}\circ\mathrm{em}^{N})(\mathbf{s},(x_{1},\dots,x_{j-1},z,x_{j+1},\dots,x_{N}))
for every
j=1,\dots,N
and
z\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
, and for
j>N(\varepsilon,\mathbf{s})
the first function is constant in
z
, we have

\Delta_{i}(\hat{u}\circ\mathrm{em}^{N})(\mathbf{s},(x_{1},\dots,x_{N}))=0\quad\text{ for every }N(\varepsilon,\mathbf{s})<i\leq N.

To prove (
2.35
) we use the same equality and (
2.16
) to get

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Delta}\hat{u}(\mu)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\frac{\Delta_{z}\rvert_{z=x}\hat{u}(\mu+\mu_{x}\delta_{z}-\mu_{x}\delta_{x})}{(\mu_{x})^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{s_{j}}\Delta_{z}|_{z=x_{j}}\bigl[\hat{u}(\mu-s_{j}\delta_{x_{j}}+s_{j}\delta_{z})\bigr]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{N(\varepsilon,\mathbf{s})}\frac{1}{s_{j}}\Delta_{z}|_{z=x_{j}}\bigl[(\hat{u}\circ\mathrm{em}^{N})((s_{1},\dots,s_{N}),(x_{1},\dots,x_{j-1},z,x_{j+1},\dots,x_{N}))\bigr]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{N(\varepsilon,\mathbf{s})}\frac{1}{s_{j}}\Delta_{i}(\hat{u}\circ\mathrm{em}^{N})((s_{1},\dots,s_{N}),(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})).

∎

The next result relies on the definition of
\mathfrak{TZ}(I)
given at the very end of Subsection
2.2.1
, where
I\subset\mathbb{R}
is an interval,
\varepsilon\in[0,1)
and
\mathfrak{Z}
is any of the classes
\mathfrak{Z}^{\infty}
,
\hat{\mathfrak{Z}}_{c}^{\infty}
,
\hat{\mathfrak{Z}}_{\varepsilon}^{\infty}
.
We also recall that the tuple
((\varsigma^{j})_{j},(\xi^{0,j})_{j},((\beta^{j}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]})_{j},((\xi_{t}^{j})_{t\in[0,T]})_{j})
denotes the canonical process on
\Xi
.

Proposition 2.18
.

Let
t_{0}\in[0,T)
be an initial time and
(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x})\in T_{o}^{\infty}\times(\mathbb{T}^{d})_{o}^{\infty}
be an initial datum.
Then, for
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
and
u\in\mathfrak{T}\hat{\mathfrak{Z}}_{\varepsilon}^{\infty}([t_{0},T])
, the process
(u_{t}(\mu_{t}^{\infty}))_{t\in[0,T]}
can be expanded as

(2.37)

\begin{split}u_{t}(\mu^{\infty}_{t})&=u_{t_{0}}(\mu^{\infty}_{t_{0}})+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\left(\partial_{r}u_{r}(\mu^{\infty}_{r})+\boldsymbol{\Delta}u_{r}(\mu^{\infty}_{r})\right)\,\mathrm{d}r\\
&\hskip 15.0pt+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\sqrt{2\varsigma_{i}}{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}(\mu_{r}^{\infty},\xi^{i}_{r})\cdot\,\mathrm{d}\beta^{i}_{r},\quad t\in[t_{0},T],\quad\mathbf{P}^{t_{0},(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x})}\text{-a.s.}\end{split}

where, for any

\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, the function
r\in[t_{0},T]\mapsto\partial_{r}u_{r}(\mu)
is regarded as (a representative of) the Sobolev derivative of the function

r\in[t_{0},T]\mapsto u_{r}(\mu)
. (We explain in the proof how the representative, which is uniquely defined up to a null Borel subset, can be canonically chosen.)

Note that in the above display we are using the convention explained in Remark
2.15
to evaluate
{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}(\mu_{r}^{\infty},\cdot)
at
\xi_{r}^{i}
.

Proof.

For simplicity
we assume that
t_{0}
is equal to
0
.
Moreover, for the first part of the proof, we also suppose that
u
does not depend on time (i.e.
u\in\hat{\mathfrak{Z}}_{\varepsilon}^{\infty}
).
We set
N:=\lceil 1/\varepsilon\rceil
,
(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t}^{N}:=(\xi^{1}_{t},\dots,\xi^{N}_{t}))_{t\in[0,T]}
. Recall also the notation
\varpi\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{T}^{d}
as in (
2.28
) for the (smooth) canonical projection from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
\mathbb{R}^{d}/\mathbb{Z}^{d}=\mathbb{T}^{d}
and let
\varpi_{N}\colon(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{N}\to(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{N}
be the (smooth) canonical projection from
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{N}
to
(\mathbb{R}^{d}/\mathbb{Z}^{d})^{N}=(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{N}
(i.e.
\varpi
applied applied componentwise). Note that, for
v\in{\mathrm{C}}^{\infty}((\mathbb{T}^{d})^{N})
, one has
\nabla_{i}(v\circ\varpi)=(\nabla_{i}v)\circ\varpi
, where on the left-hand side the gradient is understood as an operator on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, while on the right-hand side it is understood as an operator on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
. An analogous relation holds for
\Delta_{i}
.

By applying classical Itô’s formula to
u\circ\mathrm{em}^{N}(\mathbf{s},\cdot)\circ\varpi_{N}
, which belongs to

{\mathrm{C}}^{\infty}((\mathbb{R}^{d})^{N})
, and by using the same notation
\nabla_{i}
and
\Delta_{i}
as in Lemma
2.17
, we get

\displaystyle u(\mu^{\infty}_{t})

\displaystyle=(u\circ\mathrm{em}^{N}(\mathbf{s},\cdot)\circ\varpi_{N})(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t}^{N})

\displaystyle=(u\circ\mathrm{em}^{N}(\mathbf{s},\cdot)\circ\varpi_{N})(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{0}^{N})+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{0}^{t}\sqrt{\frac{2}{s_{i}}}\nabla_{i}(u\circ\mathrm{em}^{N}(\mathbf{s},\cdot)\circ\varpi_{N})(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{r}^{N})\cdot\,\mathrm{d}\beta^{i}_{r}

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{s_{i}}\Delta_{i}(u\circ\mathrm{em}^{N}(\mathbf{s},\cdot)\circ\varpi_{N})(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{r}^{N})\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=(u\circ\mathrm{em}^{N})(\varpi_{N}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{0}^{N}))+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{0}^{t}\sqrt{\frac{2}{s_{i}}}\nabla_{i}(u\circ\mathrm{em}^{N})(\varpi_{N}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{r}^{N}))\cdot\,\mathrm{d}\beta^{i}_{r}

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{s_{i}}\Delta_{i}(u\circ\mathrm{em}^{N})(\varpi_{N}(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{r}^{N}))\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=u(\mu_{0}^{\infty})+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\sqrt{2s_{i}}{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}(\mu_{r}^{\infty},\xi^{i}_{r})\cdot\,\mathrm{d}\beta^{i}_{r}+\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\Delta}u(\mu^{\infty}_{r})\,\mathrm{d}r,\quad t\in[0,T],\quad\mathbf{P}^{0,(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x})}\text{-a.s.}

where to obtain the last line we used Lemma
2.17
.

Extending the proof to the case when
u

also depends on time requires some care, because elements of

\mathfrak{T}\hat{\mathfrak{Z}}_{\varepsilon}^{\infty})[t_{0},T])
are just assumed to be Lipschitz in
time, uniformly in
the measure argument.
Generally speaking, the point is to replace the standard version of Itô’s formula
by the Itô-Krylov one (see, e.g.,
[
43
, Theorem 1, p. 122]
).
Here, this is possible because the process
(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t}^{N})_{t\in[0,T]}

is non-degenerate. In brief,
Sobolev differentiability
in time is then enough to get the above expansion.
We thus regard
r\mapsto\partial_{r}[u_{r}\circ\mathrm{em}^{N}(\mathbf{s},\cdot)\circ\varpi_{N}](x_{1},\cdots,x_{N})

as a version of the Sobolev derivative (in
r
) of the function
r\mapsto[u_{r}\circ\mathrm{em}^{N}(\mathbf{s},\cdot)\circ\varpi_{N}](x_{1},\cdots,x_{N}).

This derivative is uniquely defined up to a null Borel subset of
[0,T]\times({\mathbb{R}}^{d})^{N}
, which
is enough for our purpose
because

(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t}^{N})_{t\in[0,T]}
does not see null Borel subsets of

({\mathbb{R}}^{d})^{N}
. Thanks to the identity
u_{r}(\mu)=(u_{r}\circ\mathrm{em}^{N}(\mathbf{s},\cdot)\circ\varpi_{N})(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})
, we can use this version when writing

\partial_{r}{u}_{r}(\mu)
.
∎

3.
The backward Kolmogorov equation

In this section we discuss existence, uniqueness and stability for a family of PDEs set on the space of probability measures. In Section
4
we will relate the solution to such PDEs to an interacting particle system.
In the whole section we let
T
be the same finite time horizon as at the beginning of Section
2
.
Recall that
\mathcal{D}
denotes the Dirichlet–Ferguson measure (see Definition
2.2
),

\overline{\mathcal{D}}
the measure in (
2.25
),
and
H
and
H^{1,2}
the spaces introduced in (
2.21
). Moreover,
{\mathrm{D}}
is defined in Proposition
2.13
, while
\boldsymbol{\Delta}
is introduced below Theorem
2.11
.
Given two functions
b_{1},b_{2}\in L^{2}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d},\overline{\mathcal{D}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we denote by
[b_{1},b_{2}]\in L^{2}({\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),{\mathcal{D}})
the function

(3.1)

\left[b_{1},b_{2}\right]_{\mu}:=\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}b_{1}(\mu,x)\cdot b_{2}(\mu,x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x),\quad\mathcal{D}\text{-a.e. }\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d}).

We report here a useful result coming from
[
6
, Theorems 3.4, 3.6]
.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{{\sf H}}
be a separable Hilbert space,
\varphi:\mathcal{{\sf H}}\to[0,\infty]
be a lower semicontinuous and convex functional,

x_{0}\in\overline{D(\varphi)}
, and

f\in L^{2}([0,T];{\sf H})
. Then, there exists a unique
x\in{\mathrm{C}}([0,T];{\sf H})\cap\mathrm{AC}_{loc}((0,T);{\sf H})
such that
x_{t}\in D(\partial\varphi)
, for a.e.
t\in(0,T)
, and

\partial_{t}x_{t}+\partial\varphi(x_{t})\ni f_{t}\quad\text{ for a.e.\penalty\ }t\in(0,T),\quad x_{t}\bigr|_{t=0}=x_{0}.

For such an
x
, it also holds that
t\mapsto\varphi(x_{t})
belongs to
L^{1}([0,T])
.

Note that the inclusion in the above statement is necessary since, in general, the subdifferential of
\varphi
at
x_{t}
, denoted by
\partial\varphi(x_{t})\subset{\sf H}
, is a set rather than a point. Therefore, we require that the element
f_{t}-\partial_{t}x_{t}\in{\sf H}
belongs to this set.

We use the result above (which statement is very general) to establish the first contributions of the paper. The next one guarantees the solvability of the heat equation associated with
\boldsymbol{\Delta}
, even when the source term and boundary condition are only in
H
, see notation (
2.21
).

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
f\in L^{2}([0,T];H)
and
g\in H
.
Then, there exists a unique
u\in{\mathrm{C}}([0,T];H)\cap\mathrm{AC}_{loc}((0,T);H)\cap L^{2}([0,T];H^{1,2})
such that
u_{t}\in D(\boldsymbol{\Delta})
for a.e.
t\in(0,T)
and

(3.2)

\partial_{t}u_{t}+\boldsymbol{\Delta}u_{t}=f_{t}\quad\text{for a.e.\penalty\ }t\in(0,T),\quad u_{t}\bigr|_{t=T}=g.

Proof.

First, let us observe that solving (
3.2
) is equivalent to solving the forward equation

\partial_{t}\tilde{u}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\Delta}\tilde{u}_{t}=-f_{T-t}\quad\text{for a.e.\penalty\ }t\in(0,T),\quad\tilde{u}_{t}\bigr|_{t=0}=g.

Indeed, to pass from the forward formulation to the backward one, it suffices to set
u_{t}:=\tilde{u}_{T-t}
.
Then, it is enough to apply Theorem
3.1
with
\mathsf{H}:=H
,
\varphi:=\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}
(so that
\partial\varphi=\{-\boldsymbol{\Delta}\}
as stated in
[
38
, Proposition 5.2.4]
),
x_{0}:=g\in\overline{D(\varphi)}=H
(see
[
38
, Proposition 5.2.6]
). This proves the existence of a solution
\tilde{u}\in{\mathrm{C}}([0,T];H)\cap\mathrm{AC}_{loc}((0,T);H)
to the forward equation. To conclude, it remains to show that
\tilde{u}\in L^{2}([0,T];H^{1,2})
. Since
t\mapsto\varphi(\tilde{u}_{t})=\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}(\tilde{u}_{t})
belongs to
L^{1}([0,T])
, we already know that

\int_{0}^{T}\mathsf{C\kern-1.0ptE}(\tilde{u}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{T}\|{\mathrm{D}}\tilde{u}_{t}\|\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty.

Therefore, we
are just left to show the strong measurability of
t\mapsto{\mathrm{D}}\tilde{u}_{t}
, cf.
[
33
, Appendix E.5]
. Since
H^{1,2}
is separable, by Pettis’ theorem (see e.g.
[
33
, Theorem 7, p. 733]
, this is equivalent to proving weak measurability.
Using the strong measurability of
t\mapsto u_{t}
and the integration by parts formula in (
2.24
), we deduce that

t\mapsto\langle{\mathrm{D}}\tilde{u}_{t},\boldsymbol{\nabla}\hat{v}\rangle=-(\tilde{u}_{t},{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}\hat{v})\text{ is measurable, for every }\hat{v}\in\hat{\mathfrak{Z}}_{c}^{\infty}.

Let us denote by
\mathrm{T}
the subspace

\mathrm{T}:=L^{2}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d},\overline{{\mathcal{D}}};\mathbb{R}^{d})\text{ - closure of }\left\{\boldsymbol{\nabla}\hat{v}:\hat{v}\in\hat{\mathfrak{Z}}^{\infty}_{c}\right\}.

It follows that

t\mapsto\langle{\mathrm{D}}\tilde{u}_{t},\xi\rangle\quad\text{ is measurable, for every }\xi\in\mathrm{T}.

By Proposition
2.13
, we have that
{\mathrm{D}}\tilde{u}_{t}\in\mathrm{T}
for every
t\in[0,T]
, so that, denoting by
P_{\mathrm{T}}:L^{2}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d},\overline{{\mathcal{D}}};\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathrm{T}
the orthogonal projection on
\mathrm{T}
, we get

t\mapsto\langle{\mathrm{D}}\tilde{u}_{t},\xi\rangle=\langle{\mathrm{D}}\tilde{u}_{t},P_{\mathrm{T}}\xi\rangle\quad\text{ is measurable, for every }\xi\in L^{2}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d},\overline{{\mathcal{D}}};\mathbb{R}^{d}).

This shows that
t\mapsto{\mathrm{D}}\tilde{u}_{t}
is weakly measurable and concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 3.3

(On the choice of a representative of
{\mathrm{D}}u
)
.

In the setting of Proposition
3.2
, the map
t\mapsto{\mathrm{D}}u_{t}
is strongly measurable from
[0,T]
to
L^{2}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d},\overline{{\mathcal{D}}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
It is natural to ask whether there exists a Borel function
(t,\mu,x)\mapsto\xi(t,\mu,x)
such that (the
L^{2}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d},\overline{{\mathcal{D}}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
-equivalence class represented by)
\xi(t,\cdot)
coincides with
{\mathrm{D}}u_{t}
for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
. By
[
38
, Propositions 1.3.19, 1.3.20, 1.3.21]
, the answer is positive. Therefore, in the sequel, for a function
u\in L^{2}([0,T];H^{1,2})
, the notation
{\mathrm{D}}u
will refer to one such Borel representative.

Our first main result establishes a form of regularization under the action of the operator
{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}
. The transport-diffusion equation on
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, driven by
{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}
and perturbed by a bounded, merely measurable velocity field, admits a unique solution in the sense defined below.

Theorem 3.4
.

Let
b:[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a bounded and measurable function,
f\in L^{2}([0,T];H)
, and
g\in H
. Then, there exists a unique
u\in{\mathrm{C}}([0,T];H)\cap\mathrm{AC}_{loc}((0,T);H)\cap L^{2}([0,T];H^{1,2})

such that
u_{t}\in D(\boldsymbol{\Delta})
for a.e.
t\in(0,T)
and

(3.3)

\partial_{t}u_{t}+\left[b_{t},{\mathrm{D}}u_{t}\right]+\boldsymbol{\Delta}u_{t}=f_{t}\quad\text{for a.e.\penalty\ }t\in(0,T),\quad u_{t}\bigr|_{t=T}=g.

Proof.

For simplicity of exposition, we focus on the forward equation

(3.4)

\partial_{t}u_{t}-\left[b_{t},{\mathrm{D}}u_{t}\right]-\boldsymbol{\Delta}u_{t}=f_{t}\quad\text{for a.e.\penalty\ }t\in(0,T),\quad u_{t}\big|_{t=0}=g,

which can be shown to be equivalent to (
3.3
) by
the same
simple time reversal as in the proof of Proposition
3.2
.
The analysis of
(
3.4
) relies on a classical fixed point argument, applied iteratively on successive short time intervals. To formulate this,
we set, for any
\bar{T}>0
,
X_{\bar{T}}:=L^{2}([0,\bar{T}];H^{1,2})
, with the corresponding Hilbertian norm being denoted by
\|\cdot\|_{X_{\bar{T}}}
.
Let us also recall that, throughout,
(\cdot,\cdot)_{H}

and
|\cdot|_{H}
stand respectively
for the scalar product and the norm in
H
, and that
\|\cdot\|
denotes the norm on the space
L^{2}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d},\overline{\mathcal{D}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
, as defined
in Remark
2.12
. We divide the proof into several steps.

Claim 1
: For any
\bar{T}\in(0,T)
,
w_{0}\in H
and
v\in X_{\bar{T}}
, there exists a unique
u^{v}\in X_{\bar{T}}
solving

(3.5)

\partial_{t}u^{v}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\Delta}u^{v}_{t}=f^{v}_{t}\quad\text{ for a.e.\penalty\ }t\in(0,\bar{T}),\quad u^{v}_{t}\bigr|_{t=0}=w_{0},

where

f^{v}_{t}:=\left[b_{t},{\mathrm{D}}v_{t}\right]+f_{t}
, for
t\in[0,T]
. This defines a map
\Phi_{\bar{T},w_{0}}:X_{\bar{T}}\to X_{\bar{T}}
associating to every
v\in X_{\bar{T}}
the function
u^{v}
as above.

Proof of claim 1:
This follows directly from Proposition
3.2
, with
f:=f^{v}
therein,
noting that

f^{v}
belongs to

L^{2}([0,\bar{T}];H)
because

b
is bounded and

u\in L^{2}([0,T];H^{1,2})
.

Claim 2
: Let
\bar{T}:=(4\sqrt{2}\|b\|_{\infty})^{-2}\wedge T/2
. Then, for every
w_{0}\in H
, the map
\Phi_{\bar{T},w_{0}}
as in claim 1 is a contraction on
X_{\bar{T}}
.

Proof of claim 2:

For
w_{0}\in H
, and
v,\tilde{v}\in X_{\bar{T}}
, set
u:=\Phi_{\bar{T},w_{0}}(v)
,
\tilde{u}:=\Phi_{\bar{T},w_{0}}(\tilde{v})
,
h:=\left[b,{\mathrm{D}}v\right]+f
, and
\tilde{h}:=\left[b,{\mathrm{D}}\tilde{v}\right]+f
.
Considering (
3.5
) for both
u
and
\tilde{u}
and
subtracting the resulting equations, we get

\partial_{t}(u_{t}-\tilde{u}_{t})-\boldsymbol{\Delta}(u_{t}-\tilde{u}_{t})=h_{t}-\tilde{h}_{t}\quad\text{ for a.e.\penalty\ }t\in(0,\bar{T}),\quad(u_{t}-\tilde{u}_{t})\bigr|_{t=0}=0.

We integrate the equation against
u-\tilde{u}
on
[0,t]\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, for
t\in[0,\bar{T}]
.
By (
2.24
) we get

(3.6)

|u_{t}-\tilde{u}_{t}|^{2}_{H}+2\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}-{\mathrm{D}}\tilde{u}_{r}\right\|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r=2\int_{0}^{t}(h_{t}-\tilde{h}_{t},u_{r}-\tilde{u}_{r})_{H}\,\mathrm{d}r.

Then, by Young’s inequality, it holds

(3.7)

\displaystyle|u_{t}-\tilde{u}_{t}|^{2}_{H}

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}-{\mathrm{D}}\tilde{u}_{r}\right\|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2\bar{T}}\int_{0}^{t}|u_{r}-\tilde{u}_{r}|^{2}_{H}\,\mathrm{d}r+2\bar{T}\int_{0}^{t}|h_{t}-\tilde{h}_{t}|^{2}_{H}\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2\bar{T}}\int_{0}^{t}|u_{r}-\tilde{u}_{r}|^{2}_{H}\,\mathrm{d}r+2\bar{T}\left\|b\right\|_{\infty}^{2}\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\mathrm{D}}v_{r}-{\mathrm{D}}\tilde{v}_{r}\right\|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2\bar{T}}\int_{0}^{\bar{T}}|u_{r}-\tilde{u}_{r}|^{2}_{H}\,\mathrm{d}r+2\bar{T}\left\|b\right\|_{\infty}^{2}\left\|v-\tilde{v}\right\|^{2}_{X_{\bar{T}}},

where we have used the definitions of
h
and
\tilde{h}
together with the
fact that
b
is bounded. In particular, neglecting the second term on the left-hand side and integrating over the interval
[0,\bar{T}]
, we obtain

(3.8)

\int_{0}^{\bar{T}}|u_{t}-\tilde{u}_{t}|_{H}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq 4\bar{T}^{2}\left\|b\right\|^{2}_{\infty}\left\|v-\tilde{v}\right\|^{2}_{X_{\bar{T}}}.

Returning to (
3.7
), inserting (
3.8
), neglecting now the first term on the left-hand side, and evaluating the second term on the left-hand side at time
t=\bar{T}
, we obtain

(3.9)

\int_{0}^{\bar{T}}\left\|{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}-{\mathrm{D}}\tilde{u}_{r}\right\|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r\leq\frac{1}{2\bar{T}}\int_{0}^{\bar{T}}|u_{r}-\tilde{u}_{r}|^{2}_{H}\,\mathrm{d}r+2\bar{T}\left\|b\right\|_{\infty}^{2}\left\|v-\tilde{v}\right\|^{2}_{X_{\bar{T}}}\leq 4\bar{T}\left\|b\right\|_{\infty}^{2}\left\|v-\tilde{v}\right\|^{2}_{X_{\bar{T}}}.

Finally, putting together (
3.8
) and (
3.9
), we get

\displaystyle\left\|\Phi_{\bar{T},w_{0}}(v)-\Phi_{\bar{T},w_{0}}(\tilde{v})\right\|_{X_{\bar{T}}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\bar{T}}\left(|u_{t}-\tilde{u}_{t}|^{2}_{H}+\left\|{\mathrm{D}}u_{t}-{\mathrm{D}}\tilde{u}_{t}\right\|^{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq 4\left\|b\right\|^{2}_{\infty}(\bar{T}+\bar{T}^{2})\left\|v-\tilde{v}\right\|^{2}_{X_{\bar{T}}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{4}\left\|v-\tilde{v}\right\|^{2}_{X_{\bar{T}}}.

Conclusion
: Claim 2 and Banach fixed point theorem yield the existence and uniqueness (as stated in the present theorem) of a solution on every interval of the form
[t_{0},t_{0}+\bar{T}]\subset[0,T]
. Therefore the
desired unique solution
u
is obtained by successively patching together the solutions
on these intervals, noting also that
\bar{T}
only depends on
\|b\|_{\infty}
and
T
.
∎

As a particular case, we can solve (
3.3
) when the source term is equal to
0
. Since we will often refer to this in what follows, we state it as a corollary.

Corollary 3.5
.

Let
b:[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a bounded and measurable function and
g\in H
. Then, there exists a unique
u\in{\mathrm{C}}([0,T];H)\cap\mathrm{AC}_{loc}((0,T);H)\cap L^{2}([0,T];H^{1,2})

such that
u_{t}\in D(\boldsymbol{\Delta})
for a.e.
t\in(0,T)
and

(3.10)

\partial_{t}u_{t}+\left[b,{\mathrm{D}}u_{t}\right]+\boldsymbol{\Delta}u_{t}=0\quad\text{for a.e.\penalty\ }t\in(0,T),\quad u_{t}\bigr|_{t=T}=g.

We conclude this section with a general stability result for the transport-diffusion equation (
3.3
) under perturbations of the drift
b
, the source term
f
, and the initial datum
g
.

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
b,b^{n}:[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, be bounded and measurable functions such that
b^{n}\to b
in
L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathscr{L}^{[0,T]}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{D}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
M:=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}\|b^{n}\|_{\infty}<\infty
. Let
g,g^{n}\in H
,
f,f^{n}\in L^{2}([0,T];H)
,
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, be such that
g^{n}\to g
in
H
and
f^{n}\to f
in
L^{2}([0,T];H)
as
n\to\infty
. If
u
and
u^{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, are the unique solutions to (
3.3
) with drifts
b
and
b^{n}
, source term
f
and
f^{n}
, and final datum
g
and
g^{n}
, respectively, then

(3.11)

\begin{split}&\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|u^{n}_{t}-u_{t}|_{H}^{2}+\int_{0}^{T}\|{\mathrm{D}}u_{t}^{n}-{\mathrm{D}}u_{t}\|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\qquad\qquad\leq C(M,T)\left(|g-g^{n}|_{H}^{2}+\int_{0}^{T}|f_{t}-f_{t}^{n}|_{H}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t+\mathcal{R}(b,b^{n},{\mathrm{D}}u,T)\right)\to 0\text{ as }n\to\infty,\end{split}

where

\mathcal{R(}b,b^{n},{\mathrm{D}}u,T):=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\lvert b_{r}(\mu,x)-b_{r}^{n}(\mu,x)\rvert^{2}\lvert{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}(\mu,x)\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\,\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{D}}(\mu)\,\mathrm{d}r,

and

C(M,T):=1+{\mathrm{e}}^{T(2+M^{2})}+(2+M^{2})T{\mathrm{e}}^{T(2+M^{2})}
.
In particular, the left-hand side of (
3.11
) tends to
0
as
n
tends to
\infty
.

Proof.

By subtracting the equations solved on
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times(0,T)
by
u
and
u^{n}
respectively, we obtain that, for a fixed
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
u-u^{n}
satisfies

\partial_{t}(u_{t}-u^{n}_{t})+\boldsymbol{\Delta}(u_{t}-u^{n}_{t})=-(\left[b_{t},{\mathrm{D}}u_{t}\right]-\left[b_{t}^{n},{\mathrm{D}}u^{n}_{t}\right])+(f_{t}-f_{t}^{n})\quad\text{ for a.e.\penalty\ }t\in(0,T),\quad(u_{t}-u^{n}_{t})\bigr|_{t=T}=g-g^{n}.

Then, by integrating the equation against
u-u^{n}
on
[t,T]\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, for a given
t\in[0,T]
, and by integrating by parts the term driven by
{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}
using (
2.24
), we get

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}|u_{t}-u^{n}_{t}|^{2}_{H}

\displaystyle+\int_{t}^{T}\left\|{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}-{\mathrm{D}}u^{n}_{r}\right\|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}|g-g^{n}|^{2}_{H}+\int_{t}^{T}(f_{r}^{n}-f_{r},u_{r}-u_{r}^{n})_{H}\,\mathrm{d}r+\int_{t}^{T}(\left[b_{r},{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}\right]-\left[b_{r}^{n},{\mathrm{D}}u^{n}_{r}\right],u_{r}-u^{n}_{r})_{H}\,\mathrm{d}r

(3.12)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}|g-g^{n}|^{2}_{H}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}|f_{r}-f_{r}^{n}|^{2}_{H}\,\mathrm{d}r+\frac{1}{2}\int_{t}^{T}|u_{r}-u_{r}^{n}|^{2}_{H}\,\mathrm{d}r

(3.13)

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{t}^{T}\big(\left[b_{r}-b_{r}^{n},{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}\right]+\left[b_{r}^{n},{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}-{\mathrm{D}}u^{n}_{r}\right],u_{r}-u^{n}_{r}\big)_{H}\,\mathrm{d}r,

where we also used Young’s inequality.
Let us focus on (
3.13
). Regarding the second summand,
we have, by Young’s inequality,

(3.14)

\displaystyle\int_{t}^{T}\big(\left[b_{r}^{n},{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}-{\mathrm{D}}u^{n}_{r}\right],u_{r}-u^{n}_{r}\big)_{H}\,\mathrm{d}r\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{t}^{T}\left\|{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}-{\mathrm{D}}u^{n}_{r}\right\|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r+\frac{M^{2}}{2}\int_{t}^{T}|u_{r}-u^{n}_{r}|^{2}_{H}\,\mathrm{d}r.

To estimate the first summand in (
3.13
), we proceed as follows: by Young’s and Jensen inequalities,

(3.15)

\begin{split}\int_{t}^{T}&\big(\left[b_{r}-b_{r}^{n},{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}\right],u_{r}-u^{n}_{r}\big)_{H}\,\mathrm{d}r\\
&\qquad\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{t}^{T}\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\lvert b_{r}(\mu,x)-b_{r}^{n}(\mu,x)\rvert^{2}\lvert{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}(\mu,x)\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\,\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{D}}(\mu)\,\mathrm{d}r\\
&\qquad\hskip 15.0pt+\frac{1}{2}\int_{t}^{T}|u_{r}-u^{n}_{r}|^{2}_{H}\,\mathrm{d}r\\
&\qquad\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{R}(b,b^{n},{\mathrm{D}}u,T)+\frac{1}{2}\int_{t}^{T}|u_{r}-u^{n}_{r}|^{2}_{H}\,\mathrm{d}r.\end{split}

Combining (
3.12
), (
3.13
), (
3.14
), and (
3.15
), we get

\displaystyle|u_{t}-u_{t}^{n}|_{H}^{2}+\int_{t}^{T}\|{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}-{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}^{n}\|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\leq(2+M^{2})\int_{t}^{T}|u_{r}-u_{r}^{n}|_{H}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r+|g-g^{n}|_{H}^{2}+\int_{0}^{T}|f_{r}-f_{r}^{n}|_{H}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\quad+\mathcal{R}(b,b^{n},{\mathrm{D}}u,T).

The inequality in the statement follows then by the above inequality and a standard application of Gronwall’s lemma. To conclude the proof, we are left to show that

(3.16)

\mathcal{R}(b,b^{n},{\mathrm{D}}u,T)\to 0\quad\text{ as }n\to\infty.

Since
b^{n}\to b
in
L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathscr{L}^{[0,T]}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{D}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we have

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\left|b_{r}(\mu,x)-b_{r}^{n}(\mu,x)\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\,\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{D}}(\mu)\,\mathrm{d}r\to 0\quad\text{ as }n\to\infty,

which implies that, as

n\to\infty
,

\left((r,\mu,x)\mapsto\left|b_{r}(\mu,x)-b_{r}^{n}(\mu,x)\right|^{2}\right)\rightarrow 0,

in

{\mathscr{L}}^{[0,T]}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{D}}
-measure over

[0,T]\times{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}
. Since
b
and
(b^{n})_{n}
are uniformly bounded by the constant
M
,
and the function

(r,\mu,x)\mapsto|{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}(\mu,x)|^{2}

is integrable on
[0,T]\times{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}
under
{\mathscr{L}}^{[0,T]}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{D}}
, we deduce that
the collection

\left((r,\mu,x)\mapsto\left|b_{r}(\mu,x)-b_{r}^{n}(\mu,x)\right|^{2}|{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}(\mu,x)|^{2}\right)_{n}

is uniformly integrable under

{\mathscr{L}}^{[0,T]}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{D}}
. As it converges to
0

in
{\mathscr{L}}^{[0,T]}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{D}}
-measure, this proves that it also converges in
L^{1}
-norm under
{\mathscr{L}}^{[0,T]}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{D}}
-measure, which completes the proof.
∎

4.
The (massive) interacting particle system

The purpose of this section is to provide a probabilistic interpretation of the solution to the transport-diffusion equation (
3.10
) using a particle system built from the free particle system presented in Subsection
2.3
. More generally, the entire section is based on the material introduced earlier, and in particular on
the notation defined in Section
2
. In the whole section we let
T
be the same finite time horizon as at the beginning of Section
2
.

Given a collection of uniformly bounded and measurable drifts
(b^{i}\colon[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d})_{i}
(corresponding, at least when all the
b^{i}
’s are the same, to the velocity field in the PDE (
3.10
)), an initial time
t_{0}\in[0,T)
, and an initial distribution
\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}\in\mathcal{P}(T_{o}^{\infty}\times(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{\infty}_{o})
, we aim at solving (in a suitable weak sense to be precised later) the following system

(4.1)

\begin{cases}\,\mathrm{d}X^{i}_{t}&=b_{t}^{i}(\mu^{\infty}_{t},X^{i}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\frac{2}{s_{i}}}\,\mathrm{d}W^{i}_{t},\quad t\in[t_{0},T],\,i\in\mathbb{N}_{+},\\
X^{i}_{t_{0}}&=\imath(x_{i}),\quad i\in\mathbb{N}_{+},\end{cases}

with
(W_{i})_{i}
being a family of independent
d
-dimensional
\{{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\}_{t\in[t_{0},T]}
-Brownian motions, the initial datum
(\mathbf{s}=(s_{i})_{i},\mathbf{x}=(x_{i})_{i})
has distribution
m
, and where
\mu^{\infty}_{t}:=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}s_{j}\delta_{X^{j}_{t}}
,
t\in[t_{0},T]
is the
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
-valued process defined as in (
2.28
).
The necessity of allowing
b
to depend on
i
will become apparent in Section
5
. We emphasize that, by uniform bounded collection, we mean that
\sup_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}}\|b^{i}\|_{\infty}<+\infty
. Note also that we are using the convention in Remark
2.15
to evaluate
b_{t}^{i}(\mu_{t}^{\infty},\cdot)
at
X_{t}^{i}
.

4.1.
Weak existence and uniqueness in law

4.1.1.
Notion of weak solution and related statements

Here we address the well-posedness of equation (
4.1
). Given the low regularity of the drift, it is natural to look for weak solutions. In particular, for a collection of uniformly bounded and measurable drifts
(b^{i}\colon[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d})_{i}
, an initial time
t_{0}\in[0,T)
, and an initial distribution
\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}\in\mathcal{P}(T^{\infty}_{o}\times(\mathbb{T}^{d})_{o}^{\infty})
, we say that the tuple
({\Omega},{{\mathcal{F}}},{\mathbb{Q}},\{{{\mathcal{F}}}_{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]},(({\mathbf{s}},{\mathbf{x}}),({X}^{i})_{i},({W}^{i})_{i}))
is a
weak solution
of the equation (
4.1
) driven by the data
(b^{i})_{i}
,
t_{0}
,
m
if:

i.

({W}^{i})_{i}
is a family of independent
d
-dimensional
\{{{\mathcal{F}}}_{t}\}_{t\in[t_{0},T]}
-Brownian motions on the filtered probability space
({\Omega},{{\mathcal{F}}},{\mathbb{Q}},\{{{\mathcal{F}}}_{t}\}_{t\in[t_{0},T]})
;

ii.

The initial datum
({\mathbf{s}},{\mathbf{x}})
is a
T^{\infty}_{o}\times(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{\infty}_{o}
-valued
{{\mathcal{F}}}_{t_{0}}
-measurable random variable distributed according to
m
under
{\mathbb{Q}}
;

iii.

It holds:

{X}^{i}_{t}=\imath({x}^{i})+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}b_{r}^{i}({\mu}^{\infty}_{r},{X}^{i}_{r})\,\mathrm{d}r+\sqrt{\frac{2}{{s_{i}}}}W_{t}^{i},\quad t\in[t_{0},T],\quad i\in\mathbb{N}_{+},\quad{\mathbb{Q}}\text{-a.s.}

where
{\mu}^{\infty}_{t}:=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}{s}_{j}\delta_{{X}^{j}_{t}}
,
t\in[t_{0},T]
is the
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
-valued process defined as in (
2.28
).

Note that the solution depends on
t_{0}
,
m
, and
(b^{i})_{i}
. For the moment, we suppress this dependence to keep the notation lighter, but we will make it explicit whenever necessary.

As in the finite-dimensional setting, weak existence and uniqueness in law follow from a standard application of Girsanov theorem. Weak existence is ensured by the following statement.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
(b^{i}:[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d})_{i}
be a collection of uniformly bounded and measurable functions,
t_{0}\in[0,T)
be an initial time, and
\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}\in\mathcal{P}(T^{\infty}_{o}\times(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{\infty}_{o})
be an initial distribution.
Then, there exists a weak solution to the infinite system (
4.1
) driven by the data
(b^{i})_{i}
,
t_{0}
,
m
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, let us take
t_{0}=0
.
As in Subsection
2.3
, let us then consider a filtered probability space
(\Omega,{\mathcal{F}},\mathbb{P},\{{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]})
, an
{\mathcal{F}}_{0}
-measurable initial condition
(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x})\sim_{\mathbb{P}}\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}
and a sequence of
independent
d
-dimensional
\{{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
-Brownian motions
(B^{i})_{i}
.
We recall that the corresponding free particle system
(X^{i})_{i}
is given by

(4.2)

{\mathrm{d}}X^{i}_{t}=\sqrt{\frac{2}{s_{i}}}\,\mathrm{d}B^{i}_{t},\quad t\in[0,T],\quad X^{i}_{0}=\imath(x_{i}),\quad i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}.

We introduce the local martingale

(4.3)

Z_{t}:=\exp\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\sqrt{\frac{s_{i}}{2}}b_{r}^{i}(\mu^{\infty}_{r},X^{i}_{r})\cdot\,\mathrm{d}B^{i}_{r}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{s_{i}}{2}\lvert b_{r}^{i}(\mu^{\infty}_{r},X^{i}_{r})\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r\right\},\quad t\in[0,T],

where
\mu^{\infty}_{t}:=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}s_{j}\delta_{X^{j}_{t}}
,
t\in[0,T]
is as in (
2.28
). Since
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}s_{i}=1
and
\sup_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}}\left\|b^{i}\right\|_{\infty}<\infty
, the sum of the stochastic integrals inside the bracket forms a martingale whose quadratic variation is bounded by a deterministic constant. Therefore,
Z
is a true martingale with
Z_{0}=1
. Thus, by the infinite dimensional version of
Girsanov theorem (see e.g.
[
45
, Proposition I.0.6]
), the measure
{\mathbb{Q}}:=Z_{T}{\mathbb{P}}
is a probability measure, under which the processes

(4.4)

W^{i}_{t}:=B^{i}_{t}-\int_{0}^{t}\sqrt{\frac{s_{i}}{2}}b_{r}^{i}(\mu^{\infty}_{r},X^{i}_{r})\,\mathrm{d}r,\quad t\in[0,T],\quad i\in\mathbb{N}_{+},

are independent
{\{{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}}
-Brownian motions.
Then, the result is a consequence of the following two observations.
First, notice that the law of
(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x})

remains unchanged under the new probability
\mathbb{Q}
.
Second, by plugging (
4.4
) into (
4.2
), we obtain, for any
i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,

\,\mathrm{d}X^{i}_{t}=b_{t}^{i}(\mu^{\infty}_{t},X^{i}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\frac{2}{s_{i}}}\,\mathrm{d}W^{i}_{t},\quad t\in[0,T],

which means that
(\Omega,{\mathcal{F}},\mathbb{Q},\{{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]},((\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x}),(X^{i})_{i},(W^{i})_{i}))
is a weak solution to (
4.1
).∎

We now address uniqueness (in law).

Proposition 4.2
.

Let (
b^{i}:[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d})_{i}
be a collection of uniformly bounded and measurable functions,
t_{0}\in[0,T)
be an initial time, and
\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}\in\mathcal{P}(T^{\infty}_{o}\times(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{\infty}_{o})
be an initial distribution. If

(\Omega^{(k)},{\mathcal{F}}^{(k)},\mathbb{Q}^{(k)},\{{\mathcal{F}}^{(k)}_{t}\}_{t\in[t_{0},T]},((\mathbf{s}^{(k)},\mathbf{x}^{(k)}),(X^{i,(k)})_{i},(W^{i,(k)})_{i})),\quad k=1,2,

are
two weak solutions to (
4.1
), both driven by the data
(b^{i})_{i},t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}
, then
((\mathbf{s}^{(1)},\mathbf{x}^{(1)}),(X^{i,(1)})_{i},(W^{i,(1)})_{i})
and
((\mathbf{s}^{(2)},\mathbf{x}^{(2)}),(X^{i,(2)})_{i},(W^{i,(2)})_{i})
have the same law on
\Xi
under the probability measures
{\mathbb{Q}}^{(1)}
and

{\mathbb{Q}}^{(2)}
respectively.

Proof.

Following the proof of Proposition
4.1
, we assume
t_{0}=0
for simplicity.
Moreover,
from the two properties

\sup_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}}\|b^{i}\|_{\infty}<\infty
and
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}s^{(k)}_{i}=1
,
we notice that,
for
k=1,2
, the process defined by

\zeta^{(k)}_{t}:=\exp\left\{-\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\sqrt{\frac{s^{(k)}_{i}}{2}}b_{r}^{i}(\mu^{\infty,(k)}_{r},X^{i,(k)}_{r})\cdot\,\mathrm{d}W^{i,(k)}_{r}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{s^{(k)}_{i}}{2}\lvert b_{r}^{i}(\mu^{\infty,(k)}_{r},X^{i,(k)}_{r})\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r\right\},\quad t\in[0,T],

with
\mu^{\infty,(k)}_{t}:=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}s^{(k)}_{j}\delta_{X^{j,(k)}_{t}}
,
t\in[0,T]
as in (
2.28
),
is a
\mathbb{Q}^{(k)}
-martingale starting from
\zeta^{(k)}_{0}=1
. Then, by the same infinite dimensional version of
Girsanov theorem as in the proof of Proposition
4.1
, the processes
(B^{i,(k)})_{i}
, defined
for
k=1,2
by

(4.5)

B^{i,(k)}_{t}:=W^{i,(k)}_{t}+\int_{0}^{t}\sqrt{\frac{s^{(k)}_{i}}{2}}b_{r}^{i}(\mu^{\infty,(k)}_{r},X^{i,(k)}_{r})\,\mathrm{d}r,\quad t\in[0,T],\quad i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}

are, for each
k\in\{1,2\}
, independent
d
-dimensional
\{{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
-
Brownian motions under the new probability measure
\mathbb{P}^{(k)}:=\zeta_{T}^{(k)}{\mathbb{Q}}^{(k)}
(i.e.,

\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{(k)}/\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}^{(k)}=\zeta^{(k)}_{T}
). By plugging (
4.5
) into (
4.1
), we obtain

\,\mathrm{d}X^{i,(k)}_{t}=\sqrt{\frac{2}{s^{(k)}_{i}}}\,\mathrm{d}B^{i,(k)}_{t},\quad X^{i,(k)}_{0}=\imath(x^{(k)}_{i}),\quad t\in[0,T],\quad i\in\mathbb{N}_{+},

which proves (by returning
back to
(
4.5
))
that the tuples

((\mathbf{s}^{(1)},\mathbf{x}^{(1)}),(X^{i,(1)})_{i},(B^{i,(1)})_{i})\text{ and }((\mathbf{s}^{(2)},\mathbf{x}^{(2)}),(X^{i,(2)})_{i},(B^{i,(2)})_{i})

have the same law (on
the space
\Xi
) under the probability measures
{\mathbb{P}}^{(1)}
and

{\mathbb{P}}^{(2)}
respectively. Moreover, we can rewrite the inverse of

\zeta_{t}^{(k)}
in the form of an exponential martingale under
{\mathbb{P}}^{(k)}
, namely

\frac{1}{\zeta_{t}^{(k)}}=\exp\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\sqrt{\frac{s^{(k)}_{i}}{2}}b_{r}^{i}(\mu^{\infty,(k)}_{r},X^{i,(k)}_{r})\cdot\,\mathrm{d}B^{i,(k)}_{r}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{s^{(k)}_{i}}{2}\lvert b_{r}^{i}(\mu^{\infty,(k)}_{r},X^{i,(k)}_{r})\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r\right\},\ \ t\in[0,T].

Thanks to

[
58
, Lemmata 4.3.2 & 4.3.3]
, this
shows that
1/\zeta_{T}^{(1)}

and
1/\zeta_{T}^{(2)}

can be expressed as a common measurable function of
((\mathbf{s}^{(k)},\mathbf{x}^{(k)}),(X^{i,(k)})_{i},(B^{i,(k)})_{i})
.
Now,
for each
i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, we introduce the map (with the superscript
{\boldsymbol{b}}
in

\psi_{i}
below being a shorthand notation for
{\boldsymbol{b}}=(b^{j})_{j}
)

(4.6)

\begin{split}\psi_{i}^{\boldsymbol{b}}:T^{\infty}_{o}\times{\mathrm{C}}([0,T];{\mathbb{R}}^{d})^{\infty}\times{\mathrm{C}}([0,T];{\mathbb{R}}^{d})&\rightarrow{\mathrm{C}}([0,T];{\mathbb{R}}^{d})\\
\left((s_{j})_{j},((x_{t}^{j})_{t\in[0,T]})_{j},(w_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}\right)&\mapsto\left(w_{t}-\sqrt{\frac{s_{i}}{2}}\int_{0}^{t}b_{r}^{i}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}s_{j}\delta_{x_{r}^{j}},x_{r}^{i}\right)\,\mathrm{d}r\right)_{t\in[0,T]},\end{split}

which is clearly progressively-measurable. Again, we have used the convention that
x_{t}^{j}
is implicitly composed with the projection map as explained in Remark
2.15
. Then, we can let

(4.7)

\begin{split}\Psi_{i}^{\boldsymbol{b}}:T^{\infty}_{o}\times(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{\infty}_{o}\times{\mathrm{C}}([0,T];{\mathbb{R}}^{d})^{\infty}\times{\mathrm{C}}([0,T];{\mathbb{R}}^{d})&\rightarrow T^{\infty}_{o}\times(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{\infty}_{o}\times{\mathrm{C}}([0,T];{\mathbb{R}}^{d})\times{\mathrm{C}}([0,T];{\mathbb{R}}^{d})\\
\left((s_{j})_{j},(x_{j}^{0})_{j},(x^{j})_{j},w\right)&\mapsto\left((s_{j})_{j},(x_{j}^{0})_{j},x^{i},\psi_{i}^{\boldsymbol{b}}\left((s_{j})_{j},(x^{j})_{j},w\right)\right).\end{split}

Writing

(\mathbf{s}^{(k)},\mathbf{x}^{(k)},X^{i,(k)},W^{i,(k)})=\Psi_{i}^{\boldsymbol{b}}\left(\mathbf{s}^{(k)},\mathbf{x}^{(k)},(X^{j,(k)})_{j},B^{i,(k)}\right),

we deduce that for any integer
n\geq 1
, and any Borel subsets

\Gamma_{1},\cdots,\Gamma_{n}
of

T_{o}^{\infty}\times(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{\infty}_{o}\times{\mathrm{C}}([0,T];{\mathbb{R}}^{d})\times{\mathrm{C}}([0,T];{\mathbb{R}}^{d})
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}^{(1)}\left(\left\{(\mathbf{s}^{(1)},\mathbf{x}^{(1)},X^{1,(1)},W^{1,(1)})\in\Gamma_{1},\dots,(\mathbf{s}^{(1)},\mathbf{x}^{(1)},X^{n,(1)},W^{n,(1)})\in\Gamma_{n}\right\}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{Q}^{(1)}\left(\left\{(\mathbf{s}^{(1)},\mathbf{x}^{(1)},X^{1,(1)},B^{1,(1)})\in\Psi_{1}^{-1}(\Gamma_{1}),\dots,(\mathbf{s}^{(1)},\mathbf{x}^{(1)},X^{n,(1)},B^{n,(1)})\in\Psi_{n}^{-1}(\Gamma_{n})\right\}\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega^{(1)}}\frac{1}{\zeta^{(1)}_{T}}\mathbf{1}_{\{(\mathbf{s}^{(1)},\mathbf{x}^{(1)},X^{1,(1)},B^{1,(1)})\in\Psi_{1}^{-1}(\Gamma_{1}),\dots,(\mathbf{s}^{(1)},\mathbf{x}^{(1)},X^{n,(1)},B^{n,(1)})\in\Psi_{n}^{-1}(\Gamma_{n})\}}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega^{(2)}}\frac{1}{\zeta^{(2)}_{T}}\mathbf{1}_{\{(\mathbf{s}^{(2)},\mathbf{x}^{(2)},X^{1,(2)},B^{1,(2)})\in\Psi_{1}^{-1}(\Gamma_{2}),\dots,(\mathbf{s}^{(2)},\mathbf{x}^{(2)},X^{n,(2)},B^{n,(2)})\in\Psi_{n}^{-1}(\Gamma_{n})\}}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{Q}^{(2)}\left(\left\{(\mathbf{s}^{(2)},\mathbf{x}^{(2)},X^{1,(2)},W^{1,(2)})\in\Gamma_{1},\dots,(\mathbf{s}^{(2)},\mathbf{x}^{(2)},X^{n,(2)},W^{n,(2)})\in\Gamma_{n}\right\}\right),

where, to pass from the third to the fourth line, we used the fact that the integrands on both lines could be written as the images, by
a common measurable mapping, of
(\mathbf{s}^{(1)},\mathbf{x}^{(1)},(X^{1,(1)},B^{1,(1)}),\dots,(X^{n,(1)},B^{n,(1)}))

and
(\mathbf{s}^{(2)},\mathbf{x}^{(2)},(X^{1,(2)},B^{1,(2)}),\dots,(X^{n,(2)},B^{n,(2)}))

respectively. This completes the proof.
∎

4.1.2.
Transfer onto the canonical space

For later use, it is important to transfer weak solutions onto the canonical space

\Xi=T^{\infty}_{o}\times(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{\infty}_{o}\times{\mathrm{C}}([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\infty}\times{\mathrm{C}}([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\infty}
(see Subsection
2.3.1
).
Given a (unique in law) weak solution
({\Omega},{{\mathcal{F}}},{\mathbb{Q}},\{{{\mathcal{F}}}_{t}\}_{t\in[t_{0},T]},(({\mathbf{s}},{\mathbf{x}}),({X}^{i})_{i},({W}^{i})_{i}))
, we can indeed denote the law of the tuple
(({\mathbf{s}},{\mathbf{x}}),({X}^{i})_{i},({W}^{i})_{i})
on
\Xi
by
\mathbf{Q}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}
, where we highlight the dependence on the initial time and distribution. Then, it is easy to check that
(\Xi,{\mathcal{G}},\{{\mathcal{G}}_{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]},\mathbf{Q}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}},(((\varsigma^{j})_{j},(\xi^{0,j})_{j}),(\beta^{j})_{j},(\xi^{j})_{j}))
is another weak solution to (
4.1
), where we used the notation introduced in Subsection
2.3.1
for the canonical process. Since the probability measure
\mathbf{Q}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}
fully characterizes this solution, in what follows we simply refer to the weak solution on the canonical space through its law
\mathbf{Q}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}
. As made clear in the following statement, we can express
\mathbf{Q}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}
in terms of the law on the canonical space
\Xi
of the free particle system (
2.27
), denoted by
\mathbf{P}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}
.

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
(b^{i}:[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d})_{i}
be a collection of uniformly bounded and measurable functions,
t_{0}\in[0,T)
an initial time, and
\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{\infty}_{o}\times(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{\infty}_{o})
an initial distribution. Denote by
\mathbf{Q}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}
the law on
\Xi
of a weak solution to (
4.1
), and by
\mathbf{P}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}
the law on
\Xi
of the free particle system (
2.27
), respectively. Then

(4.8)

{\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}={\hbox to10.72pt{\vbox to10.72pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.3582pt\lower-5.3582pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.14 0 C 7.14 3.94 3.94 7.14 0 7.14 C -3.94 7.14 -7.14 3.94 -7.14 0 C -7.14 -3.94 -3.94 -7.14 0 -7.14 C 3.94 -7.14 7.14 -3.94 7.14 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.8889pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}_{\sharp}\left(\frac{1}{z_{T}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}\right),

where the mapping
{\hbox to10.72pt{\vbox to10.72pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.3582pt\lower-5.3582pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.14 0 C 7.14 3.94 3.94 7.14 0 7.14 C -3.94 7.14 -7.14 3.94 -7.14 0 C -7.14 -3.94 -3.94 -7.14 0 -7.14 C 3.94 -7.14 7.14 -3.94 7.14 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.8889pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}\colon\Xi\to\Xi
is defined by

(4.9)

{\hbox to10.72pt{\vbox to10.72pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.3582pt\lower-5.3582pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.14 0 C 7.14 3.94 3.94 7.14 0 7.14 C -3.94 7.14 -7.14 3.94 -7.14 0 C -7.14 -3.94 -3.94 -7.14 0 -7.14 C 3.94 -7.14 7.14 -3.94 7.14 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.8889pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}:\left((s_{j})_{j},(x_{j}^{0})_{j},(w^{j})_{j},(x^{j})_{j}\right)\mapsto\left((s_{j})_{j},(x_{j}^{0})_{j},\left(\psi_{j}^{\boldsymbol{b}}((s_{i})_{i},(x^{i})_{i},w^{j})\right)_{j},(x^{j})_{j}\right),

\psi_{i}^{\boldsymbol{b}}
is given by (
4.6
) for any
i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, and

(4.10)

\frac{1}{z^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}_{T}}=\exp\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\sqrt{\frac{\varsigma^{i}}{2}}\,b_{r}^{i}(\mu^{\infty}_{r},\xi_{r}^{i})\cdot\,\mathrm{d}\beta_{r}^{i}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\frac{\varsigma^{i}}{2}\,|b_{r}^{i}(\mu^{\infty}_{r},\xi_{r}^{i})|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r\right),

with
\mu^{\infty}:=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\varsigma^{i}\delta_{\xi^{i}}
(see (
2.30
)).

Note that in (
4.10
) (and in the rest of this section) we are using the same convention explained in Remark
2.15
to evaluate
b^{i}_{r}(\mu_{r}^{\infty},\cdot)
at
\xi_{r}^{i}
.

Proof.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and two families
\Gamma_{1}^{x},\cdots,\Gamma_{n}^{x}
and

\Gamma_{1}^{w},\cdots,\Gamma_{n}^{w}
of Borel subsets of
{\mathrm{C}}([0,T];{\mathbb{R}}^{d})
, let us consider the two cylinders
\Gamma_{1}^{x}\times\cdots\times\Gamma_{n}^{x}\times{\mathrm{C}}([0,T];{\mathbb{R}}^{d})^{\{n+1,\cdots\}}

and

\Gamma_{1}^{w}\times\cdots\times\Gamma_{n}^{w}\times{\mathrm{C}}([0,T];{\mathbb{R}}^{d})^{\{n+1,\cdots\}}
. Following the proof of Proposition

4.2
, we know that for an initial distribution

m
on
T^{\infty}_{o}\times({\mathbb{T}}^{d})^{\infty}_{o}
and for
a Borel subset
\Gamma_{0}
of

T^{\infty}_{o}\times({\mathbb{T}}^{d})^{\infty}_{o}
,
the law of the weak solution to (
4.1
), with

m
as initial condition,
is given by

(4.11)

\begin{split}&{\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}\left(\Gamma_{0}\times\Gamma_{1}^{x}\times\cdots\times\Gamma_{n}^{x}\times{\mathrm{C}}([0,T];{\mathbb{R}}^{d})^{\{n+1,\cdots\}}\times\Gamma_{1}^{w}\times\cdots\times\Gamma_{n}^{w}\times{\mathrm{C}}([0,T];{\mathbb{R}}^{d})^{\{n+1,\cdots\}}\right)\\
&=\int_{\Xi}\frac{1}{z^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}_{T}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbf{1}}_{(\Psi^{\boldsymbol{b}}_{i})^{-1}(\Gamma_{0}\times\Gamma^{x}_{i}\times\Gamma^{w}_{i})}((\varsigma^{j})_{j},(\xi^{0,j})_{j},\xi^{i},\beta^{i})\,\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{P}}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}},\end{split}

where
\frac{1}{z_{T}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}}
is the martingale given by (
4.10
) and
\Psi_{i}^{\boldsymbol{b}}
is defined by (
4.7
). Then (
4.8
) follows.
∎

Of course, it must be stressed that the random variable
z_{T}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}
is here regarded as a measurable function on
\Xi
. At this stage, the measurable function representing
z_{T}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}
depends on
m
, and one of the purposes of the forthcoming statements is to provide a version that is independent of
m
.

Remark 4.4

(Dependence of
\mathbf{Q}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}
and
z_{T}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}
on
(b^{i})_{i}
)
.

The map
z_{T}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}:\Xi\to\mathbb{R}
and the probability
\mathbf{Q}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}
depend also on the collection of drifts
(b^{i})_{i}
, but we do not stress this dependence in the notation, unless it is needed.

Similarly to what we did for
\mathbf{P}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}
, we adopt a lighter notation when dealing with a deterministic initial condition
(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x})\in T_{o}^{\infty}\times(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{\infty}_{o}
, setting

(4.12)

{\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x})}:={\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},\delta_{(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x})}}.

4.1.3.
A disintegration formula

This paragraph is devoted to establishing a disintegration formula for
{\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}
, closely resembling a weak Markov property. Our proof relies on the representation formula (
4.8
). While the result could alternatively be derived by adapting the notion of martingale problem to the present framework, it is more convenient here to exploit the explicit expression of the solutions.
Let us start with the analogous result for
{\mathbf{P}}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
t_{0}\in[0,T)
; then the map
\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}\in{\mathcal{P}}(T^{\infty}_{o}\times(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{\infty}_{o})\mapsto{\mathbf{P}}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}
is measurable, i.e., for any Borel subset
E\subset\Xi
, the map

\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}\in{\mathcal{P}}(T^{\infty}_{o}\times(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{\infty}_{o})\mapsto{\mathbf{P}}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}(E)
is Borel measurable.
Moreover, for any
\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}\in{\mathcal{P}}(T^{\infty}_{o}\times(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{\infty}_{o})
and any Borel subset
E\subset\Xi

(4.13)

{\mathbf{P}}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}(E)=\int_{T^{\infty}_{o}\times(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{\infty}_{o}}{\mathbf{P}}^{t_{0},(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x})}(E)\,\mathrm{d}\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x}).

Observe that,
T^{\infty}_{o}\times(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{\infty}_{o}
being a Polish space, the notion of measurability that is used in the statement is consistent with the one described in Subsection
2.0.2
.

Proof.

We first notice that the mapping
\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}\mapsto\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}\otimes\mathcal{W}_{t_{0}}^{\infty}
is continuous, where
\mathcal{W}_{t_{0}}^{\infty}
has been defined in Subsection
2.3.1
. We deduce that, for any Borel subset
E\subset\Xi
, the mapping

\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}\mapsto{\mathbf{P}}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}(E)=\left(\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}\otimes\mathcal{W}_{t_{0}}^{\infty}\right)\left((\textrm{\rm id},\Phi)^{-1}(E)\right)

is measurable, where we recall (
2.29
) for the definition
of

(\textrm{\rm id},\Phi)
. This proves the first claim in the statement.

As for (
4.13
), we notice that, for any Borel subset
E\subset\Xi
,

\begin{split}{\mathbf{P}}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}(E)&=\int_{\Xi^{0}}{\mathbf{1}}_{E}\circ(\textrm{\rm id},\Phi)\left(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x},(w^{i})_{i}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\left(\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}\otimes\mathcal{W}_{t_{0}}^{\infty}\right)\left(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x},(w^{i})_{i}\right)\\
&=\int_{T^{\infty}_{o}\times(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{\infty}_{o}}\left[\int_{{\mathrm{C}}([0,T];{\mathbb{R}}^{d})^{\infty}}{\mathbf{1}}_{E}\circ(\textrm{\rm id},\Phi)\left(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x},(w^{i})_{i}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{W}_{t_{0}}^{\infty}\left((w^{i})_{i}\right)\right]\,\mathrm{d}\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x})\\
&=\int_{T^{\infty}_{o}\times(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{\infty}_{o}}{\mathbf{P}}^{t_{0},(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x})}(E)\,\mathrm{d}\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x}),\end{split}

which completes the proof.
∎

Before establishing an analogous disintegration formula for
\mathbf{Q}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}
, it is necessary to analyze the measurability of the map
\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}\mapsto\mathbf{Q}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}
, which in turn requires several technical preliminaries. The first step is to consider the measurability of
\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}\mapsto z_{T}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}
, where
z_{T}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}
is defined in Proposition
4.3
. Observe that
z_{T}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}
depends on the
collection of drifts
(b^{i})_{i}
, although we suppress this dependence in the notation for the time being.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
(b^{i}:[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d})_{i}
be a collection of uniformly bounded and measurable functions and
t_{0}\in[0,T)
be an initial time. There exists a measurable mapping
\zeta_{T}^{t_{0}}:\Xi\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
such that, for

{\mathcal{D}}
-a.e.
m\in{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
,
{\mathbf{P}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m)}(\{\zeta_{T}^{t_{0}}=z_{T}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m)}\})=1
.

The main difficulty in the above statement lies in constructing a mapping
\zeta_{T}^{t_{0}}
that is independent of
m
, at least when
m
ranges over a
{\mathcal{D}}
-full subset of
{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
. The existence of such a
\zeta_{T}^{t_{0}}
, however, follows directly from the next lemma. For clarity, recall that
\Xi
is equipped with its Borel
\sigma
-field
\mathcal{G}
and the canonical filtration
\{{\mathcal{G}_{t}}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
(b^{i}:[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d})_{i}
be a collection of measurable functions
that are all dominated in norm by some
b\in L^{2}([0,T],L^{2}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d},\overline{\mathcal{D}};\mathbb{R}^{d}))
,
and
t_{0}\in[0,T]
be an initial time. There exists a progressively-measurable mapping
{\mathcal{I}}^{t_{0}}:\Xi\rightarrow{\mathrm{C}}([0,T];{\mathbb{R}}^{d})
such that for
{\mathcal{D}}
-a.e.
m\in{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
,

{\mathbf{P}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m)}\left(\left\{\forall t\in[0,T],\quad{\mathcal{I}}_{t}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\sqrt{\frac{\varsigma^{i}}{2}}b_{r}^{i}(\mu^{\infty}_{r},\xi^{i}_{r})\cdot\,\mathrm{d}\beta_{r}^{i}\right\}\right)=1.

The fact that Lemma

4.7
implies
Lemma
4.6
is quite obvious. It suffices to let

\zeta_{T}^{t_{0}}:=\exp\left(-{\mathcal{I}}^{t_{0}}_{T}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\frac{\varsigma^{i}}{2}|b_{r}^{i}(\mu^{\infty}_{r},\xi^{i}_{r})|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r\right).

We thus concentrate on the proof of the first lemma.

Proof of Lemma
4.7
.

By the domination condition,
we first observe that

\begin{split}{\mathbb{E}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}_{\sharp}({\mathcal{D}})}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\varsigma^{i}|b_{r}^{i}(\mu^{\infty}_{r},\xi_{r}^{i})|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r\right]&\leq{\mathbb{E}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}_{\sharp}({\mathcal{D}})}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|b_{r}(\mu^{\infty}_{r},x)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{r}^{\infty}(x)\right)\,\mathrm{d}r\right]\\
&=\int_{0}^{T}\left[\int_{{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|b_{r}(m,x)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}m(x)\right)\,\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{D}}(m)\right]\,\mathrm{d}r<\infty,\end{split}

where we used
Proposition
2.14
to get the second line. This implies that the stochastic integrals appearing in the statement are well-defined and form a continuous martingale.
Following
[
58
, Lemmata 4.3.2 & 4.3.3]
, we can construct a progressively-measurable mapping

{\mathcal{I}}^{t_{0}}:\Xi\rightarrow{\mathrm{C}}([0,T];{\mathbb{R}}^{d})
such that

{\mathbf{P}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}_{\sharp}({\mathcal{D}})}\left(\left\{\forall t\in[0,T],\quad{\mathcal{I}}^{t_{0}}_{t}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\sqrt{\frac{\varsigma^{i}}{2}}b_{r}^{i}(\mu_{r}^{\infty},\xi_{r}^{i})\cdot\,\mathrm{d}\beta_{r}^{i}\right\}\right)=1.

Note that
\mathcal{I}_{t}^{t_{0}}=0
for every
t\in[0,t_{0}]
, since, by the definition of
\mathbf{P}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}_{\sharp}({\mathcal{D}})}
(see (
2.29
)), the processes
(\beta^{i})_{i}
coincide with the path identical to zero on
[0,t_{0}]
. In fact, the same results say that there exists a sequence

({\mathcal{I}}^{t_{0},(n)})_{n}

of stochastic Riemann sums
such that

\forall\varepsilon>0,\quad\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbf{P}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}_{\sharp}({\mathcal{D}})}\left(\left\{\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|{\mathcal{I}}^{t_{0},(n)}_{t}-{\mathcal{I}}^{t_{0}}_{t}|\geq\varepsilon\right\}\right)=0.

By stochastic Riemann sums, we mean that each

{\mathcal{I}}^{t_{0},(n)}:\Xi\to{\mathrm{C}}([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d})
can be written in the form

(4.14)

{\mathcal{I}}^{t_{0},(n)}_{t}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N(n)}H_{k}^{(n),i}\cdot\left(\beta_{t_{k}^{(n)}\wedge t}^{i}-\beta_{t_{k-1}^{(n)}\wedge t}^{i}\right),\quad t\in[0,T],

for

N(n)\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
(typically,
N(n)
tends to
\infty
as
n
tends to
\infty
), for

0=t_{0}^{(n)}<t_{1}^{(n)}<\cdots<t_{N(n)}^{(n)}=T
a partition of
[0,T]

(typically, its step-size tends
0
as
n
tends to
\infty
),
and for

((H_{k}^{(n),i})_{i})_{k=1}^{N(n)}

a collection of

N(n)

{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
-valued
bounded random variables such that

H_{k}^{(n),i}
is
{\mathcal{G}}_{t_{k-1}^{(n)}}
-measurable
for each
k\in\{1,\cdots,N(n)\}
and each
i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
.
Using the disintegration formula (
4.13
) for
{\mathbf{P}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}_{\sharp}({\mathcal{D}})}
,
we can rewrite this as

\forall\varepsilon>0,\quad\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}{\mathbf{P}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m)}\left(\left\{\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|{\mathcal{I}}^{t_{0},(n)}_{t}-{\mathcal{I}}^{t_{0}}_{t}|\geq\varepsilon\right\}\right)\,\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{D}}(m)=0.

Up to a subsequence
k\mapsto n_{k}
, we easily deduce that, for

{\mathcal{D}}
-a.e.
m
,

\forall\varepsilon\in(0,\infty)\cap\mathbb{Q},\quad\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbf{P}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m)}\left(\left\{\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|{\mathcal{I}}^{t_{0},(n_{k})}_{t}-{\mathcal{I}}^{t_{0}}_{t}|\geq\varepsilon\right\}\right)=0.

(Obviously, the same result holds true when
\varepsilon
is taken in the entire
(0,\infty)
)
Since
{\mathcal{I}}^{t_{0},(n_{k})}
is a stochastic Riemann sum, we deduce that, for
(\mathrm{em}^{-1})_{\sharp}{\mathcal{D}}
-a.e.
(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x})
,

{\mathcal{I}}^{t_{0}}
provides a version of the stochastic integral
(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{t}{\sqrt{\varsigma^{i}/2}}b_{r}^{i}(\mu_{r}^{\infty},\xi_{r}^{i})\cdot\,\mathrm{d}\beta_{r}^{i})_{t\in[0,T]}
.
∎

As a result of the disintegration Lemma
4.5
, we notice that, for any
t_{0}\in[0,T)
and for any bounded
{\mathcal{D}}
-density
p
(that is,
p:{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\rightarrow[0,\infty)
is a measurable function, with the
property that
\int_{{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}p(m)\,\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{D}}(m)=1
), it holds

(4.15)

\begin{split}\text{ for every Borel subset }E\subset\Xi,\quad{\mathbf{P}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}_{\sharp}(p{\mathcal{D}})}(E)&=\int_{{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}p(m){\mathbf{P}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m)}(E)\,\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{D}}(m)\\
&\leq\|p\|_{\infty}\int_{{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}{\mathbf{P}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m)}(E)\,\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{D}}(m)\\
&=\|p\|_{\infty}{\mathbf{P}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}_{\sharp}{\mathcal{D}}}(E).\end{split}

From (
4.15
) and Proposition
2.14
we also immediately infer that

(4.16)

(\mu_{t}^{\infty})_{\sharp}{\mathbf{P}}^{t_{0},{\mathrm{em}_{\sharp}^{-1}}(p{\mathcal{D}})}\leq\|p\|_{\infty}{\mathcal{D}}\quad\text{ for every }t\in[t_{0},T].

And, then repeating the proof of Lemmata
4.6

and

4.7
, we obtain the following result.

Corollary 4.8
.

In the same framework as
Lemma
4.6
, if
p
is a bounded
{\mathcal{D}}
-density, then

{\mathbf{P}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}_{\sharp}(p{\mathcal{D}})}\left(\left\{\zeta_{T}^{t_{0}}=z_{T}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}_{\sharp}(p{\mathcal{D}})}\right\}\right)=1.

We now state the main disintegration formula.

Proposition 4.9
.

Let

(b^{i}:[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d})_{i}
be a
collection of uniformly bounded and measurable functions, and
t_{0}\in[0,T)
be an initial time. Then, for every Borel subset
E\subset\Xi
, the mapping

(4.17)

m\in{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\mapsto{\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m)}(E)

is measurable for the completion of the Borel
\sigma
-field on

{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
under
{\mathcal{D}}
. Moreover, if
p
is a bounded
{\mathcal{D}}
-density, then for every Borel subset
E\subset\Xi

(4.18)

{\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}_{\sharp}(p{\mathcal{D}})}(E)=\int_{{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}p(m){\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m)}(E)\,\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{D}}(m).

Proof.

By combining (
4.8
)
(but omitting, for simplicity, the superscript
{\boldsymbol{b}}
in the notation
\hbox to10.72pt{\vbox to10.72pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.3582pt\lower-5.3582pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.14 0 C 7.14 3.94 3.94 7.14 0 7.14 C -3.94 7.14 -7.14 3.94 -7.14 0 C -7.14 -3.94 -3.94 -7.14 0 -7.14 C 3.94 -7.14 7.14 -3.94 7.14 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.8889pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}
)
with Lemma
4.6
, we deduce that there exists a Borel
subset
O
of
{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
such that

{\mathcal{D}}(O)=1
and, for any
m\in O

and any Borel subset
E\subset\Xi
,

{\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m)}(E)={\hbox to10.72pt{\vbox to10.72pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.3582pt\lower-5.3582pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.14 0 C 7.14 3.94 3.94 7.14 0 7.14 C -3.94 7.14 -7.14 3.94 -7.14 0 C -7.14 -3.94 -3.94 -7.14 0 -7.14 C 3.94 -7.14 7.14 -3.94 7.14 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.8889pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}_{\sharp}\left(\frac{1}{z_{T}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m)}}{\mathbf{P}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m)}\right)(E)=\int_{\Xi}\frac{1}{\zeta_{T}^{t_{0}}}{\mathbf{1}}_{{\hbox to8.18pt{\vbox to8.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.08989pt\lower-4.08989pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.38 0 C 5.38 2.97 2.97 5.38 0 5.38 C -2.97 5.38 -5.38 2.97 -5.38 0 C -5.38 -2.97 -2.97 -5.38 0 -5.38 C 2.97 -5.38 5.38 -2.97 5.38 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.06946pt}{-2.39166pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}^{-1}(E)}\,\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{P}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m)}.

We observe from Lemma
4.5
that the right-hand side is measurable w.r.t.

m
(using in addition the fact that any positive measurable function can be approximated, pointwise,
by non-decreasing limits of simple functions).
This proves the measurability of the mapping
(
4.17
).

As in the proof of the disintegration formula, we can
restart from the last display, but under the measure

\mathrm{em}^{-1}_{\sharp}(p{\mathcal{D}})

(which is possible thanks to Corollary
4.8
). And then, using also (
4.13
), we get

\begin{split}{\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}_{\sharp}(p{\mathcal{D}})}(E)&=\int_{\Xi}\frac{1}{\zeta_{T}^{t_{0}}}{\mathbf{1}}_{{\hbox to8.18pt{\vbox to8.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.08989pt\lower-4.08989pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.38 0 C 5.38 2.97 2.97 5.38 0 5.38 C -2.97 5.38 -5.38 2.97 -5.38 0 C -5.38 -2.97 -2.97 -5.38 0 -5.38 C 2.97 -5.38 5.38 -2.97 5.38 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.06946pt}{-2.39166pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}^{-1}(E)}\,\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{P}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}_{\sharp}(p{\mathcal{D}})}\\
&=\int_{{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\left(\int_{\Xi}\frac{1}{\zeta_{T}^{t_{0}}}{\mathbf{1}}_{{\hbox to8.18pt{\vbox to8.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.08989pt\lower-4.08989pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.38 0 C 5.38 2.97 2.97 5.38 0 5.38 C -2.97 5.38 -5.38 2.97 -5.38 0 C -5.38 -2.97 -2.97 -5.38 0 -5.38 C 2.97 -5.38 5.38 -2.97 5.38 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.06946pt}{-2.39166pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}^{-1}(E)}\,\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{P}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m)}\right)p(m)\,\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{D}}(m)\\
&=\int_{{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}p(m){\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m)}(E)\,\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{D}}(m),\end{split}

which
proves
(
4.18
), and then
completes the proof.
∎

4.1.4.
On the choice of a version for
b

In this paragraph, we restrict ourselves to the case when all the entries of the collection
(b^{i})_{i}
are the same, i.e., there exists
b
such that
b^{i}=b
for each
i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
. In this framework,
it is important (especially for the applications in Section
5
) to see what our construction becomes when only a version of
b
is given.
Assume indeed that
\tilde{b}
is another measurable version of
b
, that is, for
\mathscr{L}^{[0,T]}\otimes{\mathcal{D}}
almost every

(t,\mu)\in[0,T]\times{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
(so that
\mu
is purely atomic),
and for any
x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
such that
\mu_{x}>0

b_{t}(\mu,x)=\tilde{b}_{t}(\mu,x),

or, equivalently,
for
\mathscr{L}^{[0,T]}\otimes{\mathcal{D}}
-almost every

(t,\mu)
,
b(t,\mu,\cdot)
and
\tilde{b}(t,\mu,\cdot)
coincide under
\mu
. Given these two measurable versions, we can consider the two associated systems of weak solutions (on the canonical space) to (
4.1
)
(with velocity fields that are independent of
i
), namely

\left({\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x})}\right)_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x}}\quad\text{and}\quad\left(\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x})}\right)_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x}},

where
t_{0}\in[0,T)
and
(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x})
varies in
T^{\infty}_{o}\times(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{\infty}_{o}
.
The goal of this subsection is to prove the following consistency result.

Proposition 4.10
.

Let
t_{0}\in[0,T)
,
b\colon[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be bounded and measurable, and
\tilde{b}\colon[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a measurable version of
b
in the sense described above. Then, for
{\mathcal{D}}
-a.e.
m
,

{\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m)}=\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m)},

where
{\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m)}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m)}
denote the laws of the weak solutions to (
4.1
) with

i
-independent
drifts
b
and
\tilde{b}
, respectively.

The proof of Proposition
4.10
relies on the following lemma, proved later in this subsection.

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
t_{0}\in[0,T)
,
p
be a bounded
{\mathcal{D}}
-density,
and denote by

{\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},{\mathrm{em}_{\sharp}^{-1}}(p{\mathcal{D}})}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},{\mathrm{em}_{\sharp}^{-1}}(p{\mathcal{D}})}

the laws of the two weak solutions of (
4.1
), both initialized from the measure

{\mathrm{em}_{\sharp}^{-1}}(p{\mathcal{D}})
(equivalently,
\mu^{\infty}_{t_{0}}
is sampled from
p{\mathcal{D}}
), and driven by the velocity fields
b
and

\tilde{b}
respectively.
Then,

{\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},{\mathrm{em}_{\sharp}^{-1}}(p{\mathcal{D}})}=\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},{\mathrm{em}_{\sharp}^{-1}}(p{\mathcal{D}})}.

Assuming the above lemma, we now turn to the proof of Proposition
4.10
.

Proof of Proposition

4.10
.

Let
p
be a fixed bounded
{\mathcal{D}}
-density. By the disintegration formula
(
4.18
), it holds

{\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},{\mathrm{em}_{\sharp}^{-1}}(p{\mathcal{D}})}=\int_{{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}p(m){\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m)}\,\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{D}}(m).

Analogously, we can write the same formula for the weak solution associated to the version
\tilde{b}
of the drift. Then, by Lemma
4.11
, we can equate the two formulas and obtain

\begin{split}\int_{{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}p(m){\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m)}\,\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{D}}(m)=\int_{{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}p(m)\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m)}\,\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{D}}(m),\end{split}

which completes the proof of Proposition
4.10
.
∎

The next step is to prove Lemma
4.11
, and to do so, we need to introduce another lemma.

Lemma 4.12
.

With the same notation as in the statement of Lemma

4.11
, one has, for any

t\in[t_{0},T)
,

(\mu_{t}^{\infty})_{\sharp}{\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},{\mathrm{em}_{\sharp}^{-1}}(p{\mathcal{D}})}\ll{\mathcal{D}},

i.e., the (time) marginal laws of

\mu^{\infty}
(see (
2.30
)) under

{\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},{\mathrm{em}_{\sharp}^{-1}}(p{\mathcal{D}})}

are absolutely
continuous with respect to

{\mathcal{D}}.

Proof.

For any Borel subset
E
of
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
satisfying
{\mathcal{D}}(E)=0
, we aim to prove that, for a fixed
t\in[t_{0},T]
,

(4.19)

{\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},{\mathrm{em}_{\sharp}^{-1}}(p{\mathcal{D}})}\left(\left\{\mu_{t}^{\infty}\in E\right\}\right)=0.

To simplify the exposition, let us denote

{\mathrm{em}_{\sharp}^{-1}}(p{\mathcal{D}})

by
m
. We recall from
(
4.8
) that

{\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}
is given by

{\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}=\hbox to10.72pt{\vbox to10.72pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.3582pt\lower-5.3582pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.14 0 C 7.14 3.94 3.94 7.14 0 7.14 C -3.94 7.14 -7.14 3.94 -7.14 0 C -7.14 -3.94 -3.94 -7.14 0 -7.14 C 3.94 -7.14 7.14 -3.94 7.14 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.8889pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{b}_{\sharp}\left(\frac{1}{z^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}_{T}}{\mathbf{P}}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}\right),

where
{\hbox to10.72pt{\vbox to10.72pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.3582pt\lower-5.3582pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.14 0 C 7.14 3.94 3.94 7.14 0 7.14 C -3.94 7.14 -7.14 3.94 -7.14 0 C -7.14 -3.94 -3.94 -7.14 0 -7.14 C 3.94 -7.14 7.14 -3.94 7.14 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.8889pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{b}}
is as in Proposition
4.3
with
b

i
-independent.
Then, we observe that
\mu^{\infty}
is invariant by
{\hbox to10.72pt{\vbox to10.72pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.3582pt\lower-5.3582pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.14 0 C 7.14 3.94 3.94 7.14 0 7.14 C -3.94 7.14 -7.14 3.94 -7.14 0 C -7.14 -3.94 -3.94 -7.14 0 -7.14 C 3.94 -7.14 7.14 -3.94 7.14 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.8889pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{b}}
, that is

\mu^{\infty}\circ{\hbox to10.72pt{\vbox to10.72pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.3582pt\lower-5.3582pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.14 0 C 7.14 3.94 3.94 7.14 0 7.14 C -3.94 7.14 -7.14 3.94 -7.14 0 C -7.14 -3.94 -3.94 -7.14 0 -7.14 C 3.94 -7.14 7.14 -3.94 7.14 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.8889pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{b}}=\mu^{\infty}
. As a result, we have

\begin{split}{\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}\left(\left\{\mu_{t}^{\infty}\in E\right\}\right)=\left(\frac{1}{z^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}_{T}}{\mathbf{P}}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}\right)\left(\left\{\mu_{t}^{\infty}\in E\right\}\right).\end{split}

In particular, to prove
(
4.19
), it suffices to prove that
the right-hand side on the above display is equal to
0
. But, then, it is enough to show that

{\mathbf{P}}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}\left(\left\{\mu_{t}^{\infty}\in E\right\}\right)=0

which follows from (
4.16
).
∎

Now, we can finally prove Lemma
4.11
.

Proof of Lemma
4.11
.

By uniqueness in law, it is sufficient to prove that, with probability 1 under

{\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}
, with

\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}:={\mathrm{em}_{\sharp}^{-1}}(p{\mathcal{D}})
, for any

i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and

t\in[t_{0},T]
,

\int_{t_{0}}^{t}b_{r}(\mu^{\infty}_{r},\xi^{i}_{r})\,\mathrm{d}r=\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\tilde{b}_{r}(\mu^{\infty}_{r},\xi^{i}_{r})\,\mathrm{d}r.

Obviously, it suffices to check that

(4.20)

\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{\Xi}\left|b_{t}(\mu_{t}^{\infty},\xi^{i}_{t})-\tilde{b}_{t}(\mu_{t}^{\infty},\xi^{i}_{t})\right|\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{Q}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}\,\mathrm{d}t=0\quad\text{ for all }i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}.

Since by Lemma
4.12
we have that
(\mu_{t}^{\infty})_{\sharp}\mathbf{Q}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}\ll{\mathcal{D}}
, the
\mathscr{L}^{[0,T]}\otimes\overline{{\mathcal{D}}}
-a.e. equality between
b
and
\tilde{b}
gives that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\left|b_{t}(\mu,x)-\tilde{b}_{t}(\mu,x)\right|\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\,\mathrm{d}\left[(\mu_{t}^{\infty})_{\sharp}\mathbf{Q}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}\right](\mu)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{\Xi}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\left|b_{t}(\mu_{t}^{\infty},x)-\tilde{b}_{t}(\mu_{t}^{\infty},x)\right|\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}^{\infty}(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{Q}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\int_{\Xi}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\varsigma_{i}\left|b_{t}(\mu_{t}^{\infty},\xi_{t}^{i})-\tilde{b}_{t}(\mu_{t}^{\infty},\xi_{t}^{i})\right|\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{Q}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}\,\mathrm{d}t.

In particular,

\left|b_{t}(\mu_{t}^{\infty},\xi_{t}^{i})-\tilde{b}_{t}(\mu_{t}^{\infty},\xi_{t}^{i})\right|=0\quad\mathscr{L}^{[0,T]}\otimes{\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}}\text{-a.e.}

This gives (
4.20
) and concludes the proof.
∎

4.2.
Chain rule and relation with the backward Kolmogorov equation

The main purpose of this subsection is to identify the generator of the solution to
the particle system (
4.1
)
(when driven by a drift
b
that is independent of
i
)
with the operator governing the PDE (
3.10
). Throughout, we make a repeated use of the spaces
H
and
H^{1,2}
introduced in
(
2.21
). For convenience, we recall their definitions below:
