for any
k\in\{1,\cdots,N(n)\}
and
i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
.
Then,

(4.61)

{\mathcal{I}}^{t_{0},(n)}_{t}\circ\hbox to10.72pt{\vbox to10.72pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.3582pt\lower-5.3582pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.14 0 C 7.14 3.94 3.94 7.14 0 7.14 C -3.94 7.14 -7.14 3.94 -7.14 0 C -7.14 -3.94 -3.94 -7.14 0 -7.14 C 3.94 -7.14 7.14 -3.94 7.14 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.8889pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{N(n)}H_{k}^{(n),i}\cdot\left(\left[\beta_{t_{k}^{(n)}\wedge t}^{i}-\beta_{t_{k-1}^{(n)}\wedge t}^{i}\right]+\sqrt{\frac{\varsigma^{i}}{2}}\int_{t_{k-1}^{(n)}\wedge t}^{t_{k}^{(n)}\wedge t}\alpha_{r}^{i}\left(\mu_{r}^{\infty},\xi_{r}^{i}\right)\,\mathrm{d}r\right),\quad t\in[0,T].

In order to complete this sub-step, we must derive further estimates on the rate of convergence of the time-discrete approximation considered above. We compute

(4.62)

\begin{split}&\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\left|\sum_{k=1}^{N(n)}{\mathbf{1}}_{[t_{k-1}^{(n)},t_{k}^{(n)})}(s)H_{k}^{(n),i}-h_{s}^{(n),i}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{N(n)}\int_{t_{k-1}^{(n)}}^{t_{k}^{(n)}}\left|h_{t_{k}^{(n)}}^{(n),i}-h_{s}^{(n),i}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&\leq\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}2\varsigma^{i}n^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N(n)}\int_{t_{k-1}^{(n)}}^{t_{k}^{(n)}}\left|\int_{0}^{T}\left[\varrho(n(t_{k}^{(n)}-r))-\varrho(n(s-r))\right]\eta_{n}\left({\rm D}u_{r}\left(\mu_{r}^{\infty},\xi_{r}^{i}\right)\right)\,\mathrm{d}r\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&\leq\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}2\|\varrho^{\prime}\|_{\infty}^{2}\varsigma^{i}n^{4}T^{2}\max_{k=1,\cdots,N(n)}\left|t_{k}^{(n)}-t_{k-1}^{(n)}\right|^{2}\int_{0}^{T}\left|{\rm D}u_{r}\left(\mu_{r}^{\infty},\xi_{r}^{i}\right)\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r\\
&=2T^{2}\|\varrho^{\prime}\|_{\infty}^{2}n^{-2}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\varsigma^{i}\left|{\rm D}u_{r}\left(\mu_{r}^{\infty},\xi_{r}^{i}\right)\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r,\end{split}

where we used the choice of the step size in (
4.59
). As a consequence of (
4.54
), (
4.62
) and standard approximation techniques in
L^{2}
, we conclude that, for
m\in O
, we have,
{\mathbf{P}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m)}
a.s.,

(4.63)

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\left|\sqrt{2\varsigma^{i}}{\rm D}u_{r}(\mu^{\infty}_{r},\xi_{r}^{i})-\sum_{k=1}^{N(n)}{\mathbf{1}}_{[t_{k-1}^{(n)},t_{k}^{(n)})}(r)H_{k}^{(n),i}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r=0.

Using
(
4.63
), once again that
\mu^{\infty}\circ\hbox to10.72pt{\vbox to10.72pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.3582pt\lower-5.3582pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.14 0 C 7.14 3.94 3.94 7.14 0 7.14 C -3.94 7.14 -7.14 3.94 -7.14 0 C -7.14 -3.94 -3.94 -7.14 0 -7.14 C 3.94 -7.14 7.14 -3.94 7.14 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.8889pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=\mu^{\infty}
,

\xi^{i}\circ\hbox to10.72pt{\vbox to10.72pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.3582pt\lower-5.3582pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.14 0 C 7.14 3.94 3.94 7.14 0 7.14 C -3.94 7.14 -7.14 3.94 -7.14 0 C -7.14 -3.94 -3.94 -7.14 0 -7.14 C 3.94 -7.14 7.14 -3.94 7.14 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.8889pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=\xi^{i}
,
H^{(n),i}_{k}\circ\hbox to10.72pt{\vbox to10.72pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.3582pt\lower-5.3582pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.14 0 C 7.14 3.94 3.94 7.14 0 7.14 C -3.94 7.14 -7.14 3.94 -7.14 0 C -7.14 -3.94 -3.94 -7.14 0 -7.14 C 3.94 -7.14 7.14 -3.94 7.14 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.8889pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=H^{(n),i}_{k},

for any
k\in\{1,\cdots,N(n)\}
and
i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
, and (
4.55
), we notice that, for any
m\in O
,

{\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m),{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}
-a.s.

(4.64)

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\left|\sqrt{2\varsigma^{i}}{\rm D}u_{r}(\mu^{\infty}_{r},\xi_{r}^{i})-\sum_{k=1}^{N(n)}{\mathbf{1}}_{[t_{k-1}^{(n)},t_{k}^{(n)})}(r)H_{k}^{(n),i}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r=0.

The objective, in the rest of this sub-step, is to prove that
the
above convergence
also holds in

L^{1}
under
{\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m),\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
.
Using the contraction property of the convolution in
L^{2}
-norm,
it is standard to check that

\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{T}|h_{s}^{(n),i}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\leq 2\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\varsigma^{i}\int_{0}^{T}\left|{\rm D}u_{r}\left(\mu_{r}^{\infty},\xi_{r}^{i}\right)\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r.

From this and (
4.62
), we deduce that

(4.65)

\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\left|\sum_{k=1}^{N(n)}{\mathbf{1}}_{[t_{k-1}^{(n)},t_{k}^{(n)})}(r)H_{k}^{(n),i}\right|^{2}\leq 4(1+T^{2}\|\varrho^{\prime}\|^{2}_{\infty})\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\varsigma^{i}\int_{0}^{T}\left|{\rm D}u_{r}\left(\mu_{r}^{\infty},\xi_{r}^{i}\right)\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r.

We further notice
that, since the collection

(\alpha^{i})_{i}
is uniformly bounded, we have an improved version of the estimate (
4.49
). Precisely, for any
m\in O
,
(
4.56
)
can be strengthened into

{\mathbb{E}}^{{\mathbf{P}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m)}}\left[\left(\frac{1}{z_{T}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m),\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\right)^{p}\right]<\infty,

for every
p\geq 1
.
Recalling (
4.54
) and (
4.55
), and using the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality,
together with the facts that

\mu^{\infty}\circ\hbox to10.72pt{\vbox to10.72pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.3582pt\lower-5.3582pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.14 0 C 7.14 3.94 3.94 7.14 0 7.14 C -3.94 7.14 -7.14 3.94 -7.14 0 C -7.14 -3.94 -3.94 -7.14 0 -7.14 C 3.94 -7.14 7.14 -3.94 7.14 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.8889pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=\mu^{\infty}
and

\xi^{i}\circ\hbox to10.72pt{\vbox to10.72pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.3582pt\lower-5.3582pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.14 0 C 7.14 3.94 3.94 7.14 0 7.14 C -3.94 7.14 -7.14 3.94 -7.14 0 C -7.14 -3.94 -3.94 -7.14 0 -7.14 C 3.94 -7.14 7.14 -3.94 7.14 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.8889pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=\xi^{i}
,
we then deduce that, for every
q\in(1,2)
,

(4.66)

\begin{split}&{\mathbb{E}}^{{\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m),{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}}\left[\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\varsigma^{i}\left|{\rm D}u_{r}(\mu^{\infty}_{r},\xi_{r}^{i})\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r\right)^{q/2}\right]\\
&={\mathbb{E}}^{{\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m),{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}}\left[\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\varsigma^{i}\left|{\rm D}u_{r}\left(\mu^{\infty}_{r}\circ\hbox to10.72pt{\vbox to10.72pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.3582pt\lower-5.3582pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.14 0 C 7.14 3.94 3.94 7.14 0 7.14 C -3.94 7.14 -7.14 3.94 -7.14 0 C -7.14 -3.94 -3.94 -7.14 0 -7.14 C 3.94 -7.14 7.14 -3.94 7.14 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.8889pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}},\xi_{r}^{i}\circ\hbox to10.72pt{\vbox to10.72pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.3582pt\lower-5.3582pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.14 0 C 7.14 3.94 3.94 7.14 0 7.14 C -3.94 7.14 -7.14 3.94 -7.14 0 C -7.14 -3.94 -3.94 -7.14 0 -7.14 C 3.94 -7.14 7.14 -3.94 7.14 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.8889pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\right)\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r\right)^{q/2}\right]\\
&={\mathbb{E}}^{{\mathbf{P}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m)}}\left[\frac{1}{z_{T}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m),{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\varsigma^{i}\left|{\rm D}u_{r}\left(\mu^{\infty}_{r},\xi_{r}^{i}\right)\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r\right)^{q/2}\right]\\
&\leq{\mathbb{E}}^{{\mathbf{P}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m)}}\left[\left(\frac{1}{z_{T}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m),{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}}\right)^{p}\right]^{1/p}{\mathbb{E}}^{{\mathbf{P}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m)}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\varsigma^{i}\left|{\rm D}u_{r}(\mu^{\infty}_{r},\xi_{r}^{i})\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r\right]^{q/2}<\infty,\end{split}

where
p=2/(2-q)
is the conjugate exponent of
2/q
.
Thanks to (
4.66
) and (
4.64
), we deduce from a uniform integrability argument that
for any

m\in O
,

(4.67)

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbb{E}}^{{\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m),{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}}\left[\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\left|\sqrt{2\varsigma^{i}}{\rm D}u_{r}(\mu^{\infty}_{r},\xi_{r}^{i})-\sum_{k=1}^{N(n)}{\mathbf{1}}_{[t_{k-1}^{(n)},t_{k}^{(n)})}(r)H_{k}^{(n),i}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r\right)^{1/2}\right]=0.

Using the expression in (
4.61
), Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality, and (
4.67
), we obtain for any
m\in O

\begin{split}\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}&{\mathbb{E}}^{{\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m),{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|{\mathcal{I}}^{t_{0},(n)}_{t}\circ\hbox to10.72pt{\vbox to10.72pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.3582pt\lower-5.3582pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.14 0 C 7.14 3.94 3.94 7.14 0 7.14 C -3.94 7.14 -7.14 3.94 -7.14 0 C -7.14 -3.94 -3.94 -7.14 0 -7.14 C 3.94 -7.14 7.14 -3.94 7.14 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.8889pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\phantom{\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{t}}\right.\right.\\
&\hskip 15.0pt-\left.\left.\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\sqrt{2\varsigma^{i}}{\rm D}u_{r}(\mu^{\infty}_{r},\xi^{i}_{r})\cdot\,\mathrm{d}\beta^{i}_{r}-\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\varsigma^{i}\int_{0}^{t}{\rm D}u_{r}(\mu^{\infty}_{r},\xi^{i}_{r})\cdot\alpha_{r}^{i}(\mu_{r}^{\infty},\xi^{i}_{r})\,\mathrm{d}r\right|\right]=0.\end{split}

Returning to
(
4.58
), this gives, for
any

m\in O
,

{\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m),{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}
-a.s., for all

t\in[0,T]
,

{\mathcal{I}}^{t_{0}}_{t}\circ\hbox to10.72pt{\vbox to10.72pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.3582pt\lower-5.3582pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.14 0 C 7.14 3.94 3.94 7.14 0 7.14 C -3.94 7.14 -7.14 3.94 -7.14 0 C -7.14 -3.94 -3.94 -7.14 0 -7.14 C 3.94 -7.14 7.14 -3.94 7.14 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\sqrt{2\varsigma^{i}}{\rm D}u_{r}(\mu^{\infty}_{r},\xi^{i}_{r})\cdot\,\mathrm{d}\beta^{i}_{r}+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\varsigma^{i}\int_{0}^{t}{\rm D}u_{r}(\mu^{\infty}_{r},\xi^{i}_{r})\cdot\alpha^{i}_{r}(\mu_{r}^{\infty},\xi^{i}_{r})\,\mathrm{d}r.

\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\circ\hbox to10.72pt{\vbox to10.72pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.3582pt\lower-5.3582pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.14 0 C 7.14 3.94 3.94 7.14 0 7.14 C -3.94 7.14 -7.14 3.94 -7.14 0 C -7.14 -3.94 -3.94 -7.14 0 -7.14 C 3.94 -7.14 7.14 -3.94 7.14 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.8889pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}.\end{split}

This gives, for any

m\in O
,
under

{\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m),{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}
,
for any
t\in[t_{0},T]
,

\begin{split}g\left(\mu^{\infty}_{T}\circ\hbox to10.72pt{\vbox to10.72pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.3582pt\lower-5.3582pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.14 0 C 7.14 3.94 3.94 7.14 0 7.14 C -3.94 7.14 -7.14 3.94 -7.14 0 C -7.14 -3.94 -3.94 -7.14 0 -7.14 C 3.94 -7.14 7.14 -3.94 7.14 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.8889pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\right)&=u_{t}\left(\mu^{\infty}_{t}\circ\hbox to10.72pt{\vbox to10.72pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.3582pt\lower-5.3582pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.14 0 C 7.14 3.94 3.94 7.14 0 7.14 C -3.94 7.14 -7.14 3.94 -7.14 0 C -7.14 -3.94 -3.94 -7.14 0 -7.14 C 3.94 -7.14 7.14 -3.94 7.14 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.8889pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\right)+\int_{t}^{T}f_{r}\bigl(\mu^{\infty}_{r}\circ\hbox to10.72pt{\vbox to10.72pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.3582pt\lower-5.3582pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.14 0 C 7.14 3.94 3.94 7.14 0 7.14 C -3.94 7.14 -7.14 3.94 -7.14 0 C -7.14 -3.94 -3.94 -7.14 0 -7.14 C 3.94 -7.14 7.14 -3.94 7.14 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.8889pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}r+{\mathcal{I}}^{t_{0}}_{T}\circ\hbox to10.72pt{\vbox to10.72pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.3582pt\lower-5.3582pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.14 0 C 7.14 3.94 3.94 7.14 0 7.14 C -3.94 7.14 -7.14 3.94 -7.14 0 C -7.14 -3.94 -3.94 -7.14 0 -7.14 C 3.94 -7.14 7.14 -3.94 7.14 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.8889pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}-{\mathcal{I}}^{t_{0}}_{t}\circ\hbox to10.72pt{\vbox to10.72pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.3582pt\lower-5.3582pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.14 0 C 7.14 3.94 3.94 7.14 0 7.14 C -3.94 7.14 -7.14 3.94 -7.14 0 C -7.14 -3.94 -3.94 -7.14 0 -7.14 C 3.94 -7.14 7.14 -3.94 7.14 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.8889pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}.\end{split}

Using the fact that
\mu^{\infty}\circ\hbox to10.72pt{\vbox to10.72pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.3582pt\lower-5.3582pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.14 0 C 7.14 3.94 3.94 7.14 0 7.14 C -3.94 7.14 -7.14 3.94 -7.14 0 C -7.14 -3.94 -3.94 -7.14 0 -7.14 C 3.94 -7.14 7.14 -3.94 7.14 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.8889pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=\mu^{\infty}
and then inserting the expression of

{\mathcal{I}}^{t_{0}}_{t}\circ\hbox to10.72pt{\vbox to10.72pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.3582pt\lower-5.3582pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.14 0 C 7.14 3.94 3.94 7.14 0 7.14 C -3.94 7.14 -7.14 3.94 -7.14 0 C -7.14 -3.94 -3.94 -7.14 0 -7.14 C 3.94 -7.14 7.14 -3.94 7.14 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.8889pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
, we complete the proof.
∎

4.3.
Finite-dimensional approximation

Let
b:[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a bounded and measurable function and
g\in H
(to ease the presentation, the source term
f
is assumed be null in this subsection, but
the results stated below could be adapted, without any difficulty, to the case when
f
is non-zero). The main result of this section is to provide a suitable finite-dimensional reduction of the PDE (
3.10
) and the related particle system (
4.1
). Unlike what is typically done in the classical mean-field literature, here, it is not possible to directly substitute the (uniform) empirical measure of a finite particle system for the measure
\mu^{\infty}
.
What we do instead is to intervene at the level of the coefficients, introducing approximations of the velocity field
b
and of the terminal condition
g
that depend on a probability measure only through a finite number of (sufficiently massive) atoms.
The goal of this section is to show how this approximation propagates at both the PDE and particle levels, and, in particular,
how it allows us to reduce (
3.10
)
to a PDE set in finite dimension,
and
(
4.1
)
to a system with finitely many particles.

To make it precise, the approximations that are used next rely on the following notion of compatibility between two probability measures.

Definition 4.22
.

For a given
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
, two measures
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
are said to be
\varepsilon
-compatible if they have the same atoms of mass greater than or equal to
\varepsilon
and assign the same mass to these atoms, i.e.,

\mu_{x}=\nu_{x}\text{ for every }x\in\{x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}:\mu_{x}\geq\varepsilon\}=\{x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}:\nu_{x}\geq\varepsilon\}.

This induces an equivalence relation between elements of
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
which we denote by
\mu\approx_{\varepsilon}\nu
.
The
\mathbb{R}^{m}
-valued,
m\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, functions defined
on the quotient space
(and also depending on time and space)
can be identified with
functions belonging to the set

\mathscr{A}_{\varepsilon}^{m}:=\left\{{\mathfrak{u}}:[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}:{\mathfrak{u}}_{t}(\mu,x)={\mathfrak{u}}_{t}(\nu,x)\begin{array}[]{l}\text{for every }\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\,\,\text{s.t. $\mu\approx_{\varepsilon}\nu$}\\
\text{and for every }(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d}\end{array}\right\},

which is an algebra. Elements of

\mathscr{A}_{\varepsilon}^{m}
depend on the measure argument only through atoms of mass greater than or equal to
\varepsilon
. Note that if
\varepsilon<\delta
then
\mathscr{A}_{\delta}^{m}\subseteq\mathscr{A}_{\varepsilon}^{m}
. More generally, we say that a function
v\colon\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
belongs to
\mathscr{A}_{\varepsilon}^{m}
if its trivial extension to
[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}
lies in
\mathscr{A}_{\varepsilon}^{m}
.

We then consider three sequences
(\varepsilon_{n})_{n}
,
(g^{n})_{n}
and
(b^{n})_{n}
such that:

i.

the sequence

(\varepsilon_{n})_{n}
takes values in
(0,1)
, is decreasing and tends to
0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
;

ii.

for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
,

b^{n}\in\mathscr{A}_{\varepsilon_{n}}^{d}
,

\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}}\|b^{n}\|_{\infty}<\infty
,
and
b^{n}\rightarrow b
in the space

L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathscr{L}^{[0,T]}\otimes\overline{{\mathcal{D}}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
as
n\rightarrow\infty
;

iii.

for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
,
g^{n}\in\mathscr{A}_{\varepsilon_{n}}^{1}

and

\|g^{n}\|_{\infty}<\infty
;
and,
g^{n}\rightarrow g
in the space
H

as
n\rightarrow\infty
.

Existence of the three sequences
(\varepsilon_{n})_{n}
,

(g^{n})_{n}
and

(b^{n})_{n}
is guaranteed by Proposition
2.7
and property (
2.9
). However, other constructions are conceivable; we come back to this point next.

For each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
,
we denote by
\mathbf{Q}^{t_{0},(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x}),b^{n}}
the solution to the particle system (
4.1
) (with
i
-independent drift) with data
b^{n},t_{0}
and
(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x})\in T^{\infty}_{o}\times(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{\infty}_{o}
. Since
b^{n}\in\mathscr{A}_{\varepsilon_{n}}^{d}
, we can consider the following finite-dimensional sub-system of (
4.1
), which is given in closed form by

(4.68)

\begin{cases}\,\mathrm{d}X^{i}_{t}&=b_{t}^{n}(\mathrm{em}^{N(\varepsilon_{n},\mathbf{s})}(\mathbf{s},(X^{i}_{t})_{i=1}^{N(\varepsilon_{n},\mathbf{s})}),X^{i}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\frac{2}{s_{i}}}\,\mathrm{d}W^{i}_{t},\quad t\in(t_{0},T),\,i=1,\dots,N(\varepsilon_{n},\mathbf{s}),\\
X^{i}_{t_{0}}&=\iota(x_{i}),\quad i=1,\dots,N(\varepsilon_{n},\mathbf{s}),\end{cases}

where
N(\varepsilon_{n},\mathbf{s})
is as in (
2.32
).
Since the drift

b^{n}\circ\mathrm{em}^{N(\varepsilon_{,}n\mathbf{s})(\mathbf{s},\cdot)}
is bounded, the system (
4.68
) has a unique strong solution,
see

[
61
]
. Of course, this solution is also unique in law. In this context, the purpose of the next statement is to clarify how the finite-dimensional structure of the particle system (
4.68
) is reflected in the PDE (
3.10
) (with
b^{n}
being substituted for
b
). Notice indeed that the latter is still posed in infinite dimension despite the seemingly simpler form of
b^{n}
.

In order to provide a finite-dimensional version of (
3.10
), we first introduce the following functional spaces. For any
N\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, we denote by

{\mathrm{C}}^{0,1,N}
the space of continuous real-valued functions
on
[0,T]\times(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{N}
that are differentiable in space and whose space derivative is jointly continuous in time and space on
[0,T)\times(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{N}
, and for any real
p>1
,
we denote by

W^{1,2,N}_{p}
the space of functions
k:[0,T]\times({\mathbb{T}}^{d})^{N}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
that belong to

L^{p}([0,T]\times({\mathbb{T}}^{d})^{N},\mathscr{L}^{[0,T]}\otimes\textrm{vol}_{d}^{N})

and possess generalized Sobolev derivatives
\partial_{t}k
,

(\partial_{x_{i}^{j}}k)_{i=1,\cdots,N;j=1,\cdots,d}
and

(\partial^{2}_{x_{i}^{j}x_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}}}k)_{i,i^{\prime}=1,\cdots,N;j,j^{\prime}=1,\cdots,d}
in

L^{p}([0,T]\times({\mathbb{T}}^{d})^{N},\mathscr{L}^{[0,T]}\otimes\textrm{vol}_{d}^{N})
, with the convention that an element of

({\mathbb{T}}^{d})^{N}
is represented in the form
(x_{i}^{j})_{i=1,\cdots,N;j=1,\cdots,d}
.

Theorem 4.23
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
b^{n}
and
g^{n}
be as above, and
u^{n}
be the solution of
(
3.10
)
with drift
b^{n}
and final datum
g^{n}
. Then, for every
t_{0}\in[0,T)
and
{\mathcal{D}}
-a.e.
m\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
,

u^{n}_{t_{0}}(m)=h_{t_{0}}^{\mathbf{s}(m)}(x_{1}(m),\dots,x_{N(\varepsilon_{n},\mathbf{s}(m))}(m)),

where, for
\mathbf{s}\in T_{o}^{\infty}
,
h^{\mathbf{s}}
is the unique solution, in the space

{\mathrm{C}}^{0,1,N(\varepsilon_{n},\mathbf{s})}\cap(\cap_{p>1}W^{1,2,N(\varepsilon_{n},\mathbf{s})}_{p}
),
of the PDE, set on
[0,T]\times(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{N(\varepsilon_{n},\mathbf{s})}
:

(4.69)

\partial_{t}h_{t}+\sum_{i=1}^{N(\varepsilon_{n},\mathbf{s})}\frac{1}{s_{i}}\Delta_{i}h_{t}+\sum_{i=1}^{N(\varepsilon_{n},\mathbf{s})}b^{n}_{t}(\mathrm{em}^{N(\varepsilon_{n},\mathbf{s})}(\mathbf{s},\cdot),\cdot_{i})\cdot\nabla_{i}h_{t}=0\quad\text{ for a.e. }t\in(0,T)

with final condition

h_{t}\rvert_{t=T}=g^{n}\circ\mathrm{em}^{N(\varepsilon_{n},\mathbf{s})}(\mathbf{s},\cdot).

Proof.

From Theorem
4.19
, we know that, for every
t_{0}\in[0,T)
and
{\mathcal{D}}
-a.e.
m\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
,

u^{n}_{t_{0}}(m)=\mathbb{E}^{\mathbf{Q}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m),b^{n}}}[g^{n}(\mu^{\infty}_{T})]
. For the rest of the proof, we fix
m
such that the last equality holds true, and for this
m
, we set
(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x}):=(\mathbf{s}(m),\mathbf{x}(m))
and, then,
N:=N(\varepsilon_{n},\mathbf{s})
. Since
g^{n}\in\mathscr{A}_{\varepsilon_{n}}^{1}
, we have that
g^{n}(m)=g^{n}\circ\mathrm{em}^{N}(\mathbf{s},(x_{i})_{i=1}^{N})
. Moreover, the mapping

(y_{1},\dots,y_{N})\in(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{N}\mapsto g^{n}\circ\mathrm{em}^{N}(\mathbf{s},(y_{i})_{i=1}^{N})

belongs to
{\mathrm{C}}^{\infty}((\mathbb{T}^{d})^{N})
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle u_{t_{0}}(m)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{\mathbf{Q}^{t_{0},(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x}),b^{n}}}\left[(g^{n}\circ\mathrm{em}^{N})(\mathbf{s},(\xi^{1}_{T},\dots,\xi^{N}_{T}))\right].

It is plain to see that, under the probability measure

{\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x}),b^{n}},

the process
(\xi^{i})_{i=1}^{N}
is a solution to the particle system (
4.68
) (with
\beta^{1},\cdots,\beta^{N}
as driving Brownian motions).

Consider now the PDE
(
4.69
). Existence and uniqueness of a solution
(within the class specified in the statement of Theorem
4.23
) are standard in the literature (viewing the equation as a PDE posed on
[0,T]\times({\mathbb{R}}^{d})^{N}
, with periodic coefficients);
for convenience,
we refer to
[
22
, Theorem 2.1]
, as the result therein fits exactly our needs.
Since the operator driving the PDE (
4.69
) is the generator of
(
4.68
), we deduce from Krylov’s version of Itô’s formula, see
[
43
, Theorem 2.10.1]
, that the right-hand side in the above display is also equal to

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{\mathbf{Q}^{t_{0},(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x}),b^{n}}}\left[(g^{n}\circ\mathrm{em}^{N})(\mathbf{s},(\xi^{1}_{T},\dots,\xi^{N}_{T}))\right]=h^{\mathbf{s}}_{t_{0}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N}),

which completes the proof.
∎

Remark 4.24
.

It must be stressed that the
finite-dimensional approximation constructed above satisfies the assumption of
Proposition
3.6
.
In particular, with the same notation
as in the statement
of Theorem

4.23
, Proposition

3.6

guarantees that

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|u^{n}_{t}-u_{t}|_{H}\to 0\quad\text{ as }n\to\infty.

Of course, the rate of convergence depends on the rates at which

(b^{n})_{n\geq 1}
and

(g^{n})_{n\geq 1}
converge to
b
and
g
respectively.
When

(b^{n})_{n\geq 1}

and
(g^{n})
are obtained by applying
Proposition
2.7
and property (
2.9
),
the rate is not explicit (as the approximation is constructed by means of a Stone-Weierstrass
argument).
Still, we can wonder whether it is possible to obtain more explicit rates
when
b
and
g
are continuous. We explain in Appendix
B
that,
when
b
and
g
are indeed continuous (the space
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
being equipped with the weak topology), there exists (at least, in
reasonable
situations) a function

\vartheta:{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, with

\lim_{r\downarrow 0}\vartheta(r)=0
, such that

\begin{split}|g^{n}-g|_{H}^{2}&\leq\vartheta(\varepsilon_{n}),\\
\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\left[\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|b_{r}(\mu,x)-b_{r}^{n}(\mu,x)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\right]\,\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{D}}(\mu)\,\mathrm{d}r&\leq\vartheta(\varepsilon_{n}),\end{split}

with the behaviour of the function

\vartheta

being dictated by the shape of a certain modulus of continuity of
b
and
g
.
Inserting these two bounds in (
3.11
), we see that
the term

\mathcal{R(}b,b^{n},{\mathrm{D}}u,T)
therein remains non-explicit: this is due to the presence, inside the integral, of
\lvert{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}(\mu,x)\rvert^{2}
, which is just known to be integrable. Without stronger integrability properties, the best bound we can obtain is:

\begin{split}\mathcal{R(}b,b^{n},{\mathrm{D}}u,T)\leq\inf_{a>0}\left[a^{2}\vartheta(\varepsilon_{n})+4\|b\|_{\infty}^{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}{\mathbf{1}}_{\{|{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}(\mu,x)|>a\}}\lvert{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}(\mu,x)\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\,\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{D}}(\mu)\,\mathrm{d}r\right].\end{split}

The above
inequality is not completely explicit; this explains why we refrain from providing more details on the construction of the function
\vartheta
.
Actually, the fact that we cannot get a better bound should not come as a surprise: since we are working with low regularity properties on the coefficients
b
and
g
, the resulting rate of convergence
remains rather poor and depends on the regularity of
u
through the integrability properties of the gradient.
Obviously, if
{\mathrm{D}}u

were bounded, the rate would be directly given by
\vartheta(\varepsilon_{n})
, but, without any
stronger regularization property of the semi-group of the Dirichlet-Ferguson diffusion, we cannot expect this bound to hold, except if we assume
b
and
g
to be regular in a
sufficiently strong sense. Obviously, the latter assumption would not fit the framework of this article.

That said, the rate becomes explicit when
b=0
and
g
is continuous. It is
given by
\vartheta(\varepsilon_{n})
for the same function
\vartheta
as in the last paragraph. As we explain in Appendix

B
, the rate of growth of the function

\vartheta
is algebraic if the modulus of continuity of
g
(in total variation distance) is also algebraic. In this case, the rate of convergence
can be bounded by

C\varepsilon_{n}^{\alpha}
, for a certain
constant
C>0
and a certain

\alpha>0
. Observing that
N(\varepsilon_{n},\mathbf{s})
is always less than
\lceil 1/\varepsilon_{n}\rceil
, this says that the rate of convergence decays, in this situation, algebraically fast with the number of particles that are used in the approximation. This observation is consistent with the rates that can be obtained by
using the
semi-group (or master equation)

approach in standard mean field systems; see, for example,

[
8
,
9
]
.

5.
The semilinear case

The aim of this section is to apply the tools developed earlier in a nonlinear framework inspired by mean-field control theory. In Subsection
5.1
, we establish a general existence and uniqueness result for a class of semilinear PDEs driven by the operator
{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}
.
When the nonlinear term can be interpreted as a Hamiltonian, i.e., as the Legendre transform of a Lagrangian, we provide an interpretation of the solution to the nonlinear PDE as the value function of a control problem posed on a particle system of the form (
4.1
). The main result in this regard is Theorem
5.7
. In Subsection
5.3
we introduce an approximation of the infinite-dimensional control problem in the same spirit of Subsection
4.3
.

In the whole section we let
T
be the same finite time horizon as at the beginning of Section
2
.
As in Section
4
, we make repeated use of the material introduced in Section
2
and of the results established thus far.

5.1.
Well-posedness of a semilinear PDE

Given a measurable function
\mathscr{H}\colon\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, we define a
new function
{\mathcal{H}}:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times L^{2}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d},\overline{\mathcal{D}};\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
by letting

(5.1)

{\mathcal{H}}(\mu,\gamma):=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathscr{H}(x,\gamma(\mu,x))\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x),

whenever the integral makes sense. For coefficients
\mathscr{F}:[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
and
\mathscr{G}:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
, we then aim at solving the following semilinear PDE:

(5.2)

\partial_{t}u_{t}+\boldsymbol{\Delta}u_{t}-{\mathcal{H}}(\cdot,{\mathrm{D}}u_{t})+\mathscr{F}_{t}=0\quad\text{for a.e.\penalty\ }t\in(0,T),\quad u_{t}\bigr|_{t=T}=\mathscr{G}.

We work under the following assumption.

Assumption 5.1
.

The function
\mathscr{H}\colon\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is jointly measurable and satisfies the following two properties:

a)

it holds

\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\lvert\mathscr{H}(x,0)\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{D}(\mu)<\infty.

b)

The mapping
p\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\mapsto\mathscr{H}(x,p)
is Lipschitz continuous, uniformly with respect to
x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
, i.e., there exists a constant
L>0
, such that, for
any
x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
and
any
p,q\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\lvert\mathscr{H}(x,p)-\mathscr{H}(x,q)\rvert\leq L\lvert p-q\rvert.

Remark 5.2
.

We notice from Assumption
5.1
a) that, for

\mathcal{D}
-a.e.
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
,

(5.3)

\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\lvert\mathscr{H}(x,0)\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)<\infty.

And then, Assumption
5.1
b)
gives, for any
\mu
in the Borel subset of

\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
on which the above inequality holds true, and any

\gamma\in L^{2}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d},\overline{\mathcal{D}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\lvert\mathscr{H}\left(x,\gamma(\mu,x)\right)\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\right)^{1/2}\leq\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\lvert\mathscr{H}\left(x,0\right)\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\right)^{1/2}+L\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\lvert\gamma(\mu,x)\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\right)^{1/2},

so that the left-hand side is automatically finite if, in addition to
(
5.3
),
it holds

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\lvert\gamma(\mu,x)\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)<\infty
.
Obviously, the latter is true
for
\mathcal{D}
-a.e.
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
.
We deduce that
{\mathcal{H}}(\mu,\gamma)
is well defined for
\mathcal{D}
-a.e.
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and
induces a mapping

{\mathcal{H}}(\cdot,\gamma):\mu\mapsto{\mathcal{H}}(\mu,\gamma)
that can be regarded as an element of
H
. Moreover, for any

\gamma^{1},\gamma^{2}\in L^{2}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d},\overline{\mathcal{D}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
for any
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
such that
(
5.3
) holds true and

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}(\lvert\gamma^{1}(\mu,x)\rvert^{2}+\lvert\gamma^{2}(\mu,x)\rvert^{2})\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)
is finite (and thus for

{\mathcal{D}}
-a.e
\mu
),
we get, from Assumption

5.1
b),

(5.4)

\begin{split}\lvert{\mathcal{H}}(\mu,\gamma^{1})-{\mathcal{H}}(\mu,\gamma^{2})\rvert&\leq\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\lvert\mathscr{H}(x,\gamma^{1}(\mu,x))-\mathscr{H}(x,\gamma^{2}(\mu,x))\rvert\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\\
&\leq L\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\lvert\gamma^{1}(\mu,x)-\gamma^{2}(\mu,x)\rvert\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x).\end{split}

Proposition 5.3
.

Let Assumption
5.1
be in force,
\mathscr{F}\in L^{2}([0,T];H)
, and
\mathscr{G}\in H
. Then there exists a unique
u\in{\mathrm{C}}([0,T];H)\cap\mathrm{AC}_{loc}((0,T);H)\cap L^{2}([0,T];H^{1,2})
such that
u_{t}\in D(\boldsymbol{\Delta})
for a.e.
t\in(0,T)
and

\partial_{t}u_{t}+\boldsymbol{\Delta}u_{t}-{\mathcal{H}}(\cdot,{\mathrm{D}}u_{t})+\mathscr{F}_{t}=0\quad\text{for a.e.\penalty\ }t\in(0,T),\quad u_{t}\bigr|_{t=T}=\mathscr{G}.

Proof.

Thanks to the Lipschitz regularity of
\mathscr{H}
, the proof proceeds along the same lines as in Theorem
3.4
, which notation we adopt here.
We briefly outline the reasoning, emphasizing the key differences. As a preliminary remark, note that for
v\in X_{\bar{T}}
, the function

t\mapsto{\mathcal{H}}(\cdot,Dv_{t})
belongs to
X_{\bar{T}}
. Indeed, by Assumption
5.1
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\bar{T}}\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\lvert{\mathcal{H}}(\mu,{\mathrm{D}}v_{t})\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{D}(\mu)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\bar{T}}\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\left\lvert\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathscr{H}(x,{\mathrm{D}}v_{t}(\mu,x))\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\right\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{D}(\mu)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq 2L^{2}\int_{0}^{\bar{T}}\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\lvert{\mathrm{D}}v_{t}(\mu,x)\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{D}(\mu)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\quad+2\int_{0}^{\bar{T}}\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\lvert\mathscr{H}(x,0)\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{D}(\mu)\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty.

The core of the argument is to show that, for a suitably small
\bar{T}>0
, for every
w_{0}\in H
,
the operator
\Phi_{\bar{T},w_{0}}\colon X_{\bar{T}}\to X_{\bar{T}}
that maps every
v\in X_{\bar{T}}
to the unique solution of

(5.5)

\partial_{t}u^{v}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\Delta}u^{v}_{t}=-{\mathcal{H}}(\cdot,{\mathrm{D}}v_{t})+\mathscr{F}_{t}\quad\text{for a.e.\penalty\ }t\in(0,\bar{T}),\quad u^{v}_{t}\bigr|_{t=0}=w_{0}\in H,

is a contraction. As in the proof of Theorem
3.4
, we can focus on the forward-in-time equation and then recover the result for the backward-in-time version via a simple time-reversal argument.

Claim 1
:
For any
\bar{T}\in(0,T)
,
w_{0}\in H
and
v\in X_{\bar{T}}
there exists a unique
u^{v}\in X_{\bar{T}}
solving (
5.5
). This defines a map
\Phi_{\bar{T},w_{0}}:X_{\bar{T}}\to X_{\bar{T}}
associating to every
v\in X_{\bar{T}}
the function
u^{v}
as above.

Proof of claim 1:
This follows directly from Proposition
3.2
, with
f
given by
t\mapsto f_{t}:=-{\mathcal{H}}(\cdot,{\mathrm{D}}v_{t})+\mathscr{F}_{t}
, noting that the latter belongs to to
L^{2}([0,\bar{T}];H)

Claim 2
: There exists
\bar{T}\in(0,T)
, only depending on the Lipschitz constant
L
of
\mathscr{H}
, such that, for any
w_{0}\in H
, the map
\Phi_{\bar{T},w_{0}}
defined in claim 1 is a contraction on
X_{\bar{T}}
.

Proof of claim 2:
For
w_{0}\in H
and
v,\tilde{v}\in X_{\bar{T}}
, set
u:=\Phi_{\bar{T},w_{0}}(v)
,
\tilde{u}:=\Phi_{\bar{T},w_{0}}(\tilde{v})
. The argument used in the proof of Theorem
3.4
can be repeated directly. The only new point concerns the treatment of the difference between the two terms driven by
{\mathcal{H}}
. Here, we handle the difference by means of
(
5.4
):

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\lvert{\mathcal{H}}(\mu,{\mathrm{D}}v_{r})-{\mathcal{H}}(\mu,{\mathrm{D}}\tilde{v}_{r})\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{D}(\mu)\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\qquad\leq L^{2}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\lvert{\mathrm{D}}v_{r}(\mu,x)-{\mathrm{D}}\tilde{v}_{r}(\mu,x)\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{D}(\mu)\,\mathrm{d}r\leq L^{2}\left\|v-\tilde{v}\right\|^{2}_{X_{\bar{T}}}.

Conclusion
: The end of the proof is similar to that of Theorem
3.4
.
The main point is to note that
\bar{T}
depends only on the Lipschitz constant
L
of
{\mathcal{H}}
and, in particular, is independent of the initial condition
w_{0}\in H
in (
5.5
).
The argument of Claim 2 can then be iterated over successive subintervals of
[0,T]
of length smaller than
\bar{T}
, as in the proof of Theorem
3.4
.
∎

Remark 5.4
.

The form of
{\mathcal{H}}
imposed by relation (
5.1
), with

\mathscr{H}
satisfying Assumption
5.1
, is motivated by the application to optimal control given below.
Nevertheless, the result of Proposition
5.3
still holds when
{\mathcal{H}}
is a more general measurable function from

\mathcal{P}_{o}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times L^{2}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d},\overline{\mathcal{D}};\mathbb{R}^{d})

into
\mathbb{R}
(recall that

\mathcal{P}_{o}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}

has full measure under

\overline{\mathcal{D}}
;
therefore, restricting the first factor in the space

\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times L^{2}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d},\overline{\mathcal{D}};\mathbb{R}^{d})

to
\mathcal{P}_{o}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
is consistent with our choice of equipping

\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}
with the measure
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
)
satisfying the following two conditions:

\text{a}^{\star}
)

it holds

\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\lvert{\mathcal{H}}(\mu,0)\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{D}(\mu)<\infty;

\text{b}^{\star}
)

there exists a constant
L>0
such that for any
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{o}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
,
\gamma^{1},\gamma^{2}\in L^{2}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d},\overline{\mathcal{D}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

\lvert{\mathcal{H}}(\mu,\gamma^{1})-{\mathcal{H}}(\mu,\gamma^{2})\rvert\leq L\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\lvert\gamma^{1}(\mu,x)-\gamma^{2}(\mu,x)\rvert\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x).

5.2.
Infinite-dimensional control problem

We now use the results of the previous subsection in order to characterize the value function of a stochastic control problem set over a controlled version of the massive particle system.

For a compact and convex subset
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
0\in A
,
we define the
space of admissible controls (in Markov feedback form), denoted by
{\mathcal{A}}
, as the
space of sequences
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha^{i})_{i}
,
where each
\alpha^{i}\colon[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}\to A\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a measurable function.
Throughout, we work within the framework of Subsection
4.1.2
. Given an initial time
t_{0}\in[0,T)
and an initial distribution
\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}\in\mathcal{P}(T_{o}^{\infty}\times(\mathbb{T}^{d})_{o}^{\infty})
, we denote by
\mathbf{Q}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}},\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
the law of
((\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x}),(X^{i})_{i},(W^{i})_{i})
on the canonical space
\Xi
, where
({\Omega},{{\mathcal{F}}},{\mathbb{Q}},\{{{\mathcal{F}}}_{t}\}_{t\in[t_{0},T]},(({\mathbf{s}},{\mathbf{x}}),({X}^{i})_{i},({W}^{i})_{i}))
is a (unique in law) weak solution (according to the definition given in Subsection
4.1
) to the system

(5.6)

\begin{cases}\,\mathrm{d}X^{i}_{t}&=\alpha^{i}_{t}(\mu^{\infty}_{t},X_{t}^{i})\,\mathrm{d}t+{\sqrt{\frac{2}{s_{i}}}}\,\mathrm{d}W^{i}_{t},\quad t\in[t_{0},T],\quad i\in\mathbb{N}_{+},\\
X^{i}_{t_{0}}&=\iota(x_{i}).\end{cases}

Since the controls
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha^{i})_{i}
are assumed to be uniformly bounded (as the set
A
itself is bounded),
the system (
5.6
) can be regarded as a system of
the same type as
(
4.1
), and the approach developed in
Subsection
4.1

applies. In particular,
Propositions
4.1
and

4.2
guarantee existence and uniqueness in law of weak solutions.
In this approach, the index
i
should be viewed as the label of a rational agent; accordingly, the massive particle system becomes, in this subsection, a countable system of agents (or players).
The control (or feedback function) played by the agent
i
is allowed to depend on
i
, on the collective state of the system (here encoded through
\mu^{\infty}
), and on the private state of the agent (encoded through
X_{t}^{i}
). Whilst this form looks rather restrictive, it covers in fact a wide class of feedback functions. Indeed, in contrast with standard mean-field models, which are symmetric, the current model is not symmetric: for instance, the first player is, by construction, the most massive one (i.e.,
s_{1}
is greater than all the other masses
(s_{i})_{i\geq 2}
); more generally, the
measure
\mu^{\infty}_{t}
provides a complete description of the masses and the atom locations of the agents, in the sense that the mass and location of the agent number
i
can be deduced from the observation of
\mu^{\infty}_{t}
. This follows from the fact that the masses are ordered and (almost-surely) pairwise distinct and the locations are also (almost-surely) pairwise distinct. Mathematically, we can retrieve the masses via the map

\mathbf{s}
and the atom locations via the map
\mathbf{x}
as in (
2.5
): observing
\mu_{t}^{\infty}
, we can observe both

\mathbf{s}(\mu^{\infty}_{t})
and

\mathbf{x}(\mu^{\infty}_{t})
. As such, any (measurable) function of the masses and the atom locations can be rewritten as a (measurable) function of
\mu^{\infty}_{t}
. In particular, with the given form of feedback functions, we allow each agent to play a control depending on the state of the
whole
system. By the way, notice that, in the notation

\alpha^{i}_{t}(\mu_{t}^{\infty},X_{t}^{i})
, the variable
X_{t}^{i}
is somewhat redundant: as we just explained,

X_{t}^{i}
can be observed from
the observation of
\mu^{\infty}_{t}
. That said, we feel more consistent with the usual mean-field control theory to stick to the notation
\alpha^{i}_{t}(\mu_{t}^{\infty},X_{t}^{i})
.

We associate a cost with
\mathbf{Q}^{t_{0},m,\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
; to do so, we consider measurable coefficients

\mathscr{L}\colon\mathbb{T}^{d}\times A\to\mathbb{R}
,
\mathscr{F}:[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
, and
\mathscr{G}\colon\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
, with

\mathscr{L}
being understood as the Lagrangian of the control problem, and

{\mathscr{F}}
and
{\mathscr{G}}
as (respectively) running and terminal interaction potentials. For
an initial time
t_{0}\in[0,T)
, an initial distribution
\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}\in\mathcal{P}(T_{o}^{\infty}\times(\mathbb{T}^{d})_{o}^{\infty})
, and
an admissible control

{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in{\mathcal{A}}
, the cost associated to
(t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}},{\boldsymbol{\alpha}})
is defined as

J(t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}},\boldsymbol{\alpha}):=\mathbb{E}^{\mathbf{Q}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}},\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\left[\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\left(\mathscr{F}_{r}(\mu_{r}^{\infty})+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\varsigma_{i}\mathscr{L}(\xi^{i}_{r},\alpha^{i}_{r}(\mu^{\infty}_{r},\xi^{i}_{r}))\right)\,\mathrm{d}r+\mathscr{G}(\mu^{\infty}_{T})\right].

Here we are using the same convention as in Remark
2.15
to evaluate
\mathscr{L}
and
\alpha^{i}_{r}
at
\xi_{r}^{i}
.
Conditions are given below under which the right-hand side is well-defined for any

{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in{\mathcal{A}}
. Of course, the purpose is to minimize

J(t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}},{\boldsymbol{\alpha}})
with respect to
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in{\mathcal{A}}
.
The corresponding value function is defined as

(5.7)

V(t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}):=\inf_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{A}}}J(t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}},\boldsymbol{\alpha}),\quad(t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}})\in[0,T)\times\mathcal{P}(T_{o}^{\infty}\times(\mathbb{T}^{d})_{o}^{\infty}).

In analogy with the shorthand notation
\mathbf{Q}^{t_{0},(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x})}

introduced in (
4.12
) as a substitute for the notation

\mathbf{Q}^{t_{0},\delta_{(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x})}}
, we write
V(t_{0},(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x}))
in place of
V(t_{0},\delta_{(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x})})
when the particle system is initialized with the deterministic initial condition
(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x})
.

We study the control problem under the following assumption.

Assumption 5.5
.

The functions
\mathscr{L}\colon\mathbb{T}^{d}\times A\to\mathbb{R}
,
\mathscr{F}:[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
, and
\mathscr{G}\colon\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
satisfy:

a)

for every
x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
, the function

A\ni a\mapsto\mathscr{L}(x,a)
is strictly convex and lower semicontinuous;

b)

there exists
C\geq 0
such that, for every
x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
,

|\inf_{a}\mathscr{L}(x,a)|\leq C
;

c)

\mathscr{F}\in L^{2}([0,T];H)
and
\mathscr{G}\in H
.

In the following, we often assume that Assumption
5.5
is in force.

In order to connect the control problem introduced above with
Subsection
5.1
, we associate to the Lagrangian
\mathscr{L}
the
Hamiltonian

(5.8)

\mathscr{H}(x,p):=\sup_{a\in A}\left\{-\mathscr{L}(x,a)-p\cdot a\right\},\qquad(x,p)\in\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},

which will play, in what follows, the same role as

\mathscr{H}
in
Subsection
5.1
.

By construction,
\mathscr{H}
is convex
[
54
, Theorem 12.2]
and Lipschitz in
p
uniformly with respect to
x
. Moreover,

|\mathscr{H}(x,0)|=|\inf_{a\in A}\mathscr{L}(x,a)|\leq C
, for any
x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}.

In particular,
\mathscr{H}
satisfies Assumption
5.1
with a Lipschitz constant
L
depending only on
A
. Moreover, since
\mathscr{L}
is strictly convex, then
\mathbb{R}^{d}\ni p\mapsto\mathscr{H}(x,p)
is everywhere differentiable for any
x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}

[
54
, Theorem 26.3]
, with derivative bounded again by a constant depending solely on
A
. By convexity and lower semicontinuity in
a
of
\mathscr{L}
, it holds
\mathscr{L}(x,a)=\sup_{p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\{-\mathscr{H}(x,p)-a\cdot p\}
for any
(x,a)\in\mathbb{T}^{d}\times A

[
54
, Corollary 12.2.1]
.

Proposition 5.6
.

Let Assumption
5.5
be in force and
t_{0}\in[0,T)
be an initial time. If
u
is the unique solution of (
5.2
), then there exists a Borel subset
O
of
{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, with
{\mathcal{D}}(O)=1
, such that,
for any
m\in O
and any
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\mathcal{A}

\displaystyle\mathscr{G}(\mu^{\infty}_{T})

\displaystyle=u_{t}(\mu^{\infty}_{t})-\int_{t}^{T}\mathscr{F}_{r}(\mu^{\infty}_{r})\,\mathrm{d}r+\int_{t}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathscr{H}(x,{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}(\mu_{r}^{\infty},x))\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\infty}_{r}(x)\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{t}^{T}\varsigma_{i}{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}(\mu_{r}^{\infty},\xi_{r}^{i})\cdot\alpha_{r}^{i}(\mu_{r}^{\infty},\xi_{r}^{i})\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int^{T}_{t}\sqrt{2\varsigma_{i}}{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}(\mu^{\infty}_{r},\xi^{i}_{r})\cdot\,\mathrm{d}\beta^{i}_{r},\quad t\in[t_{0},T],\quad{\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m),\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\text{-a.s.}

Note that in the above display we are using the convention explained in Remark
2.15
to evaluate
{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}(\mu_{r}^{\infty},\cdot)
and
\alpha_{r}^{i}(\mu_{r}^{\infty},\cdot)
at
\xi_{r}^{i}
.

Proof.

The proof is a consequence of Proposition
4.21
,
replacing
t\in[0,T]\mapsto f_{t}

by

t\in[0,T]\mapsto{\mathcal{H}}(\cdot,{\mathrm{D}}u_{t})-{\mathscr{F}_{t}}
, which is indeed in
L^{2}([0,T];H)
(see the proof of Proposition
5.3
).
∎

We now have all the ingredients to state and prove a verification theorem for our mean-field control problem.

Theorem 5.7
.

Let Assumption
5.5
be in force and
u
be the unique solution of (
5.2
); then for every
t_{0}\in[0,T)
there exists a Borel subset
\tilde{O}_{t_{0}}
of
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, with
{\mathcal{D}}(\tilde{O}_{t_{0}})=1
, such that

(5.9)

u_{t_{0}}(m)=V(t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m))\quad\text{ for every }m\in\tilde{O}_{t_{0}}.

Moreover, if
t_{0}\in[0,T)
and
m\in\tilde{O}_{t_{0}}
, then the control

\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=(\hat{\alpha}^{i})_{i}
, with the

\hat{\alpha}^{i}
’s being all equal to
the same mapping
\hat{\alpha}:[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}\ni(t,\mu,x)\mapsto\hat{\alpha}_{t}(\mu,x):=-\nabla_{p}\mathscr{H}(x,{\mathrm{D}}u_{t}(\mu,x))
, is optimal for
V(t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m))
and it is unique in the following sense: if
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in\mathcal{A}
is also optimal for
V(t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m))
, then,
for any
i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
, for a.e.
r\in(t_{0},T)
,

{\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m),\tilde{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\left(\left\{\tilde{\alpha}_{r}^{i}(\mu_{r}^{\infty},\xi_{r}^{i})=\hat{\alpha}_{r}(\mu_{r}^{\infty},\xi_{r}^{i})\right\}\right)=1.

Remark 5.8
.

The following remarks are in order, regarding the shape of the optimal feedback function.

i)

It is worth mentioning that the definition of the function

\hat{\alpha}
depends in fact on the choice of a version of the mapping
(t,\mu,x)\mapsto{\mathrm{D}}u_{t}(\mu,x)
, which is viewed as an element of
L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d},{\mathscr{L}}^{[0,T]}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{D}})
.
Proposition

4.10

says that the law of the particle system would
remain the same (at least for

{\mathcal{D}}
-almost every
initial
condition
m\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
) if we used another version of this mapping.

ii)

We also notice that the resulting form of the optimal feedback function
\hat{\alpha}
is independent of
the label of the player: at the optimum, all the agents play the same feedback function (but implemented at a different location, as it depends on the own state of the agent). This observation is consistent with the theory of mean-field control.

Consequently, the control problem (
5.7
) can be equivalently formulated by considering controls that are identical across particles. Therefore,
for any

t_{0}\in[0,T]
, and for

{\mathcal{D}}
-a.e.
m\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
,

V(t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m))=\inf_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{A}}}J(t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m),\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\inf_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{u}}J(t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m),\boldsymbol{\alpha}),

where
{\mathcal{A}}_{u}
denotes the set of controls
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha^{i})_{i}
with the
\alpha^{i}
being all equal to a single measurable function
\alpha\colon[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}\to A
. Notice that, for such an
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{u}
, the cost functional can be written as

J(t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}},\alpha)=\mathbb{E}^{\mathbf{Q}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}},\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\left[\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\left(\mathscr{F}_{r}(\mu_{r}^{\infty})+\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathscr{L}(x,\alpha_{r}(\mu^{\infty}_{r},x))\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{r}^{\infty}(x)\right)\,\mathrm{d}r+\mathscr{G}(\mu^{\infty}_{T})\right].

iii)

We strongly believe that the proof of Theorem

5.7

that is achieved below
could be adapted, at least up to some extent,
to accommodate open-loop controls.
The result would be as follows. For an initial time
t_{0}\in[0,T)
, a filtered probability space

(\Omega,{\mathcal{F}},{\mathbb{P}},\{{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]})
, equipped with a collection
(B^{i})_{i}

of independent
d
-dimensional
\{{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\}_{t\in[t_{0},T]}
-Brownian motions,
for any collection
(\alpha^{i})_{i}=((\alpha^{i}_{r})_{r\in[t_{0},T]})_{i}
of
A
-valued
\{{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\}_{t\in[t_{0},T]}
-progressively measurable processes,
for any

{\mathcal{F}}_{t_{0}}
-measurable
random initial distributions
(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x})
with values in
T_{o}^{\infty}\times(\mathbb{T}^{d})_{o}^{\infty}
such that

\mu_{t_{0}}^{\infty}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}s_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}\sim{\mathcal{D}},

the conditional cost

(5.10)

\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}\left[\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\left(\mathscr{F}_{r}\left(\mu_{r}^{\infty}\right)+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\varsigma_{i}\mathscr{L}(X^{i}_{r},\alpha^{i}_{r})\right)\,\mathrm{d}r+\mathscr{G}\left(\mu_{T}^{\infty}\right)\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{t_{0}}\right]

would be higher than
u_{t_{0}}(\mu_{t_{0}}^{\infty})
with probability 1 under
{\mathbb{P}}
,
where

\mu_{r}^{\infty}:=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}s_{i}\delta_{X_{r}^{i}},\quad r\in[t_{0},T],

and

X_{t}^{i}=\iota(x_{i})+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\alpha_{r}^{i}\,\mathrm{d}r+\sqrt{\frac{2}{s_{i}}}\left(B_{t}^{i}-B_{t_{0}}^{i}\right),\quad t\in[t_{0},T],

with
\iota:\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
the canonical projection and we are using the same convention as in (
2.28
).
Equivalently, our guess is that

(5.11)

\underset{(\alpha^{i})_{i}}{\rm essinf}\ \mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}\left[\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\left(\mathscr{F}_{r}\left(\mu_{r}^{\infty}\right)+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\varsigma_{i}\mathscr{L}(X^{i}_{r},\alpha^{i}_{r})\right)\,\mathrm{d}r+\mathscr{G}\left(\mu_{T}^{\infty}\right)\,|\,{\mathcal{F}}_{t_{0}}\right]\geq u_{t_{0}}(\mu_{t_{0}}).

Conditioning on
{\mathcal{F}}_{t_{0}}

is here a way to fix the initial value

\mu_{t_{0}}^{\infty}
. As

\mu_{t_{0}}^{\infty}
is
assumed to follow the distribution
{\mathcal{D}}
, this is consistent with the fact that
the result of
Theorem

5.7
is stated for
m
in a
{\mathcal{D}}
-full subset of
{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. That said, Theorem

5.7
is stronger. In comparison, it can be summarized in the following way: for

{\mathcal{D}}
-a.e.
m\in{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
,

\inf_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}J(t_{0},m,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}})\geq u_{t_{0}}(m),

with equality when

{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=(\hat{\alpha})_{i}
; implicitly, the
{\mathcal{D}}
-full subset
of
{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
on which the inequality is true is independent of
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
, which explains why the essential infimum can be replaced by an infimum.

The proof of
(
5.11
)
would rely on the same principle as the first step in the proof of
Theorem

5.7
below, this step itself being a consequence of the version of Itô’s formula stated in Proposition
5.6
. In fact, one of the difficulties in the proof of Itô’s formula is to establish that the subset
O
(on which the expansion holds true) is independent of
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
, but in order to derive
(
5.11
),
a simpler version would be in fact enough: it would suffice to get the
Itô expansion under
{\mathbb{P}}
, for a fixed
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
(recalling that

\mu_{t_{0}}^{\infty}\sim_{\mathbb{P}}{\mathcal{D}}
), and then to take conditional expectation given
{\mathcal{F}}_{t_{0}}
. For this reason, the proof would be easier. To get the expansion for a fixed
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
, one could come back to the proof of Proposition
4.18
and then expand

(u^{n}_{t}(\mu_{t}^{\infty}))_{t\in[t_{0},T]}
under the tilted probability measure

{\mathbb{Q}}
, given by

\frac{\,\mathrm{d}{\mathbb{Q}}}{\,\mathrm{d}{\mathbb{P}}}:=\exp\left(-\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\sqrt{\frac{s_{i}}{2}}\alpha_{r}^{i}\cdot\,\mathrm{d}B_{r}^{i}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\frac{s_{i}}{2}|\alpha_{r}^{i}|^{2}\right).

Details are left to the reader.

We come back to the notion of open-loop controls in the framework of the finite-dimensional approximation addressed in Subsection
5.3.2
.

iv)

The case where the feedback functions are just required to depend on
t
and
\mu_{t}^{\infty}

is also very interesting
from a practical viewpoint.
This
corresponds to
the framework used in the mean-field approach
of residual networks with a continuum of layers, see for instance
[
31
]
.
While
entering the details of this model would be out of the scope
of this article, it is worth mentioning that the related HJB equation has the form

\begin{split}&\partial_{t}u_{t}(\mu)+\sup_{\nu}\left[\int_{A}\left[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}b(x,a)\cdot{\rm D}u_{t}(\mu,x,y)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x,y)\right]\,\mathrm{d}\nu(a)+{\mathscr{K}}(\nu)\right]=0,\quad t\in(0,T),\\
&u_{t}\bigr|_{t=T}(\mu)=\mathscr{G}(\mu),\end{split}

for
(t,\mu)\in[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, where
b

is a generic activation function
taking as inputs the current state
x
of an
n
-dimensional feature and the current state
a
of a neuron (in a set
A
),
{\mathscr{K}}
is a possible penalization function, and

\mathscr{G}
is a loss function, measuring the
distance between the outputs of the network and the labels.
The variable
\mu
stands for the (initial) joint distribution of the features (represented by the variable
x
) and the labels (represented by the variable
y
), so that the dimension
d
is greater than
n
; the variable
\nu
represents
an instantaneous
distribution of the neurons and is thus viewed as an element of
\mathcal{P}(A)
. We refer to
[
18
]
for a complete description of this model.

When the state space is the torus and a Laplacian is added, this equation is covered by Remark
5.4
(provided that the coefficients satisfy mild conditions). The fact that our results are stated without any convexity assumption is especially adapted to this situation.

Proof of Theorem

5.7
.

The proof is divided into two steps: the first establishes the upper bound
in (
5.9
), and the second establishes the lower bound.

First step: proving the upper bound

u_{t_{0}}(m)\leq V(t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m))
.

Back to the main expansion in the statement of Proposition
5.6
, we first establish that the local martingale therein is in fact a true martingale (up to a possibly new choice of the set
O=O_{t_{0}}
in the statement). To do so, we come back to the proof of Theorem

4.19
, and especially to (
4.47
).
We note that the bound (
4.49
) is, in the present context, independent of
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha^{i})_{i}
since each

\alpha^{i}
is required to take values in the bounded set
A
. Moreover, the same bound holds not only when
\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}=\mathrm{em}_{\sharp}^{-1}(\mathcal{D})
but also when
\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}=\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m)
, for any
m\in\mathcal{P}_{o}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
.
Then, we can repeat the computations carried out in the first step of the proof of Theorem
4.19
, especially those achieved to handle (
4.50
)
and (
4.51
), and deduce that there exists a constant
C
such that, for any
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in{\mathcal{A}}

and any

m\in{\mathcal{P}}_{o}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
,

\mathbb{E}^{\mathbf{Q}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m),\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\left[\sup_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\sqrt{2\varsigma_{i}}{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}(\mu_{r}^{\infty},\xi_{r}^{i})\cdot\,\mathrm{d}\beta_{r}^{i}\right|\right]\leq C\mathbb{E}^{\mathbf{P}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m)}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\varsigma_{i}|{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}(\mu_{r}^{\infty},\xi_{r}^{i})|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r\right].

We already know from (
4.48
) that the right-hand side above is finite when we substitute
\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m)
for
\mathrm{em}_{\sharp}^{-1}(\mathcal{D})
.
In particular, we can find a subset

O^{\prime}_{{t_{0}}}
, of full measure under
{\mathcal{D}}
, such that, for any

{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in{\mathcal{A}}

and any

m\in O^{\prime}
, the left-hand side is finite. This shows in particular that,
for any

{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in{\mathcal{A}}
and
any
m\in O^{\prime}_{t_{0}}
,
the process

\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\sqrt{2\varsigma_{i}}{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}(\mu_{r}^{\infty},\xi_{r}^{i})\cdot\,\mathrm{d}\beta_{r}^{i}\right)_{t\in[t_{0},T]}

is
{\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m),\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
-martingale.
This says that, for any

{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in{\mathcal{A}}

and any
m\in\tilde{O}_{t_{0}}:=O_{t_{0}}\cap O^{\prime}_{t_{0}}
,
the stochastic integral in the
statement of Proposition

5.6

has zero expectation.
We obtain, for any

{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in{\mathcal{A}}
and any

m\in\tilde{O}_{t_{0}}
,

(5.12)

\begin{split}&{\mathbb{E}}^{{\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m),\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\left[{\mathscr{G}}(\mu_{T}^{\infty})+\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\left({\mathscr{F}}_{r}(\mu_{r}^{\infty})+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\varsigma_{i}{\mathscr{L}}(\xi_{r}^{i},\alpha_{r}^{i}(\mu_{r}^{\infty},\xi_{r}^{i}))\right)\,\mathrm{d}r\right]\\
&=u_{t_{0}}(m)+{\mathbb{E}}^{{\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m),\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\varsigma_{i}\left[\mathscr{H}(\xi_{r}^{i},{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}(\mu_{r}^{\infty},\xi_{r}^{i}))\right.\\
&\hskip 150.0pt\left.+{\mathscr{L}}(\xi_{r}^{i},\alpha_{r}^{i}(\mu_{r}^{\infty},\xi_{r}^{i}))+{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}(\mu_{r}^{\infty},\xi_{r}^{i})\cdot\alpha_{r}^{i}(\mu_{r}^{\infty},\xi_{r}^{i})\right]\,\mathrm{d}r.\end{split}

And then, recalling (
5.8
), we deduce that

J(t_{0},m,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}})\geq u_{t_{0}}(m)
.
Since

{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
is arbitrary,
this implies

V(t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m))\geq u_{t_{0}}(m).

We now study the case when

J(t_{0},m,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}})\geq u_{t_{0}}(m)

is in fact an equality, i.e.,

J(t_{0},m,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}})=u_{t_{0}}(m)
.
Recalling that

\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m)
belongs to
T^{\infty}_{o}\times({\mathbb{T}}^{d})^{\infty}_{o}
(since

m\in{\mathcal{P}}_{o}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
), we observe that all the weights
(\varsigma_{i})_{i}

are (strictly) positive. Back to
(
5.12
), this implies that, for any
i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
, for a.e.

r\in[t_{0},T]
,

{\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m),\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\left(\left\{\mathscr{H}(\xi_{r}^{i},{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}(\mu_{r}^{\infty},\xi_{r}^{i}))+{\mathscr{L}}(\xi_{r}^{i},\alpha_{r}^{i}(\mu_{r}^{\infty},\xi_{r}^{i}))+{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}(\mu_{r}^{\infty},\xi_{r}^{i})\cdot\alpha_{r}^{i}(\mu_{r}^{\infty},\xi_{r}^{i})=0\right\}\right)=1.

Since, for any
x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
,
{\mathscr{L}}(x,\cdot)
is strictly convex in
a\in A
, this implies, for any
i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
, for a.e.
r\in[t_{0},T]
,

{\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m),\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\left(\left\{\alpha_{r}^{i}(\mu_{r}^{\infty},\xi_{r}^{i})=\hat{\alpha}_{r}(\mu_{r}^{\infty},\xi_{r}^{i})\right\}\right)=1.

This proves that
there is at most one control
whose cost
is equal to
u_{t_{0}}(m)
.

Second step: proving the lower bound

u_{t_{0}}(m)\leq V(t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m))
.

In order to complete the proof, it suffices to show that
the control

\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}:=(\hat{\alpha})_{i}
(all the entries are the same), which obviously belongs to

{\mathcal{A}}
, satisfies

u_{t_{0}}(m)=J(t_{0},m,\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}})
, for

m
in a full subset of
{\mathcal{D}}
.
This is a consequence
of (
5.12
), noticing that the right-hand side therein then reduces to
u_{t_{0}}(m).

∎

5.3.
Finite-dimensional approximation

5.3.1.
The approximate PDE

In this subsection we construct a finite dimensional approximation of the PDE (
5.2
). By the density in (
2.9
) we can find sequences
(\varepsilon_{n})_{n},(\mathscr{F}^{n})_{n},(\mathscr{G}^{n})_{n}
such that
\varepsilon_{n}\in(0,1)
,
\mathscr{F}^{n}_{t}\in\hat{\mathfrak{Z}}^{\infty}_{\varepsilon_{n}}
for every
t\in[0,T]
,
\mathscr{G}^{n}\in\hat{\mathfrak{Z}}_{\varepsilon_{n}}^{\infty}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, and
\mathscr{F}^{n}\to\mathscr{F}
in
L^{2}([0,T];H)
,
\mathscr{G}^{n}\to\mathscr{G}
in
H
, and
\varepsilon_{n}\downarrow 0
as
n\to\infty
. Starting from
\mathscr{H}
as in (
5.8
), for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we define a function
{\mathcal{H}}^{n}:\mathcal{P}_{o}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
as

{\mathcal{H}}^{n}(\mu,p):=\sum_{j=1}^{N(\varepsilon_{n},\mathbf{s})}s_{j}\mathscr{H}(x_{j},p),\quad\mu=\mathrm{em}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x})\in\mathcal{P}_{o}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),\,p\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

where
N(\varepsilon_{n},\mathbf{s})
is as in (
2.32
).
With a small abuse of notation, we still denote by
{\mathcal{H}}^{n}
the map over
\mathcal{P}_{o}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times L^{2}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d},\overline{\mathcal{D}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
defined by

(5.13)

{\mathcal{H}}^{n}(\mu,\gamma):=\sum_{j=1}^{N(\varepsilon_{n},\mathbf{s})}s_{j}\mathscr{H}(x_{j},\gamma(\mu,x_{j})),\quad\mu=\mathrm{em}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x})\in\mathcal{P}_{o}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),\,\gamma\in L^{2}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d},\overline{\mathcal{D}};\mathbb{R}^{d}).

A first object we are interested in is the Hamilton-Jacobi equation associated with
{\mathcal{H}}^{n},\mathscr{F}^{n}
and
\mathscr{G}^{n}
.

Proposition 5.9
.

Let Assumption
5.5
be in force; for a given

n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
, let
{\mathcal{H}}^{n}
,
\mathscr{F}^{n}
, and
\mathscr{G}^{n}
be as above. Then
{\mathcal{H}}^{n}
satisfies the conditions
\textrm{\rm a}^{\star}
)
and
\textrm{\rm b}^{\star}
)

in Remark
5.4
with the same Lipschitz constant
L
as the one of
\mathscr{H}
in Assumption
5.1
. In particular, there exists a unique
u^{n}\in{\mathrm{C}}([0,T];H)\cap\mathrm{AC}_{loc}((0,T);H)\cap L^{2}([0,T];H^{1,2})
such that
u^{n}_{t}\in D(\boldsymbol{\Delta})
for a.e.
t\in(0,T)
and

(5.14)

\partial_{t}u^{n}_{t}+\boldsymbol{\Delta}u^{n}_{t}-{\mathcal{H}}^{n}(\cdot,{\mathrm{D}}u^{n}_{t})+\mathscr{F}^{n}_{t}=0\quad\text{for a.e.\penalty\ }t\in(0,T),\quad u_{t}^{n}\bigr|_{t=T}=\mathscr{G}^{n}.

Proof.

As far
as

\text{a}^{\star}
) is concerned, it follows from the following straightforward inequalities

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\lvert{\mathcal{H}}^{n}(\mu,0)\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{D}(\mu)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\Bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{N(\varepsilon_{n},\mathbf{s}(\mu))}s_{j}(\mu)\lvert\mathscr{H}(x_{j}(\mu),0)\rvert\Bigg)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{D}(\mu)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\Bigg(\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\lvert\mathscr{H}(x,0)\rvert\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\Bigg)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{D}(\mu)<\infty,

where we have denoted by
(\mathbf{s}(\mu),\mathbf{x}(\mu))
the inverse of
\mathrm{em}
applied to
\mu
, see (
2.5
), and used that
\mathscr{H}
satisfies Assumption
5.1
a).

We now check
\text{b}^{\star}
).
For any
\mu=\mathrm{em}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x})\in\mathcal{P}_{o}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
,
\gamma^{1},\gamma^{2}\in L^{2}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d},\overline{\mathcal{D}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we have,
from the Lipschitz property of
\mathscr{H}
in Assumption
5.1
b),

(5.15)

\displaystyle\lvert{\mathcal{H}}^{n}(\mu,\gamma^{1})-{\mathcal{H}}^{n}(\mu,\gamma^{2})\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{N(\varepsilon_{n},\mathbf{s})}s_{j}\lvert\mathscr{H}(x_{j},\gamma^{1}(\mu,x_{j}))-\mathscr{H}(x_{j},\gamma^{2}(\mu,x_{j}))\rvert

(5.16)

\displaystyle\leq L\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\lvert\gamma^{1}(\mu,x)-\gamma^{2}(\mu,x)\rvert\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x),

and so
\text{b}^{\star}
) holds. Since
\mathscr{F}^{n}\in L^{2}([0,T];H)
and
\mathscr{G}^{n}\in H
,
the existence of a solution to (
5.14
)
(as claimed in the
statement) follows directly from Proposition
5.3
and Remark
5.4
.
∎

The following stability result shows that the solutions to the PDE (
5.14
) converge to the one of the PDE (
5.2
).

Proposition 5.10
.

Let Assumption
5.5
be in force,
({\mathcal{H}}^{n})_{n}
,
(\mathscr{F}^{n})_{n}
,
(\mathscr{G}^{n})_{n}
be as above, and
u^{n}
be the solution of (
5.14
) for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
. Then,
for
u
the solution of (
5.2
), it holds

(5.17)

\begin{split}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|u^{n}_{t}-u_{t}|_{H}^{2}+&\int_{0}^{T}\|{\mathrm{D}}u_{t}^{n}-{\mathrm{D}}u_{t}\|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\leq C(L,T)\left(|\mathscr{G}^{n}-\mathscr{G}|^{2}_{H}+\int_{0}^{T}|\mathscr{F}^{n}_{t}-\mathscr{F}_{t}|^{2}_{H}\,\mathrm{d}t+\mathcal{R}^{n}(\mathscr{H},{\mathrm{D}}u,T)\right)\to 0\text{ as }n\to\infty,\end{split}

where

\mathcal{R}^{n}(\mathscr{H},{\mathrm{D}}u,T):=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\sum_{i\geq N(\varepsilon_{n},\mathbf{s}(\mu))+1}^{\infty}s_{i}(\mu)|\mathscr{H}(x_{i}(\mu),{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}(x_{i}(\mu),\mu))|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{D}}(\mu)\,\mathrm{d}r,

and
C(L,T):=(1+L^{-2})(1+{\mathrm{e}}^{T(1+2L^{2})}+(1+2L^{2})T{\mathrm{e}}^{T(1+2L^{2})})
.
In particular, the left-hand side of (
5.17
) tends to
0
as
n
tends to
\infty
.

Proof.
