We use the same stability arguments as in the proof of Proposition
3.6
. Writing the equation solved by
u-u^{n}
, testing it against
u-u^{n}
, integrating
with respect to time on the interval
[t,T]
for any
t\in[0,T]
, and integrating by parts the term driven by
\boldsymbol{\Delta}
using (
2.24
), we get

(5.18)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}|u_{t}^{n}-u_{t}|_{H}^{2}+\int_{t}^{T}\|{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}^{n}-{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}\|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}|\mathscr{G}^{n}-\mathscr{G}|^{2}_{H}+\int_{t}^{T}(\mathcal{H}(\cdot,{\mathrm{D}}u_{r})-\mathcal{H}^{n}(\cdot,{\mathrm{D}}u^{n}_{r}),u_{r}^{n}-u_{r})_{H}\,\mathrm{d}r+\int_{t}^{T}(\mathscr{F}^{n}_{r}-\mathscr{F}_{r},u_{r}^{n}-u_{r})_{H}\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}|\mathscr{G}^{n}-\mathscr{G}|^{2}_{H}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{t}^{T}|u_{r}^{n}-u_{r}|^{2}_{H}\,\mathrm{d}r+L^{2}\int_{t}^{T}|u_{r}^{n}-u_{r}|^{2}_{H}\,\mathrm{d}r+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}|\mathscr{F}^{n}_{r}-\mathscr{F}_{r}|^{2}_{H}\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{4L^{2}}\int_{t}^{T}\left|\mathcal{H}(\cdot,{\mathrm{D}}u_{r})-\mathcal{H}^{n}(\cdot,{\mathrm{D}}u^{n}_{r})\right|^{2}_{H}\,\mathrm{d}r

(5.19)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}|\mathscr{G}^{n}-\mathscr{G}|^{2}_{H}+(1/2+L^{2})\int_{t}^{T}|u_{r}^{n}-u_{r}|^{2}_{H}\,\mathrm{d}r+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}|\mathscr{F}^{n}_{r}-\mathscr{F}_{r}|^{2}_{H}\,\mathrm{d}r

(5.20)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2L^{2}}\int_{t}^{T}\left|\mathcal{H}(\cdot,{\mathrm{D}}u_{r})-\mathcal{H}^{n}(\cdot,{\mathrm{D}}u_{r})\right|^{2}_{H}\,\mathrm{d}r

(5.21)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2L^{2}}\int_{t}^{T}\left|\mathcal{H}^{n}(\cdot,{\mathrm{D}}u_{r})-\mathcal{H}^{n}(\cdot,{\mathrm{D}}u^{n}_{r})\right|^{2}_{H}\,\mathrm{d}r,

where we have used Young’s inequality and we recall that
L
is the Lipschitz constant (depending solely on the set
A
) appearing in Assumption
5.1
b) for
\mathscr{H}
as well as in condition
\text{b}^{\star}
) in Remark
5.4
for
{\mathcal{H}}^{n}
, see the statement of Proposition
5.9
.

Next, we explain how to handle the last two terms
(
5.20
) and
(
5.21
).
For (
5.21
), we get, using once again
\text{b}^{\star}
) in Remark
5.4
for
{\mathcal{H}}^{n}
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2L^{2}}\int_{t}^{T}\left|{\mathcal{H}}^{n}(\cdot,{\mathrm{D}}u_{r})-{\mathcal{H}}^{n}(\cdot,{\mathrm{D}}u^{n}_{r})\right|^{2}_{H}\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2L^{2}}\int_{t}^{T}\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\left|\mathcal{H}^{n}(\mu,{\mathrm{D}}u_{r})-\mathcal{H}^{n}(\mu,{\mathrm{D}}u^{n}_{r})\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{D}(\mu)\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2L^{2}}\int_{t}^{T}\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\left[L\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}(x,\mu)-{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}^{n}(x,\mu)|\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\right]^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{D}(\mu)\,\mathrm{d}r

(5.22)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{t}^{T}\|{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}-{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}^{n}\|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r.

For (
5.20
), we observe that we can write

(5.23)

\begin{split}\frac{1}{2L^{2}}\int_{t}^{T}|\mathcal{H}(\mu,{\mathrm{D}}u_{r})&-\mathcal{H}^{n}(\mu,{\mathrm{D}}u_{r})|^{2}_{H}\,\mathrm{d}r\\
&=\frac{1}{2L^{2}}\int_{t}^{T}\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\left|\sum_{i=N(\varepsilon_{n},\mathbf{s}(\mu))+1}^{\infty}s_{i}(\mu)\mathscr{H}(x_{i}(\mu),{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}(x_{i}(\mu),\mu))\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{D}(\mu)\,\mathrm{d}r\\
&\leq\frac{1}{2L^{2}}\int_{t}^{T}\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\sum_{i=N(\varepsilon_{n},\mathbf{s}(\mu))+1}^{\infty}s_{i}(\mu)|\mathscr{H}(x_{i}(\mu),{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}(x_{i}(\mu),\mu))|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{D}}(\mu)\,\mathrm{d}r\\
&\leq\frac{1}{2L^{2}}\mathcal{R}^{n}(\mathscr{H},{\mathrm{D}}u,T).\end{split}

We write
\mathcal{R}^{n}(\mathscr{H},{\mathrm{D}}u,T)
in the form

\begin{split}\mathcal{R}^{n}(\mathscr{H},{\mathrm{D}}u,T)&=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}r_{n}(r,\mu)\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{D}(\mu)\,\mathrm{d}r\\
{\rm with}\quad r_{n}(r,\mu)&:=\sum_{i=N(\varepsilon_{n},\mathbf{s}(\mu))+1}^{\infty}s_{i}(\mu)|\mathscr{H}(x_{i}(\mu),{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}(x_{i}(\mu),\mu))|^{2}.\end{split}

Next, we prove by means of the dominated convergence theorem that

\mathcal{R}^{n}(\mathscr{H},{\mathrm{D}}u,T)\to 0
as

n\rightarrow\infty
. Domination follows from the bound

\displaystyle|r_{n}(r,\mu)|\leq\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}s_{i}(\mu)|\mathscr{H}(x_{i}(\mu),{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}(x_{i}(\mu),\mu))|^{2}=\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}|\mathscr{H}(x,{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}(x,\mu))|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x),

the right-hand side, seen as a function of
(r,\mu)
, belonging, as a consequence of Remark
5.2
and the fact that
\mathscr{H}
satisfies Assumption
5.1
, to
the space
L^{1}([0,T]\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),\mathscr{L}^{[0,T]}\otimes{\mathcal{D}})
. The above bound proves that the sequence
(|r_{n}(r,\mu)|)_{n}
is dominated by a common function in
L^{1}([0,T]\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),\mathscr{L}^{[0,T]}\otimes{\mathcal{D}})
. At the same time, it shows that

r_{n}(r,\mu)\to 0
pointwise as
n\to\infty
, since it is bounded by the remainder
of a convergent series. Therefore,

(5.24)

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathcal{R}^{n}(\mathscr{H},{\mathrm{D}}u,T)=0.

Combining (
5.19
),
(
5.20
),
(
5.21
),
(
5.22
), and (
5.23
), we deduce that, for every
t\in[0,T]
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}

\displaystyle|u_{t}^{n}-u_{t}|_{H}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{t}^{T}\|{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}^{n}-{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}\|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}|\mathscr{G}^{n}-\mathscr{G}|^{2}_{H}+(1/2+L^{2})\int_{t}^{T}|u_{r}^{n}-u_{r}|^{2}_{H}\,\mathrm{d}r+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}|\mathscr{F}^{n}_{r}-\mathscr{F}_{r}|^{2}_{H}\,\mathrm{d}r+\frac{1}{2L^{2}}\mathcal{R}^{n}(\mathscr{H},{\mathrm{D}}u,T).

The inequality in the statement follows then by the above inequality and a standard application of Gronwall’s lemma. The convergence in the statement follows from (
5.24
).
∎

Remark 5.11
.

Questions related to the rate of convergence can be approached as in the linear case, see Remark
4.24
.
Since the integrand in the definition of
{\mathcal{R}}^{n}
(in the statement of Proposition

5.10
) grows like

|{{\mathrm{D}}}u_{r}|^{2}
, the difficulties are the same, especially those stemming from the poor integrability properties of the gradient of
u
.

It is not difficult to see that an appropriate version of Proposition
5.6

also holds in the framework of this subsection. We state the result in the following proposition, omitting the proof since it can be obtained by repeating exactly the argument used for proving Proposition
5.6
.

Proposition 5.12
.

Let Assumption
5.5
be in force. For
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
, let
{\mathcal{H}}^{n}
,
\mathscr{F}^{n}
,
\mathscr{G}^{n}
be as above, and
u^{n}
be the (corresponding) unique solution of (
5.14
).
Let

t_{0}\in[0,T)
be an initial time. There exists a Borel subset
O
of
{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, with
{\mathcal{D}}(O)=1
, such that,
for any
m\in O
and any
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\mathcal{A}

\displaystyle\mathscr{G}^{n}(\mu^{\infty}_{T})

\displaystyle=u_{t}^{n}(\mu^{\infty}_{t})-\int_{t}^{T}\mathscr{F}^{n}_{r}(\mu^{\infty}_{r})\,\mathrm{d}r+\int_{t}^{T}\sum_{i=1}^{N(\varepsilon_{n},\mathbf{s}(m))}\varsigma_{i}\mathscr{H}(\xi_{r}^{i},{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}^{n}(\mu_{r}^{\infty},\xi_{r}^{i}))\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{t}^{T}\varsigma_{i}{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}^{n}(\mu_{r}^{\infty},\xi_{r}^{i})\cdot\alpha_{r}^{i}(\mu_{r}^{\infty},\xi_{r}^{i})\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int^{T}_{t}\sqrt{2\varsigma_{i}}{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}^{n}(\mu^{\infty}_{r},\xi^{i}_{r})\cdot\,\mathrm{d}\beta^{i}_{r},\quad t\in[t_{0},T],\quad{\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m),\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\text{-a.s.}

5.3.2.
The approximate control problem

Here we discuss, similarly
to Subsection
4.3
, a finite-dimensional approximation of the mean-field control problem introduced in
Subsection
5.2
.
The purpose of this approximation is twofold:
(i) on the one hand, it aims to provide, within the context of this paper, a counterpart to previously established results on the approximation of a mean-field control problem by a finite-dimensional (in that case, exchangeable) control problem;
(ii) on the other hand, it also aims to give an interpretation of the partial differential equation (
5.14
) and to explain how the solution to the latter can be viewed as the value function of an auxiliary control problem.

Our first step is thus to introduce a suitable class of controls to connect the approximate PDE (
5.14
) to the system of particles (
5.6
)
(taking advantage of the fact that
the
feedback functions
\alpha^{i}

may differ from one index

i
to another).
For every
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, we define

\mathcal{A}_{n}:=\left\{(\alpha^{i})_{i}\in\mathcal{A}:\alpha_{t}^{i}(\mu,x)=0\text{ for every }(t,\mu,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}\text{ and }i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\text{ s.t.\penalty\ }N(\varepsilon_{n},\mathbf{s}(\mu))<i\right\}.

For
an initial time
t_{0}\in[0,T)
, an initial distribution
\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}\in\mathcal{P}(T_{o}^{\infty}\times(\mathbb{T}^{d})_{o}^{\infty})
, and
an admissible control

{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
, the
n
-cost associated to
(t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}},{\boldsymbol{\alpha}})
is defined as

(5.25)

J^{n}(t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}},\boldsymbol{\alpha}):=\mathbb{E}^{\mathbf{Q}^{t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}},\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\left[\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\left(\mathscr{F}^{n}_{r}(\mu_{r}^{\infty})+\sum_{i=1}^{N(\varepsilon_{n},(\varsigma_{i})_{i})}\varsigma_{i}\mathscr{L}(\xi^{i}_{r},\alpha^{i}_{r}(\mu_{r}^{\infty},\xi_{r}^{i}))\right)\,\mathrm{d}r+\mathscr{G}^{n}(\mu^{\infty}_{T})\right].

The corresponding value function is defined as

(5.26)

V^{n}(t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}}):=\inf_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{n}}J^{n}(t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}},\boldsymbol{\alpha}),\quad(t_{0},\textrm{\rm\text{{m}}})\in[0,T)\times\mathcal{P}(T_{o}^{\infty}\times(\mathbb{T}^{d})_{o}^{\infty}).

The following result is the analogue of Theorem
5.7
for the approximate PDE (
5.14
).

Theorem 5.13
.

Let Assumption
5.5
be in force,
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, and
u^{n}
be the solution of (
5.14
). Then for every
t_{0}\in[0,T)
there exists a Borel subset
\tilde{O}_{t_{0}}
of
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, with
{\mathcal{D}}(\tilde{O}_{t_{0}})=1
, such that

(5.27)

u^{n}_{t_{0}}(m)=V^{n}(t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m))\quad\text{ for all }m\in\tilde{O}_{t_{0}}.

Moreover, if
t_{0}\in[0,T)
and
m\in\tilde{O}_{t_{0}}
, then the control

\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{n}=(\hat{\alpha}^{i,n})_{i}
, with the

\hat{\alpha}^{i,n}
’s being given by

\hat{\alpha}^{i,n}_{t}(\mu,x):=-\nabla_{p}\mathscr{H}(x,{\mathrm{D}}u_{t}^{n}(\mu,x))\mathbf{1}_{i\leq N(\varepsilon_{n},\mathbf{s}(\mu))},\quad(t,\mu,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d},

is optimal for
V^{n}(t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m))
and it is unique in the following sense: if
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in\mathcal{A}_{n}
is also optimal for
V^{n}(t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m))
, then,
for any
i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, for a.e.
r\in(t_{0},T)
,

{\mathbf{Q}}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m),\tilde{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\left(\left\{\tilde{\alpha}_{r}^{i}(\mu_{r}^{\infty},\xi_{r}^{i})=\hat{\alpha}_{r}^{i,n}(\mu_{r}^{\infty},\xi_{r}^{i})\right\}\right)=1.

Proof.

The proof follows the same argument as the one of Theorem
5.7
, using Proposition
5.12
instead of Proposition
5.6
, also noting that

\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\varsigma_{i}{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}^{n}(\mu_{r}^{\infty},\xi_{r}^{i})\cdot\alpha_{r}^{i}(\mu_{r}^{\infty},\xi_{r}^{i})\,\mathrm{d}r=\sum_{i=1}^{N(\varepsilon_{n},\mathbf{s}(m))}\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\varsigma_{i}{\mathrm{D}}u_{r}^{n}(\mu_{r}^{\infty},\xi_{r}^{i})\cdot\alpha_{r}^{i}(\mu_{r}^{\infty},\xi_{r}^{i})\,\mathrm{d}r\quad\mathbf{Q}^{t_{0},\mathrm{em}^{-1}(m),\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\text{-a.s.}

for every
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\mathcal{A}_{n}
,
t_{0}\in[0,T)
, and
m\in\tilde{O}_{t_{0}}
.
∎

Remark 5.14
.

The following observations are in order:

1)

Above, the threshold
N(\varepsilon_{n},{\mathbf{s}})
is defined as a function of
{\mathbf{s}}
, see (
2.32
). Recalling that

N(\varepsilon,\mathbf{s})\leq\lceil 1/\varepsilon\rceil
, we could also use

\lceil 1/\varepsilon\rceil
as a universal cap for the index
i
. The statement of Theorem

5.13
would remain unchanged.

2)

Although the feedback functions
(\alpha^{i})_{i}
in
{\mathcal{A}}_{n}
are identically zero for
i>N(\varepsilon_{n},\mathbf{s}(\mu))
, the corresponding particle system (
5.6
) remains infinite-dimensional, and each
\alpha^{i}
is still defined on an infinite-dimensional space.
This leads us to consider, in the statement below, a truly finite-dimensional version of the control problem.

In the statement below, we use the same finite-dimensional functional spaces as in the statement of Theorem
4.23
.

Theorem 5.15
.

Let Assumption
5.5
be in force,
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, and
u^{n}
be the solution of
(
5.14
)
. Then for every
t_{0}\in[0,T)
and
{\mathcal{D}}
-a.e.
m\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})

u^{n}_{t_{0}}(m)=k_{t_{0}}^{\mathbf{s}(m)}(x_{1}(m),\dots,x_{N(\varepsilon_{n},\mathbf{s}(m))}(m)),

where, for
\mathbf{s}\in T_{o}^{\infty}
,
k^{\mathbf{s}}
is the unique solution, in the space

{\mathrm{C}}^{0,1,N(\varepsilon_{n},\mathbf{s})}\cap(\cap_{p>1}W^{1,2,N(\varepsilon_{n},\mathbf{s})}_{p}
),
of the PDE, set on
[0,T]\times(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{N(\varepsilon_{n},\mathbf{s})}
,

(5.28)

\partial_{t}k_{t}+\sum_{i=1}^{N(\varepsilon_{n},\mathbf{s})}\frac{1}{s_{i}}\Delta_{i}k_{t}+\sum_{i=1}^{N(\varepsilon_{n},\mathbf{s})}s_{i}\mathscr{H}(\cdot,s_{i}^{-1}\nabla_{i}k_{t})+\mathscr{F}^{n}_{t}\circ\mathrm{em}^{N(\varepsilon_{n},\mathbf{s})}(\mathbf{s},\cdot)=0\quad\text{ for a.e. }t\in(0,T)

with final condition

k_{t}\rvert_{t=T}=\mathscr{G}^{n}\circ\mathrm{em}^{N(\varepsilon_{n},\mathbf{s})}(\mathbf{s},\cdot).

Before presenting the proof of Theorem
5.15
, we explain its meaning from the perspective of stochastic control.
For a fixed

\mathbf{s}\in T^{\infty}_{o}
,
equation (
5.28
) is indeed a finite-dimensional Hamilton-Jacobi-Bellman equation. It is associated
with a stochastic control problem in finite dimension, which can be described as follows.
With the shorthand notation

N:=N(\varepsilon_{n},\mathbf{s})
,
and for an arbitrary filtered probability space
(\Omega,{\mathcal{F}},{\mathbb{P}},\{{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]})
endowed with a collection
\{B^{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
of independent
d
-dimensional
\{{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\}_{t\in[t_{0},T]}
-Brownian motions, we consider the collection of
A^{N}
-valued
\{{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
-progressively-measurable open-loop controls

\Gamma^{N}_{o}:=\left\{{\mbox{\boldmath$\delta$}}^{N}:=(\delta^{1},\dots,\delta^{N}):\delta^{i}:[0,T]\times\Omega\to A\text{ is progressively measurable }\forall\,i\in\{1,\dots,N\}\right\}.

For the
same collection
of weights

\mathbf{s}\in T^{\infty}_{o}
,
and with
an initial condition

(t_{0},\mathbf{x}^{N}=(x_{i})_{i=1}^{N})\in[0,T)\times(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{N}
, and a control
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{N}\in\Gamma^{N}_{o}
, we associate the cost

(5.29)

{J}_{o}^{N,\mathbf{s}}(t_{0},\mathbf{x}^{N},{\mbox{\boldmath$\delta$}}^{N}):=\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}\left[\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\left(\mathscr{F}^{n}_{r}\circ\mathrm{em}^{N}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{X}^{N}_{r})+\sum_{i=1}^{N}s_{i}\mathscr{L}(X_{r}^{i},\delta^{i}_{r}))\right)\,\mathrm{d}r+\mathscr{G}^{n}\circ\mathrm{em}^{N}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{X}^{N}_{T})\right],

where
\mathbf{X}^{N}=\{(X^{i}_{t})_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\}_{i=1}^{N}
is the stochastic process on
(\Omega,{\mathcal{F}},{\mathbb{P}},\{{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]})
defined by

(5.30)

\begin{cases}\,\mathrm{d}X^{i}_{t}&=\delta^{i}_{t}\,\mathrm{d}t+{\sqrt{\frac{2}{s_{i}}}}\,\mathrm{d}B^{i}_{t},\quad t\in[t_{0},T],\quad i\in\{1,\dots,N\},\\
X^{i}_{t_{0}}&=\iota(x_{i}),\quad i\in\{1,\dots,N\}.\end{cases}

As a corollary of the proof of
Theorem
5.15
, we will identify the value function with the solution of (
5.28
).

Corollary 5.16
.

Within the framework of
Theorem
5.15
,
the value function of the control problem
(
5.29
)–(
5.30
)
is the function
k^{\mathbf{s}}
, i.e.,

(5.31)

k_{t_{0}}^{\mathbf{s}}(\mathbf{x}^{N})=\inf_{{\mbox{\boldmath$\delta$}}^{N}\in\Gamma^{N}_{o}}{J}_{o}^{N,\mathbf{s}}(t_{0},\mathbf{x}^{N},{\mbox{\boldmath$\delta$}}^{N}),\quad t_{0}\in[0,T),\,\mathbf{x}^{N}\in(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{N}.

Consistently
with the control problem formulated in (
5.25
), we also introduce a version of (
5.29
)–(
5.30
) set over
(Marokvian) closed-loop controls. Here, the set of closed-loop controls is given by

\Gamma^{N}_{c}:=\left\{{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}^{N}:=(\gamma^{1},\dots,\gamma^{N}):\gamma^{i}:[0,T]\times(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{N}\to A\text{ is measurable }\forall\,i\in\{1,\dots,N\}\right\},

and, for given

t_{0}\in[0,T)

and

{\mathbf{x}}^{N}\in(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{N}
,
the cost function is

{J}_{c}^{N,\mathbf{s}}(t_{0},\mathbf{x}^{N},{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}^{N}):=\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{Q}}\left[\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\left(\mathscr{F}^{n}_{r}\circ\mathrm{em}^{N}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{X}^{N}_{r})+\sum_{i=1}^{N}s_{i}\mathscr{L}(X_{r}^{i},\gamma^{i}_{r}(\mathbf{X}^{N}_{r}))\right)\,\mathrm{d}r+\mathscr{G}^{n}\circ\mathrm{em}^{N}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{X}^{N}_{T})\right],

where, on the space
(\Omega,{\mathcal{F}},{\mathbb{P}},\{{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]},\{(B^{i}_{t})_{t\in[t_{0},T]}\}_{i=1}^{N})
,

\mathbf{X}^{N}
is
the solution to

(5.32)

\begin{cases}\,\mathrm{d}X^{i}_{t}&=\gamma^{i}_{t}(\mathbf{X}^{N}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+{\sqrt{\frac{2}{s_{i}}}}\,\mathrm{d}B^{i}_{t},\quad t\in[t_{0},T],\quad i\in\{1,\dots,N\},\\
X^{i}_{t_{0}}&=\iota(x_{i}),\quad i\in\{1,\dots,N\}.\end{cases}

Note that, in the above equations, we are using the same convention as in Remark
2.15
to evaluate
\gamma_{r}^{i}
at
\mathbf{X}^{N}_{r}\in(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{N}
.
Note also that, even though the functions

\gamma^{1},\cdots,\gamma^{N}
are just assumed to be measurable (and bounded since they are valued in
A
), the system (
5.32
) is uniquely solvable in the strong sense, see
[
61
]
. As such, it is also uniquely solvable in the weak sense, but there is no need here to deal with weak solutions; this makes a difference with the infinite dimensional setting
introduced in Subsection
5.2
.

It is a standard result in the theory of stochastic control that the control problems defined respectively over open and closed controls share the same value function. We reestablish this fact below as a consequence of the verification argument used in the proof of Theorem
5.15
. In particular, we have
the following analogue of Corollary

5.16
.

Corollary 5.17
.

Within the framework of
Theorem
5.15
,
the value function of the control problem
(
5.29
)–(
5.30
)
is the function
k^{\mathbf{s}}
, i.e.,

(5.33)

k_{t_{0}}^{\mathbf{s}}(\mathbf{x}^{N})=\inf_{{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}^{N}\in\Gamma^{N}_{c}}{J}_{c}^{N,\mathbf{s}}(t_{0},\mathbf{x}^{N},{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}^{N}),\quad t_{0}\in[0,T),\,\mathbf{x}^{N}\in(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{N}.

We stress once again that the dimension
N
in (
5.33
) depends on
\mathbf{s}
. That said, as mentioned in the
first item of Remark

5.14
, one could define another truncation in (
5.25
), with
N
therein being equal to
\lceil 1/\varepsilon_{n}\rceil
. With this definition, all the PDEs (
5.28
) (that still depend on
\mathbf{s}
) would be set over the same space.

We now turn to the proof of the last three statements.

Proofs of Theorem
5.15
and Corollaries
5.16
and
5.17
.

Existence and uniqueness of a solution to the PDE
(
5.28
)
(within the class specified in the statement of Theorem
5.15
)
follow from

[
22
, Theorem 2.1]
.
By the
Krylov version of Itô’s formula, see

[
43
, Theorem 2.10.1]
,
one can expand, for
a collection of weights

\mathbf{s}\in T^{\infty}_{o}
,
an initial condition
(t_{0},{\mathbf{x}}^{N})

(with
N:=N(\varepsilon_{n},\mathbf{s})
)
and an open-loop control
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{N}
(in
\Gamma^{N}_{o}
), the process

\left(k_{t}^{\mathbf{s}}(\mathbf{X}_{t}^{N})+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\left(\mathscr{F}^{n}_{r}\circ\mathrm{em}^{N}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{X}^{N}_{r})+\sum_{i=1}^{N}s_{i}\mathscr{L}(X_{r}^{i},\delta^{i}_{r})\right)\,\mathrm{d}r\right)_{t\in[t_{0},T]}.

Reproducing the computations carried out in the proofs of Theorems
5.7
and
5.13
(the setting is even simpler here since the underlying space is of finite dimension), we can prove that

(5.34)

{J}_{o}^{N,\mathbf{s}}(t_{0},\mathbf{x}^{N},{\mbox{\boldmath$\delta$}}^{N})\geq k_{t_{0}}^{\mathbf{s}}(\mathbf{x}^{N}),

with equality if and only if, almost everywhere under

{\mathscr{L}}^{[t_{0},T]}\otimes{\mathbb{P}}
,

\delta_{t}^{i}=-\nabla_{p}{\mathscr{H}}\left(X^{i}_{t},s_{i}^{-1}\nabla_{i}k_{t}(\mathbf{X}^{N}_{t})\right),

i.e.

\mathbf{X}^{N}
is driven by the
closed-loop
control
\gamma^{N}\in\Gamma^{N}_{c}
given by

\gamma_{t}^{i}(\mathbf{x})=-\nabla_{p}{\mathscr{H}}\left(x_{i},s_{i}^{-1}\nabla_{i}k_{t}(\mathbf{x})\right),

which proves, as a by-product, the two Corollaries
5.16

and

5.17
.

Therefore, by Theorem
5.13
, the
conclusion of Theorem
5.15
follows if we show that, for every
(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x})\in T_{o}^{\infty}\times(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{\infty}_{o}
and every
t_{0}\in[0,T)
, setting
N:=N(\varepsilon_{n},\mathbf{s})
and
\mathbf{x}^{N}:=\pi_{N}(\mathbf{x})
,
(recalling (
5.26
)
for the definition of
V^{n}
):

(i)

the infimum in (
5.33
) is larger than
V^{n}(t_{0},(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x}))
;

(ii)

the infimum in (
5.31
) is smaller than
V^{n}(t_{0},(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x}))
.

To prove (i), we proceed as follows: let
{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}^{N}\in\Gamma^{N}_{c}
be given and let us define

\alpha_{t}^{i}(\mu,x):=\gamma_{t}^{i}\left(\pi_{N(\varepsilon_{n},\mathbf{s}(\mu))}(\mathbf{x}(\mu))\right)\mathbf{1}_{i\leq N(\varepsilon_{n},\mathbf{s}(\mu))}\quad(t,\mu,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{o}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d},\,\,i\in\{1,\dots,N\},

and
\alpha^{i}=0
if
i>N
. We also set
\alpha^{i}(\mu,x)=0
if
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\setminus\mathcal{P}_{o}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. Notice that
\alpha^{i}
is Borel measurable, since
\mathcal{P}_{o}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
is a Borel subset of
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, see the discussion below (
2.4
).
For this
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
, we easily check that the

N
first coordinates
of the
(weak) solution to (
5.6
)
(constructed on
some
set-up

({\Omega},{{\mathcal{F}}},{\mathbb{Q}},\{{{\mathcal{F}}}_{t}\}_{t\in[t_{0},T]},(({\mathbf{s}},{\mathbf{x}}),({W}^{i})_{i}))
) coincides with the solution
to (
5.32
), when constructed on the same set-up
(and when the initial conditions are the same).
Since the law of the
solution to
(
5.32
) is independent of the probabilistic set-up, we deduce that

{J}_{c}^{N,\mathbf{s}}(t_{0},\mathbf{x}^{N},{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}^{N})=J^{n}(t_{0},(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x}),\boldsymbol{\alpha})\geq V^{n}(t_{0},(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x})).

Taking the infimum over
{\mbox{\boldmath$\gamma$}}^{N}\in\Gamma^{N}_{c}
in the above inequality, we conclude the proof of (i).

To prove (ii), we proceed in the opposite way: let
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\mathcal{A}_{n}
be given and, on the filtered probability space
(\Xi,{\mathcal{G}},\mathbf{Q}^{t_{0},(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x}),\boldsymbol{\alpha}},\{{\mathcal{G}}_{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]})
endowed with the collection
(\beta^{i})_{i=1}^{N}
of independent
d
-dimensional
\{{\mathcal{G}}_{t}\}_{t\in[t_{0},T]}
-Brownian motions, let us define the progressively measurable functions

\delta^{i}_{t}:=\alpha_{t}^{i}(\mu_{t}^{\infty},\xi_{t}^{i})\quad t\in[0,T],\,i\in\{1,\dots,N\}.

Then
{\mbox{\boldmath$\delta$}}^{N}:=(\delta^{1},\dots,\delta^{N})\in\Gamma_{o}^{N}
and the process
\mathbf{X}^{N}:=(\xi^{1},\dots,\xi^{N})
satisfy (
5.30
) (on the canonical set-up). Reproducing the proof of
(
5.34
), we deduce that

k_{t_{0}}^{\mathbf{s}}(\mathbf{x}^{N})\leq{J}_{o}^{N,\mathbf{s}}(t_{0},\mathbf{x}^{N},{\mbox{\boldmath$\delta$}}^{N})=J^{n}(t_{0},(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x}),\boldsymbol{\alpha}).

Taking the infimum over
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\mathcal{A}_{n}
in the above inequality, we conclude the proof of (ii).
∎

Appendix A
L-derivative and its relation with the vector-valued gradient
{\mathrm{D}}

We compare the notion of derivative discussed in Subsection
2.2
with another popular definition, which is particularly relevant in the context of mean-field games and mean-field control.

Definition A.1
.

We say that a continuous function
u:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
is linearly differentiable at
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
if the limit

{\mathrm{D}}_{F}u(\mu,x):=\lim_{t\downarrow 0}\frac{u((1-t)\mu+t\delta_{x})-u(\mu)}{t}

exists and it is finite for every
x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
. The real number above is called
flat or linear functional derivative
of
u
at
(\mu,x)
. The space of continuous functions which are everywhere linearly differentiable and whose flat derivative is everywhere continuous in
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}
is denoted by
{\mathrm{C}}^{1}_{{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))
.

The flat derivative of
u\in{\mathrm{C}}^{1}_{{\mathrm{F}}}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))
can be also characterized as the unique continuous function
G:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying

(A.1)

u(\mu)-u(\nu)=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}G(t\mu+(1-t)\nu,x)\,\mathrm{d}(\mu-\nu)(x)\,\mathrm{d}t,\quad\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}G(\mu,x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=0

for every
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
(see
[
8
, Chapter 2.2]
).

Definition A.2
.

We say that
u:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
is L-differentiable at
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
if
u
is linearly differentiable at
\mu
and the gradient

{\mathrm{D}}_{L}u(\mu,x):=\nabla{\mathrm{D}}_{F}(\mu,x)

exists for every
x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
, where
\nabla
denotes the gradient with respect to the
x
-variable. The vector above is called
L-derivative of

u
at
(\mu,x)
. The space of continuous functions which are everywhere L-differentiable and whose L-derivative is everywhere continuous (and so bounded) is denoted by
{\mathrm{C}}^{1}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))
.

Remark A.3
.

The L-derivative introduced here is strongly related, at least in some cases, to the one introduced by P.-L. Lions in
[
44
]
(see also
[
9
, Chapter 5.2]
) through the so-called lifting approach. For our purposes, it is enough to note that if a function
u
belongs to
{\mathrm{C}}^{1}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))
, then it is differentiable in the sense of Lions, and the two derivatives coincide (see
[
9
, Proposition 5.84]
). The converse may also be true under further assumptions on the Fréchet derivative of the lifting of
u
(see
[
9
, Proposition 5.51]
).

Remark A.4
.

It is easy to see that if
u\in{\mathrm{C}}^{1}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))
, then it is
W_{2}
-Lipschitz with Lipschitz constant
\left\|{\mathrm{D}}_{L}u\right\|_{\infty}
. Indeed, since
{\mathrm{D}}_{L}u
is bounded, we have that for any
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
the map
x\mapsto{\mathrm{D}}_{F}u(\mu,x)
is Lipschitz with Lipschitz constant bounded by
\left\|{\mathrm{D}}_{L}u\right\|_{\infty}
. Then, by (
A.1
) and the dual representation of the
1
-Wasserstein distance
W_{1}
, see e.g.
[
62
, Theorem 1.14]
, and the inequality
W_{1}\leq W_{2}
, it holds

\lvert u(\mu)-u(\nu)\rvert\leq\left\|{\mathrm{D}}_{L}u\right\|_{\infty}\sup_{\phi\colon\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits\phi\leq 1}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\phi\,\mathrm{d}(\mu-\nu)=\left\|{\mathrm{D}}_{L}u\right\|_{\infty}W_{1}(\mu,\nu)\leq\left\|{\mathrm{D}}_{L}u\right\|_{\infty}W_{2}(\mu,\nu),

where we have denoted by
\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits\phi
the global Lipschitz constant of
\phi
.

It is clear that if
u\in\mathfrak{Z}^{\infty}
then
u\in{\mathrm{C}}^{1}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))
and
\boldsymbol{\nabla}u={\mathrm{D}}_{L}u
.

Proposition A.5
.

Let
u\in{\mathrm{C}}^{1}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))
. Then for every Borel probability measure
\mathfrak{m}
on
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
there exists a sequence of cylinder functions
(u_{n})_{n}\subset\mathfrak{Z}^{\infty}
such that

(A.2)

u_{n}\to u\text{ in }L^{2}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),\mathfrak{m}),\quad\boldsymbol{\nabla}u_{n}\to{\mathrm{D}}_{L}u\text{ in }L^{2}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d},\overline{\mathfrak{m}};\mathbb{R}^{d}),

where
\overline{\mathfrak{m}}
is the measure over
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}
defined by (
2.25
) with
\mathfrak{m}

in place of
{\mathcal{D}}
.

Proof.

We divide the proof into three claims.

Claim 1
. We define, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, the functions
h_{n}:(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
and
v_{n}:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
as

\displaystyle h_{n}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})

\displaystyle:=u\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{x_{i}}\right),\quad

\displaystyle(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{n},

\displaystyle v_{n}(\mu)

\displaystyle:=\int_{(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{n}}h_{n}\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes n},\quad

\displaystyle\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),

where
\mu^{\otimes n}
denotes the measure on
(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{n}
obtained as the product of
n
copies of
\mu
. Then
(v_{n})_{n}\subset{\mathrm{C}}^{1}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))
,
(h_{n})_{n}\subset{\mathrm{C}}^{1}((\mathbb{T}^{d})^{n})
,
\|v_{n}\|_{\infty}\leq\|u\|_{\infty}
,
\|{\mathrm{D}}_{L}v_{n}\|_{\infty}\leq\|{\mathrm{D}}_{L}u\|_{\infty}
and the following formulas hold

\displaystyle\nabla_{j}h_{n}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}{\mathrm{D}}_{L}u\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{x_{i}},x_{j}\right),\quad

\displaystyle(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{n},

\displaystyle{\mathrm{D}}_{L}v_{n}(\mu,x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d(n-1)}}{\mathrm{D}}_{L}u\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\delta_{x_{i}}+\frac{1}{n}\delta_{x},x\right)\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes(n-1)}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1}),\quad

\displaystyle(\mu,x)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d},

where
\nabla_{j}
denotes the gradient w.r.t. the
j
-th variable. Moreover,

(A.3)

\lim_{n\to\infty}v_{n}(\mu)=u(\mu),\quad\lim_{n\to\infty}{\mathrm{D}}_{L}v_{n}(\mu,x)={\mathrm{D}}_{L}u(\mu,x)\quad\text{ for every }(\mu,x)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}.

Proof of claim 1
. The fact that
(h_{n})_{n}\subset{\mathrm{C}}^{1}((\mathbb{T}^{d})^{n})
and the formula for
\nabla_{j}h_{n}
can be found for instance in
[
9
, Proposition 5.35]
. Then, it is not difficult to check (see, e.g.,
[
17
, proof of Theorem 4.4]
) that
v_{n}
is L-differentiable and that

\displaystyle{\mathrm{D}}_{L}v_{n}(\mu,x)

\displaystyle=n\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d(n-1)}}\nabla_{n}h_{n}(\cdot,x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes(n-1)}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d(n-1)}}{\mathrm{D}}_{L}u\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\delta_{x_{i}}+\frac{1}{n}\delta_{x},x\right)\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes(n-1)}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1}),\quad(\mu,x)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}.

This, together with the definition of
v_{n}
, gives the desired bounds and the fact that
(v_{n})_{n}\subset{\mathrm{C}}^{1}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))
. The convergence in (
A.3
) follows by the law of large numbers and the continuity of
u
and
{\mathrm{D}}_{L}u
. This concludes the proof of the first claim.

Claim 2
. Let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
be fixed and let
v_{n}
be as in claim 1. Then, there exists a sequence of polynomials
(p^{n}_{k})_{k}
in
(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{n}
satisfying

(A.4)

p_{k}^{n}(x_{\sigma(1)},\dots,x_{\sigma(n)})=p_{k}^{n}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\text{ for every }(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{n}

and for every permutation
\sigma
of
\{1,\dots,n\}
, such that, setting

w_{k}^{n}(\mu):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{dn}}p_{k}^{n}\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes n},\quad\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),

we have that
w_{k}^{n}
is
L
-differentiable at every
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, there exists a constant
C=C(\|u\|_{\infty},\|{\mathrm{D}}_{L}u\|_{\infty})>0
such that
\|w_{k}^{n}\|_{\infty}\leq C
,
\|{\mathrm{D}}_{L}w_{k}^{n}\|_{\infty}\leq C
for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and

(A.5)

\lim_{k\to\infty}w_{k}^{n}(\mu)=v_{n}(\mu),\quad\lim_{k\to\infty}{\mathrm{D}}_{L}w_{k}^{n}(\mu,x)={\mathrm{D}}_{L}v_{n}(\mu,x)\quad\text{ for every }(\mu,x)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}.

Proof of claim 2
.
We take any sequence of polynomials
(p_{k}^{n})_{k}
in
(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{n}
satisfying (
A.4
) such that

(A.6)

\left\|h_{n}-p_{k}^{n}\right\|_{{\mathrm{C}}^{1}((\mathbb{T}^{d})^{n})}\to 0\quad\text{ as }k\to\infty.

Up to a unrelabeled subsequence, we can assume that
\|p_{k}^{n}\|_{C^{1}((\mathbb{T}^{d})^{n})}\leq 2\|h_{n}\|_{\infty}
for every
k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
. We have (again by e.g.,
[
17
, proof of Theorem 4.4]
) that
w_{n}^{k}
is L-differentiable at every
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and, since
p_{k}^{n}
satisfies (
A.4
), it also holds

{\mathrm{D}}_{L}w_{k}^{n}(\mu,x)=n\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d(n-1)}}\nabla_{n}p_{k}^{n}(\cdot,x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes(n-1)},\quad(\mu,x)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d},

where
\nabla_{n}
denotes the gradient w.r.t. the
n
-th variable. We clearly have
\|w_{k}^{n}\|_{\infty}\leq 2\|u\|_{\infty}
and

|{\mathrm{D}}_{L}w_{k}^{n}(\mu,x)|\leq n\|\nabla_{n}p_{k}\|_{C(K^{n})}\leq 2n\|\nabla_{n}h_{n}\|_{\infty}\leq 2\|{\mathrm{D}}_{L}v_{n}\|_{\infty}\leq 2\|{\mathrm{D}}_{L}u\|_{\infty},

thanks to the estimates obtained in claim 1.
This proves the bounds. Using the dominated convergence theorem and (
A.6
) we deduce both the convergences. This concludes the proof of the second claim.

Claim 3
. Conclusion.

Proof of claim 3
. Let
\mathfrak{m}
be any Borel probability measure on
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. By claim 1, we can find a sequence
(v_{n})_{n}\subset{\mathrm{C}}^{1}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
of the form

v_{n}(\mu)=\int_{(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{n}}h_{n}\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes n},\quad\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),

for functions
h_{n}\in{\mathrm{C}}^{1}((\mathbb{T}^{d})^{n})
satisfying (
A.4
), such that

v_{n}\to u\text{ in }L^{2}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),\mathfrak{m}),\quad{\mathrm{D}}_{L}v_{n}\to{\mathrm{D}}_{L}u\text{ in }L^{2}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d},\overline{\mathfrak{m}})\text{ as }n\to\infty.

By claim 2, we can find a sequence of polynomials
(p_{n,k})_{k}
in
(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{n}
such that, defining

u_{n,k}(\mu):=\int_{(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{n}}p_{n,k}\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes n},\quad\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),

then
u_{n,k}\in\mathfrak{Z}^{\infty}
, it is L-differentiable at every
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
,
\|u_{n,k}\|_{\infty}\leq 2\|u\|_{\infty}
,
\|{\mathrm{D}}_{L}u_{n,k}\|_{\infty}\leq 2\|{\mathrm{D}}_{L}u\|_{\infty}
and

u_{n,k}(\mu)\to v_{n}(\mu),\quad{\mathrm{D}}_{L}u_{n,k}(\mu,x)\to{\mathrm{D}}_{L}v_{n}(\mu,x)\quad\text{ for every }(\mu,x)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}

as
k\to\infty
.
Moreover, by dominated convergence,
u_{n,k}\to v_{n}
in
L^{2}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),\mathfrak{m})
and
{\mathrm{D}}_{L}u_{n,k}\to{\mathrm{D}}_{L}v_{n}
in
L^{2}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d},\overline{\mathfrak{m}})
as
k\to\infty
. Then, a diagonal argument in
L^{2}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),\mathfrak{m})\times L^{2}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\overline{\mathfrak{m}})
concludes the proof of the third claim.
∎

Proposition A.6
.

Let
{\mathcal{D}}
be the Dirichlet–Ferguson measure on
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. Then

{\mathrm{C}}^{1}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))\subset H^{1,2}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),W_{2},{\mathcal{D}})

and,

{\mathrm{D}}_{L}u={\mathrm{D}}u\quad\text{ for every }u\in{\mathrm{C}}^{1}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})),

where
{\mathrm{D}}
is as in Proposition
2.13
.

Proof.

Since, by Remark
A.4
, we have that
{\mathrm{C}}^{1}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))\subset\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits_{b}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),W_{2})
and, by definition,
\mathop{\rm Lip}\nolimits_{b}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),W_{2})\subset H^{1,2}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),W_{2},{\mathcal{D}})
, we get the first inclusion in the statement. Let
u\in{\mathrm{C}}^{1}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))
and recall that closability holds (see Subsection
2.2.2
); by Proposition
A.5
, we can find a sequence of cylinder functions
(u_{n})_{n}\subset\mathfrak{Z}^{\infty}
satisfying (
A.2
) for
u
. Let also
(u_{n}^{\prime})_{n}\subset\mathfrak{Z}^{\infty}
be as in (
2.23
) and let us define
z_{n}:=u_{n}-u_{n}^{\prime}
; we have that
z_{n}\to 0
in
L^{2}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),{\mathcal{D}})
and
\boldsymbol{\nabla}z_{n}=\boldsymbol{\nabla}u_{n}-\boldsymbol{\nabla}u_{n}^{\prime}\to{\mathrm{D}}_{L}u-{\mathrm{D}}_{\mathfrak{m}}u
in
L^{2}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d},\overline{{\mathcal{D}}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
. By definition of closability (cf. (
2.14
) and
[
36
, Definition 5.6]
), we deduce that it must be
{\mathrm{D}}_{L}u={\mathrm{D}}u
.
∎

Remark A.7
.

It is easy to see that the inclusion in Proposition
A.6
holds not only for
{\mathcal{D}}
, but for every Borel probability measure
\mathfrak{m}
on
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. In particular
{\mathrm{C}}^{1}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))\subset H^{1,2}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),W_{2},\mathfrak{m})
, where a precise definition of
H^{1,2}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),W_{2},\mathfrak{m})
can be found in
[
36
]
. On the other hand, the equivalence between
{\mathrm{D}}_{L}u
and
{\mathrm{D}}u
is strongly related to the choice of
{\mathcal{D}}
and the closability of the quadratic form associated.

Appendix B
Explicit approximation
of continuous functions on
{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})

The purpose of this section is to provide a quantitative and explicit method for approximating continuous functions on
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
(or more generally, functions on
[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}
), in contrast
with Proposition

2.7

which relies on a non-explicit approach.

Throughout, we make use of the equivalence
relation introduced, for a given
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
, in Definition

4.22
, and of the related
class
\mathscr{A}_{\epsilon}^{1}

of functions defined on the subsequent quotient space. The same discussion and the same results can be also done when
\mathscr{A}_{\epsilon}^{1}
is replaced by
\mathscr{A}_{\epsilon}^{m}
for any
m\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, but, for the sake of simplicity, we work here with the simpler choice
m=1
. For
a continuous function
{\mathfrak{u}}\colon[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, we look for a family
({\mathfrak{u}}^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon\in(0,1]}

that converges
to
{\mathfrak{u}}
in a
suitable
sense as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
.
For a given
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
, we define the mapping
T_{\varepsilon}\colon\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\to\mathcal{P}^{pa}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, recalling that the latter is the subset of
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
of purely atomic measures, by

T_{\varepsilon}(\mu):=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{\mu(\{x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}\colon\mu_{x}>\varepsilon\})}\mu\rvert_{\{x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}\colon\mu_{x}>\varepsilon\}},\quad&\text{ if }\mu(\{x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}\colon\mu_{x}>\varepsilon\})>0,\\
\delta_{0},\quad&\text{ if }\mu(\{x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}\colon\mu_{x}>\varepsilon\})=0.\end{cases}

In words,

T_{\varepsilon}(\mu)
is the conditional probability of
\mu
given the subset of its atoms with mass greater than
\varepsilon
.
Notice in particular that, if
\mu=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}s_{j}\delta_{x_{j}}
, with
(x_{j})_{j}\subset\mathbb{T}^{d}

and
0<s_{j+1}\leq s_{j}
for every
j\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, then

T_{\varepsilon}(\mu)=\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{N^{\mu,\varepsilon}}s_{j}\delta_{x_{j}}}{\sum_{j=1}^{N^{\mu,\varepsilon}}s_{j}},

under the additional notation
N^{\mu,\varepsilon}:=\max\{j\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\colon s_{j}\geq\varepsilon\}
.
Also, it is easy to see that if
\mu
is purely atomic, then
T_{\varepsilon}(\mu)\to\mu
weakly as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
.
The rate of convergence can be computed explicitly, using the total variation distance, here denoted by

d_{\rm TV}
:

(B.1)

\begin{split}d_{\rm TV}\left(T_{\varepsilon}(\mu),\mu\right)&=2\sup_{B\in{\mathcal{B}}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\left|[T_{\varepsilon}(\mu)](B)-\mu(B)\right|\\
&\leq 2\sum_{j=1}^{N^{\mu,\varepsilon}}\Bigl|s_{j}-\frac{s_{j}}{\sum_{i=1}^{N^{\mu,\varepsilon}}s_{i}}\Bigr|+2\sum_{j\geq N^{\mu,\varepsilon}+1}s_{j}\leq 4\sum_{j\geq N^{\mu,\varepsilon}+1}s_{j}.\end{split}

Thus, given
{\mathfrak{u}}\colon[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, we can define its approximation
{\mathfrak{u}}^{\varepsilon}\colon[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
as

{\mathfrak{u}}_{t}^{\varepsilon}(\mu,x):={\mathfrak{u}}_{t}(T_{\varepsilon}(\mu),x),\quad(t,\mu,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}.

Notice that,
if
\mu
and
\nu
are
\varepsilon
-compatible as in in Definition

4.22
for a given
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
, then by definition of
T_{\varepsilon}
, it holds
{\mathfrak{u}}_{t}^{\varepsilon}(\mu,x)={\mathfrak{u}}_{t}^{\varepsilon}(\nu,x)
for any
(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}^{d}
. This implies that
{\mathfrak{u}}^{\varepsilon}\in\mathscr{A}_{\varepsilon}^{1}
.
Moreover, by construction, we also have
\left\|{\mathfrak{u}}^{\varepsilon}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\left\|{\mathfrak{u}}\right\|_{\infty}
for any
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
. The convergence of
{\mathfrak{u}}^{\varepsilon}
to
{\mathfrak{u}}
is immediate since
{\mathfrak{u}}
is continuous, and, by dominated convergence, the convergence also holds
in
L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{Q})
, for any Borel probability measure
\mathcal{Q}
on
[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}
whose marginal on
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
is concentrated on
\mathcal{P}^{pa}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
.
The rate of convergence can be computed under
{\mathscr{L}}^{[0,T]}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{D}}
, using the modulus of continuity (in the
\mu
-variable) of
{\mathfrak{u}}
:

\omega(\delta):=\sup_{t\in[0,T],x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\sup_{\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}^{pa}{(\mathbb{T}^{d})}:d_{\textrm{\rm TV}(\mu,\nu)\leq\delta}}|{\mathfrak{u}}(t,\mu,x)-{\mathfrak{u}}(t,\nu,x)|,\quad\delta\in[0,2].

Since
[0,T]\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\times\mathbb{T}^{d}

is compact and

{\mathfrak{u}}
is continuous
(on the product space, equipped with the product topology) and because the square of the
W_{2}
distance (that metricizes the weak topology on
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
)
is dominated by the total variation distance, it holds that

\lim_{\delta\rightarrow 0}\omega(\delta)=0.

And then,

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\left[\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\left|{\mathfrak{u}}^{\varepsilon}_{t}(\mu,x)-{\mathfrak{u}}_{t}(\mu,x)\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\right]\,\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{D}}(\mu)\,\mathrm{d}t\leq T\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\omega^{2}\left(d_{\textrm{\rm TV}}(T_{\varepsilon}(\mu),\mu)\right)\,\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{D}}(\mu).

Consider now any concave function

\vartheta:[0,2]\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
that dominates

T\omega^{2}

(e.g., if
\omega
is linear, then
\vartheta
is also linear; if
\omega
is
1/2
-Hölder, then

\vartheta
becomes linear). Deduce that

(B.2)

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\left[\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\left|{\mathfrak{u}}^{\varepsilon}_{t}(\mu,x)-{\mathfrak{u}}_{t}(\mu,x)\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\right]\,\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{D}}(\mu)\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\vartheta\left(\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}d_{\textrm{\rm TV}}(T_{\varepsilon}(\mu),\mu)\,\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{D}}(\mu)\right).

The remaining question is thus to estimate

\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}d_{\textrm{\rm TV}}(T_{\varepsilon}(\mu),\mu)\,\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{D}}(\mu)
. To do so, consider a Poisson–Dirichlet distributed sequence
(S_{i})_{i\geq 1}
, constructed on some probability space

(\Omega,{\mathcal{F}},{\mathbb{P}})
. Back to
(
B.1
), the right-hand side therein can be rewritten (up to a factor 4) as

{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{i\geq 1}S_{i}{\mathbf{1}}_{\{S_{i}>\varepsilon\}}\right].

We use notion of biased-pick sampling, see for instance
[
34
, Subsection 2.4]
. We call

I
an
{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
-valued random variable such that
{\mathbb{P}}(\{I=i\}|(S_{j})_{j})=S_{i}
, for
all
i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
. The above expectation can be reformulated as

{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{i\geq 1}S_{i}{\mathbf{1}}_{\{S_{i}<\varepsilon\}}\right]={\mathbb{E}}\left[{\mathbf{1}}_{\{S_{I}<\varepsilon\}}\right].

By
[
34
, Theorem 2.7]
, we know that the law of
S_{I}
is exactly the law of
Y_{1}
in
Remark
2.4
.
Therefore,

{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{i\geq 1}S_{i}{\mathbf{1}}_{\{S_{i}<\varepsilon\}}\right]={\mathbb{E}}\left[{\mathbf{1}}_{\{S_{I}<\varepsilon\}}\right]={\mathbb{P}}(\{Y_{1}<\varepsilon\})=\varepsilon.

Back to
(
B.2
), we obtain

(B.3)

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\left[\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\left|{\mathfrak{u}}^{\varepsilon}_{t}(\mu,x)-{\mathfrak{u}}_{t}(\mu,x)\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\right]\,\mathrm{d}{\mathcal{D}}(\mu)\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\vartheta(4\varepsilon),

where
\vartheta
is extended to the interval
(2,\infty)

by letting
\omega(r)=\omega(2)
for
r>2
.
